### [Performance of intracortical microelectrode arrays in people with implanted brain–computer interfaces over a 20-year period](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41591-026-04530-3)

- Source: `nature_medicine` · published `2026-11-26` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `78` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41591-026-04530-3`

The silicon microelectrode ‘Utah’ array has enabled people with paralysis to control computer cursors, operate prosthetic limbs and communicate using high-performance brain–computer interfaces. However, reports so far have typically been limited to one or two individuals, with no systematic assessment of array longevity, decoding accuracy and day-to-day stability. Here we present a comprehensive 20-year evaluation of neural data from the BrainGate clinical trials, spanning 2,289 recording sessions, 20 arrays and 14 participants. On average, arrays successfully recorded neural spiking waveforms on 35.6% of electrodes, with a modest 7 percentage point decline over the recording period (up to 7.3 years). Using a ‘decoding signal-to-noise ratio’ (dSNR) metric, we found that 11 of 14 arrays provided meaningful movement decoding throughout participation (median dSNR &gt;1), with three achieving &gt;70% accuracy compared with able-bodied mouse control. We also found that dSNR increased approximately logarithmically with the number of electrodes, providing a pathway for scaling performance. While longevity exceeded previous nonhuman primate studies, achieving peak performance consistently may require further improvements. Utah arrays recorded stable spiking activity for up to 7.3 years, with modest signal decline on average and sustained movement decoding in the majority of arrays analyzed, demonstrating the long-term feasibility of intracortical brain–computer interfaces.

### [Medical Imaging and Pediatric and Adolescent Brain Tumor Risk.](https://doi.org/10.1001/jama.2026.20441)

- Source: `jama_abstracts` · published `2026-11-19` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `77` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:43056070`

<h4>Importance</h4>Ionizing radiation from medical imaging may increase brain tumor risk.<h4>Objective</h4>To quantify brain tumor risk associated with radiation exposure from medical imaging in children and adolescents.<h4>Design, setting, and participants</h4>Retrospective cohort study of children born from 1996 through 2016 who were enrolled from birth in 6 US health care systems or Ontario, Canada, and were alive and cancer-free at age 2 years, with follow-up through December 31, 2017 (US sites), and December 31, 2024 (Ontario).<h4>Exposures</h4>Cumulative radiation dose to the brain from medical imaging, lagged by 2 years.<h4>Main outcomes and measures</h4>Incident brain or other central nervous system tumors. Hazard ratios (HRs) were estimated using Cox proportional hazards models for categorical dose, and excess relative risks (ERRs) were estimated using linear dose-response models for continuous dose.<h4>Results</h4>Among 3 704 860 children (1 900 285 [51.3%] male) followed up for 45 671 641 person-years (mean follow-up, 12.3 years), 1536 brain tumors were diagnosed. Mean radiation dose from 1 head computed tomography (CT) examination was 66.2 mGy (SD, 22.2). Among children with cumulative dose of 1 mGy or greater, mean cumulative dose was 73 mGy (SD, 95) overall and 100 mGy (SD, 105) among those diagnosed with brain tumors. Compared with no exposure, brain tumor risk increased with cumulative dose (>0 to <1 mGy: HR, 1.12 [95% CI, 1.01-1.26]; 1 to <5 mGy: HR, 1.71 [95% CI, 0.88-2.96]; 5 to <50 mGy: HR, 1.86 [95% CI, 1.16-2.81]; 50 to <75 mGy: HR, 2.30 [95% CI, 1.64-3.12]; 75 to <100 mGy: HR, 2.02 [95% CI, 1.13-3.30]; 100 to <150 mGy: HR, 2.81 [95% CI, 1.53-4.66]; ≥150 mGy: HR, 3.85 [95% CI, 2.43-5.75]). Cumulative dose was associated with brain tumor risk overall (ERR per 100 mGy, 1.27 [95% CI, 0.83-1.77]; P < .001), with stronger associations for aggressive histologies (ERR per 100 mGy, 1.51 [95% CI, 1.00-2.11]; P < .001). By age 21 years, exposure comparable to 1 head CT was associated with 7.9 excess brain tumors per 10 000 children, or approximately 1 excess tumor per 1266 head CT examinations. The estimated population-attributable fraction was 10.1% (95% CI, 5.8%-14.2%).<h4>Conclusions and relevance</h4>Medical imaging in children and adolescents was associated with a dose-related increase in brain tumor risk. These findings support careful clinical justification and dose minimization when pediatric imaging using ionizing radiation is indicated.

### [Reproductive health in women in Britain from 2010 to 2024: findings from repeated, cross-sectional National Surveys of Sexual Attitudes and Lifestyles (Natsal).](https://doi.org/10.1016/s0140-6736(26)01551-5)

- Source: `lancet_abstracts` · published `2026-11-19` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `77` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:43057276`

<h4>Background</h4>Major policy and funding shifts threaten access to essential reproductive services and reproductive autonomy in Britain (England, Scotland, and Wales) amid growing attention towards hormone hesitancy and declining birth rates. We report on prevalence, changes, and sociodemographic differences in reproductive health indicators among women of reproductive age in Britain from 2010 to 2024.<h4>Methods</h4>Britain's National Surveys of Sexual Attitudes and Lifestyles (Natsal) are cross-sectional, nationally representative surveys (Natsal-3 Sept 6, 2010, to Aug 31, 2012; Natsal-4 Sept 20, 2022, to April 5, 2024). Using 3198 Natsal-4 probability sample participants (assigned female at birth, aged 16-54 years, and reported vaginal sex ever), we estimated weighted prevalences of key health indicators of reproductive autonomy: reporting no effective contraception use, hormonal contraception use, abortion, unplanned or ambivalent pregnancy, infertility, help-seeking for infertility, and fertility intentions, and compared these with Natsal-3. Age-adjusted regression assessed changes between Natsal-3 and Natsal-4 by sociodemographic subgroups. Using Natsal-4 combined probability and non-probability samples (N=7053), we examined associations with age, education, area-level deprivation, ethnicity, and country of birth.<h4>Findings</h4>In Natsal-4, 18·5% (95% CI 16·6-20·7) of women at risk of unintended pregnancy reported not using any effective contraception, and 41·9% (33·8-50·6) of past-year pregnancies were scored as unplanned or ambivalent. The prevalence of ever having had an abortion increased from Natsal-3 to Natsal-4 (from 16·0% to 20·7%, age-adjusted odds ratio [OR] 1·35, 95% CI 1·18-1·55), whereas overall prevalences of other indicators were stable. Subgroup analyses showed declining overall hormonal contraception use in those younger than 35 years (eg, from 64·6% to 50·3% for those aged 16-24 years, OR 0·55, 95% CI 0·39-0·79). The proportion of those aged 16-44 years reporting definitely not wanting children more than doubled from Natsal-3 to Natsal-4 (from 7·7% to 16·6%; adjusted OR 1·66, 95% CI 1·28-2·16), driven by women younger than 35 years. In Natsal-4, women with below degree-level education, living in more deprived areas, or of Black, African, Caribbean, or Black British ethnicity were more likely to report no effective contraception use and ever having had an abortion. Women living outside the most affluent areas or reporting below degree-level education were less likely to seek help for infertility.<h4>Interpretation</h4>The increase in women not intending to have children has major demographic implications and, alongside the persistent unmet need for effective contraception, underscores the importance of high-quality, easily accessible contraceptive services. The paucity of improvement in women's reproductive health in Britain and its entrenched inequalities demand person-centred, culturally competent policies and integrated services that uphold reproductive autonomy.<h4>Funding</h4>The Wellcome Trust, the Economic and Social Research Council, and the National Institute for Health and Care Research.

### [Prenatal exposure to drug treatment for attention deficit/hyperactivity disorder and risk of neurodevelopmental disorders: Scandinavian multinational cohort study.](https://doi.org/10.1136/bmj-2026-101236)

- Source: `bmj_abstracts` · published `2026-11-25` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `76` · status: `scored`
- Event: `updated` · identity: `pmid:43085815`

<h4>Objective</h4>To assess whether prenatal exposure to drug treatment for attention deficit/hyperactivity disorder (ADHD) increases the risk of neurodevelopmental disorders in children.<h4>Design</h4>Population based cohort study.<h4>Setting</h4>Nationwide register data on births, dispensed drugs, and specialist healthcare diagnoses from Denmark, Norway, and Sweden.<h4>Participants</h4>3 643 376 children born in 2000-21 followed up for a median 9 years after birth in Sweden and 10 years in Norway and Denmark.<h4>Interventions</h4>Prenatal exposure, defined as ≥1 filled prescription for ADHD drug treatment anytime during pregnancy or in the second/third trimester (≥98 days after the last menstrual period). Analyses were also done for monotherapy with methylphenidate, amphetamines, and atomoxetine. Unexposed pregnancies had no filled prescriptions for ADHD drugs during pregnancy.<h4>Main outcome measures</h4>Neurodevelopmental disorders (any, subgroups, and individual disorders), identified on the basis of inpatient and outpatient diagnoses in specialist healthcare. Propensity score overlap weighted Cox proportional hazards regression was used to estimate adjusted (weighted) hazard ratios and 95% confidence intervals. Data from Denmark were analysed separately, and results were pooled using random effects meta-analysis.<h4>Results</h4>7595 (0.2%) children were prenatally exposed to any ADHD drug (5230 methylphenidate, 1510 amphetamines, 553 atomoxetine only). About one third (n=2389) were exposed in the second/third trimester. After control for confounders, neither any nor second/third trimester exposure to ADHD drugs overall or by drug type was associated with increased risk of any neurodevelopmental disorder (weighted hazard ratio 1.05, 95% confidence interval 0.98 to 1.12), behavioural and emotional disorders (1.04, 0.97 to 1.12), or disorders of psychological and intellectual development (1.06, 0.96 to 1.18). Some elevated hazard ratios for infrequent outcomes were observed but not consistently identified across analyses.<h4>Conclusions</h4>This multinational cohort study found no increased risk of childhood neurodevelopmental disorders associated with prenatal exposure to ADHD drugs. These findings strengthen existing evidence and can assist pregnant people and clinicians in weighing the benefits and risks of such treatment during pregnancy.

### [Medicare Shared Savings Program Accountable Care Organizations Performance Year 2025 Financial and Quality Results](https://www.cms.gov/newsroom/fact-sheets/medicare-shared-savings-program-accountable-care-organizations-performance-year-2025-financial)

- Source: `cms_fact_sheets` · published `2026-11-17` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `75` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.cms.gov/newsroom/fact-sheets/medicare-shared-savings-program-accountable-care-organizations-performance-year-2025-financial`

Medicare Shared Savings Program Accountable Care Organizations Performance Year 2025 Financial and Quality Results Medicare Shared Savings Program (Shared Savings Program) Accountable Care Organizations (ACOs) are critical in helping Make America Healthy Again — by supporting high-quality, coordinated care that addresses prevention, chronic illness and the root causes of disease. The Shared Savings Program’s unique design means ACOs are held accountable for saving money and improving healthcare quality, delivering a win for both patients and the Medicare Trust Funds. The Shared Savings Program is one of the largest programs in the country that aims to reduce overall costs of care while ensuring Medicare beneficiaries receive high-quality care. For Performance Year (PY) 2025, ACOs in the Shared Savings Program achieved the highest rates of shared savings since the inception of the program. Out of 472 ACOs, 80% of ACOs, representing 84% of the 10.9 million assigned beneficiaries, are earning performance payments totaling $4.9 billion, and Medicare saved $2.5 billion relative to benchmarks. PY 2025 had the highest share of ACOs receiving performance payments and the highest amount of savings for ACOs and Medicare since the inception of the Shared Savings Program. Financial Results ACOs had higher gross per capita savings in PY 2025 compared to PY 2024, $690 vs $643, respectively. However, ACOs had lower net per capita savings in PY 2025 compared to PY 2024, $234 vs $241. The decrease in net per capita savings represents more ACOs moving into two-sided risk tracks (a 4 percentage point increase between PY 2024 and PY 2025), and ACOs participating in two-sided tracks are eligible for higher shared savings rates and, therefore, can retain more of the savings they earned. Gross per capita savings are the savings shared by ACOs and Medicare, whereas net per capita savings represent the savings for Medicare only. Low revenue ACOs continue to outperform high revenue ACOs, generating $300 vs $169 net per capita savings. Typically, low revenue ACOs are physician-led ACOs or are comprised of federally qualified health centers and/or rural health centers, while high revenue ACOs are hospital-led. Additionally, ACOs composed predominantly of primary care clinicians had higher performance compared to ACOs with fewer primary care clinicians, with $417 vs $208 in net per capita savings. In its first PY (PY 2025), the 24 ACOs participating in the CMS Innovation Center’s ACO Primary Care Flex (ACO PC Flex) model generated $652 in gross per capita savings and $186 in net per capita savings. Notably, 92% of PC Flex ACOs (22 of 24) earned shared savings, compared with ~80% of all Shared Savings Program ACOs. The difference between gross and net per capita savings reflects, in part, that 88% of PC Flex ACOs participate under the ENHANCED track, which offers the highest shared savings rate, and that 33% of PC Flex ACOs are new ACOs. The ACO PC Flex model has a Prospective Primary Care (PPC) Payment option to shift payment for primary care away from fee-for-service, visit-based payment to a flexible, predictable revenue stream. PC Flex ACOs also performed well on quality measures, comparable to similar Shared Savings Program ACOs. Twenty-nine eligible ACOs received advance investment payments (AIPs), intended to provide advanced shared savings for upfront investments to build the infrastructure needed to succeed in the Shared Savings Program and address beneficiary needs. Twenty-five AIP ACOs shared in savings totaling $46 million, and as part of this financial reconciliation cycle, we will recoup $22 million of these advance payments from AIP ACOs that earned shared savings. ACOs that achieved savings had lower utilization compared to their benchmark across many categories of utilization including hospital discharges, Emergency Department (ED) Visits, number of Skilled Nursing Facility (SNF) stays, and SNF Utilization Days. Thirteen ACOs owed shared losses totaling $23 million. Quality Results All ACOs reported the new Alternative Payment Model (APM) Performance Pathway (APP) Plus quality measure set in PY 2025 and met quality reporting requirements under the Shared Savings Program. Adoption of all-payer digital quality measures (electronic clinical quality measures [eCQMs]) and Merit-based Incentive Payment System (MIPS) clinical quality measures (CQMs) also increased from PY 2024. CMS continues to encourage ACOs to move toward reporting digital quality measures using electronically stored and transmitted health information, leveraging interoperability, reducing burden, and improving efficiency. Medicare Clinical Quality Measures for ACOs participating in the Medicare Shared Savings Program (Medicare CQMs) became the most commonly reported collection type in PY 2025. Additionally, 264 ACOs reported at least one Medicare CQM in PY 2025, compared to 64 ACOs in PY 2024. ACOs that reported Medicare CQMs in both PYs 2024 and 2025 showed significant improvement on measures of controlling blood pressure, glycemic status assessments, and rates of depression screening with a follow-up plan. Shared Savings Program ACOs helped more patients improve markers of good health, such as controlled blood pressure and diabetes management, compared to 2024. Among ACOs that reported quality measures as eCQMs, the mean percentage of beneficiaries with adequately controlled high blood pressure increased significantly from PY 2024 to PY 2025 (from 71.21% to 73.86%). Among ACOs that reported eCQMs, the mean percentage of beneficiaries with poorly controlled diabetes (glycemic status assessment greater than 9%) declined significantly from PY 2024 to PY 2025 (from 28.36% to 24.63%). Nearly all ACOs outperformed similar types of physician groups on quality measures: ACOs performed significantly better than comparable physician groups on eCQMs for Screening for Depression and Follow-up Plan (55.03% among ACOs versus 50.64% among comparable MIPS groups) and Controlling High Blood Pressure (73.86% among ACOs versus 70.38% among comparable MIPS groups). ACOs have performed consistently better than comparable physician groups on the patient experience survey measure, Getting Timely Care, Appointments, and Information for every year that the survey has been fielded since 2019. Calendar Year 2027 Physician Fee Schedule Final Rule Updates The Trump Administration is committed to ensuring that ACOs’ participation in the Shared Savings Program promotes better chronic disease management and prevention, more efficient use of resources and innovation, and drives increased savings for the Medicare Trust Funds. To advance these goals, in the Calendar Year (CY) 2027 Medicare Physician Fee Schedule (PFS) Final Rule released on Nov. 4, 2026, and effective Jan. 1, 2027, CMS finalized policies that will strengthen incentives for ACOs to join and remain in the program, expand accountable care relationships for beneficiaries, improve the accuracy of financial benchmarks and beneficiary assignment, reduce administrative burden, and advance digital quality measurement and interoperability. Among these policies, the creation of a new growth adjustment to the historical benchmark will create a financial incentive for ACOs to recruit providers who are new to Medicare shared savings initiatives and serve beneficiaries who have not previously participated in accountable care relationships. These policies also provide opportunities to increase shared savings payments; ACOs in level E of the BASIC track will see their shared savings rate increase from 50% to 60%, and ACOs that already generate strong savings through the prior savings adjustment will have opportunities to increase shared savings payments with the prior savings adjustment’s scaling factor increasing from 50% to 75%. Additionally, starting Jan. 1, 2027, ACO-participating clinicians will have the option to receive higher payment for office visits with additional complexity, recognizing the added investments ACOs make in care coordination and giving clinicians a direct, meaningful financial incentive to join or remain in an ACO. CMS is also updating our beneficiary assignment policies to expand the number of beneficiaries eligible for the Shared Savings Program, while reducing gaming opportunities. Additionally, CMS is finalizing changes to the quality reporting requirements to: extend availability of a MIPS CQM collection type and extend the associated MIPS CQM reporting incentive, extend the use of flat benchmarks to score Medicare CQMs, revise the Shared Savings Program quality reporting requirement to allow ACOs to exclude ACO Participant TIN(s) from the ACO’s quality data submission under certain circumstances, revise the definition of a beneficiary eligible for Medicare CQMs to align with an ACO’s assigned beneficiary population, establish the Medicare eCQMs collection type to further support ACOs in the transition to dQM reporting, revise the Shared Savings Program scoring policy for excluded measures or measures that lack a benchmark, halt plans for ACOs to report more quality measures, and simplify Shared Savings Program certified electronic health record technology (CEHRT) use requirements. Lastly, Shared Savings Program ACOs, for the first time, will have the option to reduce or eliminate beneficiary cost sharing for certain Part B services, giving ACOs a direct, tangible benefit to offer the patients they serve and strengthening the beneficiary engagement case for healthcare providers joining or starting an ACO. For more information on the Medicare Shared Savings Program, visit: https://www.cms.gov/Medicare/Medicare-Fee-for-Service-Payment/sharedsavingsprogram . To view the CY 2027 PFS final rule, visit: https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2026/11/09/2026-22743/medicare-and-medicaid-programs-cy-2027-payment-policies-under-the-physician-fee-schedule-and-other . To view the CY 2027 PFS final rule Medicare Shared Savings Program Fact Sheet, visit: https://www.cms.gov/newsroom/fact-sheets/calendar-year-cy-2027-medicare-physician-fee-schedule-final-rule-cms-1848-f-medicare-shared-savings . ### CMS News and Media Group Catherine Howden, Director Media Inquiries Form 202-690-6145

### [Integrated wastewater and clinical genomic surveillance for high-resolution tracking of measles virus evolution and transmission](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41591-026-04754-3)

- Source: `nature_medicine` · published `2026-11-26` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `75` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41591-026-04754-3`

Measles outbreaks have surged globally, challenging surveillance and control efforts. In South Africa, the 2024−2025 measles outbreak led to nearly 4,000 reported cases, triggering a nationwide vaccination campaign. However, tracking measles virus (MeV; species Morbillivirus hominis) transmission and evolution remains difficult at scale, particularly in settings with limited access to care, diagnostic testing and sequencing capacity. Wastewater can enable population-scale virus surveillance, but low MeV concentrations limit detection sensitivity and sequencing quality. Here we show that overcoming these technical challenges enables integrated wastewater and clinical genomic surveillance that expands the coverage and resolution of MeV monitoring. We developed a virus concentration and whole-genome sequencing (WGS) workflow and applied it to 4,502 wastewater samples collected across all nine provinces of South Africa during the 2024−2025 outbreak. Wastewater MeV genotype prevalence tracked clinical estimates, and phylogenetic analyses combining wastewater and clinical sequences revealed geographically structured transmission and previously undetected inter-provincial spread. WGS recovered transmission links not captured by the field standard approach, which targets a 450-nucleotide region of the nucleoprotein gene (N450), highlighting the importance of WGS to accurately characterize transmission dynamics. Our study provides a cost-effective and scalable genomic surveillance approach to support measles control and elimination. Whole-genome sequencing and phylogenetic analyses of wastewater and surveillance samples during the 2024−2025 measles outbreak in South Africa reveal previously undetected inter-provincial spread and transmission links, suggesting that wastewater-integrated whole-genome surveillance can improve measles monitoring and control efforts.

### [Sexually transmitted infections, behavioural risks, and uptake of sexual health services in Britain from 2010 to 2024: findings from the repeated cross-sectional National Surveys of Sexual Attitudes and Lifestyles (Natsal).](https://doi.org/10.1016/s0140-6736(26)01605-3)

- Source: `lancet_abstracts` · published `2026-11-24` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `74` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:43078111`

<h4>Background</h4>Sexually transmitted infection (STI) diagnoses have increased in Britain since 2010, alongside major changes to sexual health policy, services, and disruptions owing to the COVID-19 pandemic. Meanwhile, sexual activity has decreased at population level, and more people report same-sex behaviours. To understand implications for HIV and STI control, we examined the general population prevalence of three STIs, sexual behaviours associated with STIs, and uptake of sexual health services, including HIV and chlamydia testing.<h4>Methods</h4>We conducted the fourth National Surveys of Sexual Attitudes and Lifestyles (Natsal-4) between Sept 20, 2022, and April 5, 2024. In this analysis, we used cross-sectional data from 5949 sexually experienced (who reported ever having sex) participants aged 16-59 years in Britain from the probability sample surveys. We estimated weighted prevalences for STIs using biosamples (vaginal swabs or urine samples; n=2721), self-reported STI-associated behaviours (including a composite of ≥4 partners, condomless sex with ≥2 partners in the past year, or overlapping partners in the past 5 years), and sexual health service uptake. Comparisons were made between groups defined by partners' sex and over time by comparing with Natsal-3 (2010-12).<h4>Findings</h4>In Natsal-4, overall prevalence of Chlamydia trachomatis, Neisseria gonorrhoeae, and Trichomonas vaginalis was less than 1·0%. C trachomatis prevalence was highest in women aged 16-24 years (4·0%, 95% CI 1·3-11·7). STI-associated behaviours (composite) were most prevalent in young people (aged 16-24 years) with only opposite-sex partners (women 26·5%, 95% CI 20·2-33·9; men 29·4%, 22·5-37·6), women reporting sex with women and men (WSWM; 60·0%, 49·0-70·1), and men reporting sex with men (MSM; 57·1%, 48·6-65·3). Among those with only opposite-sex partners, past-year uptake was higher in women than men for sexual health service use (women 8·3%, 7·2-9·6; men 3·9%, 3·0-5·1), HIV testing (women 5·8%, 4·9-7·0; men 2·8%, 2·1-3·7), and chlamydia testing (women 8·4%, 7·1-10·1; men 4·7%, 3·4-6·4). Among participants reporting STI-associated behaviours, apart from MSM (47·3%, 36·1-58·8), the majority of people who reported STI-associated behaviours had not taken up services (eg, for sexual health services in participants with opposite-sex partners: women 73·8%, 67·8-79·1; men 84·9%, 79·4-89·4; WSWM 68·6%, 54.1-80.2). Between Natsal-3 and Natsal-4, HIV testing increased in MSM, sexual health service use decreased in young men with only opposite-sex partners, and chlamydia testing markedly declined (eg, in all women aged 16-24 years: Natsal-3 54·2%, 51·4-57·0; Natsal-4 17·8%, 12·7-24·5).<h4>Interpretation</h4>Many people remain at behavioural risk for STIs, particularly young people, WSWM, and MSM. Most people with STI-associated behaviours had not used sexual health services in the past year. Declining chlamydia testing and high prevalence in young women might cause reproductive harm. Universal sexual health services remain essential.<h4>Funding</h4>Wellcome Trust, Economic and Social Research Council, and the National Institute for Health and Care Research.

### [Challenges in AI based tumor board case summarization and recommendations](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41746-026-03442-3)

- Source: `npj_digital_medicine` · published `2026-11-24` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `73` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41746-026-03442-3`

Tumor boards, recurring meetings at hospital institutions, assemble cancer specialists (e.g., medical oncology and pathology) to discuss patient care. In this work, we formally define tumor board case summarization, options generation, and meeting outcome prediction as AI tasks. We study datasets from four medical institutions and evaluate state-of-the-art large language models (LLMs) from the DeepSeek, GPT, Gemini, and Qwen families using expert human assessment. Medical oncologists created ~9.6k reference texts and 16k human judgments across tasks. Clinicians rated LLM-generated case summaries 3.57–4.59 out of 5.0 across institutions, but recommendation generation remained more challenging, with average scores of 2.0–3.6. Clinical alignment analyses showed modest correlations of ~0.2 for case summarization subtasks and higher correlations of ~0.6 for recommendation generation using LLM-as-Judge metrics. Our proposed TBFact metrics were competitive with LLM-as-Judge approaches in longer prose free-text settings, suggesting a promising direction toward explainable evaluation. This study, to our knowledge the largest expert-grading study of tumor board tasks with both granular and direct assessments, found that criterion-level scores and overall judgments are related nonlinearly. Experts also frequently assigned high ratings to responses that differed substantially from their reference texts, challenging assumptions underlying reference-based automatic evaluation metrics.

### [Intramuscular Olanzapine or Droperidol for Acute Behavioral Disturbance in Children and Adolescents: The PEAChY-M Randomized Clinical Trial.](https://doi.org/10.1001/jama.2026.25561)

- Source: `jama_abstracts` · published `2026-11-23` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `72` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:43072799`

<h4>Importance</h4>Acute behavioral disturbance is a high-risk emergency department presentation. There is limited evidence for the comparative effectiveness of intramuscular sedative medications in children and adolescents, which may be required when there is imminent risk of serious harm due to agitation.<h4>Objective</h4>To determine whether olanzapine is more effective than droperidol intramuscularly in achieving behavioral containment.<h4>Design, setting, and participants</h4>This investigator-initiated, open-label, multicenter, superiority, randomized clinical trial recruited children and adolescents aged 9 to 17 years presenting to 10 Australian emergency departments with acute behavioral disturbance clinically deemed to require intramuscular medication from November 8, 2021, to February 16, 2026. The last participant follow-up occurred on March 17, 2026. Data were analyzed from April 15 to 27, 2026.<h4>Interventions</h4>Participants were randomly assigned to a single weight-based dose of olanzapine or droperidol (<40 kg, 5 mg; ≥40 kg, 10 mg).<h4>Main outcomes and measures</h4>The primary outcome was successful sedation (Sedation Assessment Tool score ≤0) without the need for additional sedatives 1 hour after randomization. A range of secondary outcomes, including adverse events, were assessed.<h4>Results</h4>Of 348 recruited individuals (mean [IQR] age, 14.9 [13.6-16.1] years; 215 [62%] female; 75 Aboriginal or Torres Strait Islander individuals), 176 were assigned to olanzapine and 172 to droperidol. Of those randomized, 117 individuals (66%) in the olanzapine group and 121 individuals (70%) in the droperidol group were administered their study medication within 30 minutes of randomization. Successful sedation without the requirement for additional sedatives occurred in 89 individuals (53%) in the olanzapine group and 104 individuals (62%) in the droperidol group. No evidence of superiority was observed (adjusted risk difference, -8.3% [95% CI, -18.6% to 2.1%]; P = .12). One serious adverse event (apnea) occurred in the olanzapine group.<h4>Conclusions and relevance</h4>In this randomized clinical trial in children and adolescents presenting to the emergency department with behavioral disturbance, intramuscular olanzapine did not result in a clinically meaningfully greater proportion achieving successful sedation compared with droperidol. The limited effectiveness observed with both medications highlights the need for more effective pharmacological and nonpharmacological management strategies.<h4>Trial registration</h4>anzctr.org.au Identifier: ACTRN12621001238864.

### [Prehospital Antibiotics for Patients With Suspected Sepsis: The PITSTOP-Antibiotics Factorial Randomized Clinical Trial.](https://doi.org/10.1001/jama.2026.25627)

- Source: `jama_abstracts` · published `2026-11-24` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `72` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:43076168`

<h4>Importance</h4>Early treatment with antibiotics is considered an important intervention to reduce mortality for patients with sepsis.<h4>Objective</h4>To determine the effect of earlier treatment with antibiotics in the ambulance on 90-day mortality compared with usual treatment in the emergency department.<h4>Design, setting, and participants</h4>This multicenter, 2 × 2 factorial randomized, placebo-controlled trial was conducted among 4 paramedic services and their destination hospitals in Ontario, Canada. Participants were adults with suspected sepsis, defined as suspected infection, fever (≥38.0 °C), and hypotension (systolic blood pressure <100 mm Hg). Patients were enrolled between March 23, 2020, and August 20, 2025, with final follow-up in December 2025.<h4>Interventions</h4>Patients were randomized to receive 1 g of intramuscular ceftriaxone or placebo (and to receive liberal fluids vs usual fluids; reported separately) during prehospital care in the ambulance. All patients could receive open-label antibiotics in the emergency department.<h4>Main outcomes and measures</h4>The primary outcome was hospital mortality, defined as death prior to hospital discharge to day 90. There were 17 secondary outcomes, including all-cause mortality at 90 days, hospitalization, intensive care unit admission, use of mechanical ventilation, use of dialysis, positive culture results, and antibiotics within 24 hours of hospital arrival.<h4>Results</h4>Among 2091 randomized patients, 2048 were eligible for the primary analysis, with 1026 randomized to receive ceftriaxone and 1022 randomized to receive placebo. The median (IQR) age of patients was 75 (62-85) years and 1162 (56.7%) were males. The mean (SD) time from paramedic arrival at patient to administration of study drug was 37 (36) minutes in the ceftriaxone group and 39 (55) minutes in the placebo group. Most patients (735 of 795 [92.5%] in the ceftriaxone group and 730 of 792 [92.2%] in placebo group) were treated with antibiotics in the hospital after a mean (SD) of 247.3 (207.7) minutes after paramedic arrival among patients in the ceftriaxone group and 252.8 (218.0) minutes among the placebo group. The rate of hospital mortality was 182 of 1009 (18.0%) in the ceftriaxone group and 186 of 1011 (18.4%) in the placebo group (risk ratio, 0.97 [95% CI, 0.81-1.17]; P = .77). Of 17 secondary outcomes, all were similar between the groups.<h4>Conclusions and relevance</h4>Among patients with suspected sepsis on evaluation by paramedics, earlier treatment with antibiotics in the ambulance did not reduce mortality compared with usual care provided in the emergency department.<h4>Trial registration</h4>ClinicalTrials.gov Identifier: NCT03068741.

### [Seasonal mass vaccination with R21/Matrix-M against malaria in The Gambia and Burkina Faso: an open-label, cluster-randomised, controlled trial.](https://doi.org/10.1016/s0140-6736(26)01562-x)

- Source: `lancet_abstracts` · published `2026-11-19` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `71` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:43057277`

<h4>Background</h4>The R21/Matrix-M (R21) malaria vaccine candidate, recommended by WHO for use in young children, could be extended to the whole population. Seasonal mass vaccination (SMV) before peak malaria transmission could be a promising tool to accelerate progress towards malaria elimination. We aimed to evaluate the safety and effectiveness of SMV with R21 in two malaria-endemic settings in west Africa.<h4>Methods</h4>This open-label, cluster-randomised, controlled trial was conducted in The Gambia, a setting with low-to-moderate seasonal transmission, and Burkina Faso, a setting with intense seasonal transmission. 54 medium-sized villages (30 in The Gambia and 24 in Burkina Faso) were randomly assigned (1:1) by use of computer-restricted randomisation to receive either SMV with R21 (intervention group) or no SMV (control group). All study villages received national standard malaria control interventions. In the intervention group, residents who were aged 5 months and older, willing to comply with trial procedures, and could provide written informed consent (from parents or guardians for children aged <18 years plus assent for children aged 12-17 years) were eligible for vaccination. The intervention was delivered as three monthly doses of 5 μg (participants aged <15 years) or 10 μg (participants aged ≥15 years) R21 plus 50 μg of Matrix-M. The primary outcome was malaria prevalence at peak transmission, defined as the proportion of participants with Plasmodium falciparum infection assessed by quantitative PCR. This trial is registered with ClinicalTrials.gov (NCT06578572) and the Pan-African Clinical Trials Registry (PACTR202404544467702), and is ongoing.<h4>Findings</h4>The intervention was delivered between May 30, and Aug 21, 2024. A cross-sectional survey to estimate the primary endpoint was conducted between Nov 18 and Dec 6, 2024. The population available for vaccination varied across the three rounds. In The Gambia, among 4311 total residents in intervention villages, 2565 (59·5%) received three vaccine doses; in Burkina Faso, among 6235 total residents in intervention villages, 4072 (65·3%) received three vaccine doses. SMV with R21 reduced the odds of malaria infection by 70% (adjusted odds ratio 0·30 [95% CI 0·20-0·44]; p<0·0001) in The Gambia and by 25% (0·75 [95% CI 0·58-0·98]; p=0·036) in Burkina Faso. In The Gambia, prevalence decreased across all age groups, whereas in Burkina Faso the largest effect was observed in children younger than 5 years. The vaccine was safe and well tolerated. 651 adverse events were reported in 581 (13·5%) vaccine recipients in The Gambia, and 795 adverse events were reported in 705 (11·3%) vaccine recipients in Burkina Faso. Among 69 serious adverse events reported overall, only one was considered related to vaccination (febrile convulsions in a female participant aged 8 years after the second dose in The Gambia).<h4>Interpretation</h4>SMV with R21 reduced malaria prevalence in both settings. It could become an additional strategy for malaria control in endemic areas of seasonal transmission. In low-to-moderate transmission settings, the vaccine could accelerate progress towards elimination; in high-transmission areas it could further reduce the malaria burden.<h4>Funding</h4>UK Medical Research Council and the UK Foreign, Commonwealth & Development Office.

### [Human papillomavirus (HPV) infection, vaccination, and cervical screening in Britain from 1999 to 2024: findings from the repeated cross-sectional National Surveys of Sexual Attitudes and Lifestyles (Natsal).](https://doi.org/10.1016/s0140-6736(26)01554-0)

- Source: `lancet_abstracts` · published `2026-11-24` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `70` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:43078110`

<h4>Background</h4>Human papillomavirus (HPV) vaccination and cervical screening have substantially reduced overall cervical cancer risk, but differences in uptake must be quantified to inform equitable access. This study aimed to provide population-level evidence of the impact of HPV vaccination on HPV types 16 and 18 (hereafter HPV-16/18) prevalence in England, Scotland, and Wales (hereafter Britain), and uptake of cervical cancer prevention interventions (ie, vaccination and screening), 15 years after the introduction of the vaccination programme in 2008.<h4>Methods</h4>We conducted the National Surveys of Sexual Attitudes and Lifestyles (Natsal), which are nationally representative, cross-sectional population surveys with individual-level data and HPV testing of biosamples. Data from Natsal-2 (1999-2001; before the vaccination programme was introduced, and included urine biosamples), Natsal-3 (2010-12; early in the vaccination programme, and included urine biosamples), and Natsal-4 (2022-24; after the vaccination programme was established, and included vaginal swab and urine biosamples) were analysed. Age-adjusted prevalence ratios (aPRs) for HPV-16/18 infection were estimated among women eligible for routine vaccination (born from Sept 1, 1995) compared with same-aged women before the vaccination programme was introduced. Combined Natsal-4 probability and non-probability data assessed factors associated with self-reported HPV vaccination and cervical screening.<h4>Findings</h4>Among 10 418 Natsal-4 women (aged 16-59 years), 1059 women in the probability sample group (aged 18-44 years) provided usable biosamples (Natsal-2 n=1849; Natsal-3 n=2407). HPV-16/18 prevalence was 86% lower (aPR 0·14, 95% CI 0·02-0·84) among those aged 18-29 years in Natsal-4 (1·6%, 95% CI 0·3-8·2) than in Natsal-2 (8·7%, 95% CI 6·7-11·3). All HPV-16/18 infections reported in Natsal-4 occurred in unvaccinated women. Of 733 routine vaccination-eligible women, 23·0% (95% CI 19·4-27·0) reported being unvaccinated. Among 3530 women (aged 25-59 years) eligible for cervical screening, 5·3% (95% CI 4·4-6·3) reported never attending screening. Non-uptake of vaccination and screening was higher among minority ethnic groups, religious groups, and those born outside the UK. Prevalence of high-risk, non-vaccine-preventable HPV (HPV 35, 39, 51, 56, and 59) infections was 4·9% (95% CI 3·7-6·4) in the total population, without any statistically significant differences between these subgroups. Women with higher-risk sexual behaviours were more likely to both be vaccinated and attend for screening.<h4>Interpretation</h4>Britain's HPV vaccination programme has driven a dramatic decline in HPV-16/18 prevalence and WHO cervical cancer elimination targets might be achievable overall if this is sustained. However, women from minority ethnic groups, religious groups, and those not born in the UK are less likely to be vaccinated and screened while remaining at risk. It is crucial to improve the reach of prevention interventions to more equitably reduce cervical cancer morbidity and mortality.<h4>Funding</h4>Wellcome Trust, Economic and Social Research Council, and the National Institute for Health Research.

### [Telehealth to Support Coordinated Spontaneous Awakening and Breathing Trials in Invasive Ventilation: The TEACH Cluster Randomized Clinical Trial.](https://doi.org/10.1001/jama.2026.25197)

- Source: `jama_abstracts` · published `2026-11-25` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `70` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:43082011`

<h4>Importance</h4>Coordinated spontaneous awakening trials and spontaneous breathing trials (C-SAT/SBT) improve outcomes for patients receiving invasive mechanical ventilation, but implementation remains inadequate.<h4>Objective</h4>To evaluate whether a telehealth intervention added to a standard audit and feedback implementation of C-SAT/SBT increases adherence and improves outcomes compared with standard audit and feedback implementation alone (control).<h4>Design, setting, and participants</h4>A cluster randomized type 2 hybrid effectiveness-implementation clinical trial was conducted in 12 Intermountain Health hospitals in Utah and Idaho. Patients aged 16 years or older who were intubated and mechanically ventilated in the intensive care unit were enrolled from December 1, 2022, until July 31, 2025, with last follow-up on January 23, 2026.<h4>Intervention</h4>Telehealth hospitals received real-time oversight from telecritical care nurses and respiratory therapists for adherence to C-SAT/SBT and a standard audit and feedback implementation. Control hospitals received only standard audit and feedback.<h4>Main outcomes and measures</h4>Co-primary outcome measures were adherence to C-SAT/SBT and ventilator-free days to day 28. C-SAT/SBT was defined as any day that the SAT and SBT were separately adherent, and the SAT followed the SBT within 2 hours or both occurred before noon, or the patient was extubated. Change in adherence from baseline was compared between groups using a mixed-effects logistic model with prespecified 1-sided significance testing. Change in ventilator-free days was compared using a mixed-effects proportional odds model.<h4>Results</h4>There were 4544 patients at telehealth hospitals, 6438 patients at control hospitals, and 1789 preimplementation common baseline patients (median [IQR] age, 62 [47-71] years; 4826 [37.8%] female). The baseline C-SAT/SBT adherence rate of 43.9% increased to 90.3% at telehealth intervention hospitals and to 77.6% at control hospitals (odds ratio for change in telehealth vs change in control, 2.87; 1-sided 95% lower confidence limit, 2.44; P < .001; absolute difference, 11.4%; 95% CI, 8.8%-14.3%). Median (IQR) ventilator-free days from a common baseline of 24 (0-26) increased to 25 (10-27) at telehealth intervention hospitals and 25 (1-27) at control hospitals (odds ratio for change in telehealth vs change in control, 1.21; 95% CI, 1.01-1.45, P = .04; absolute difference, 1.02 days; 95% CI, 0.05-1.96 days).<h4>Conclusions and relevance</h4>Among adults intubated and mechanically ventilated in the intensive care unit, adding real-time telehealth support for C-SAT/SBT improved adherence and increased ventilator-free days more than a standard audit and feedback implementation alone.<h4>Trial registration</h4>ClinicalTrials.gov Identifier: NCT05141396.

### [Generative AI Chatbot Responses to Suicide and Self-Harm: Systematic Review](https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e103517)

- Source: `jmir` · published `2026-11-26` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `70` · status: `scored`
- Event: `new` · identity: `url:https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e103517`

&lt;strong&gt;Background:&lt;/strong&gt; A growing number of US adults and youth confide in generative AI chatbots for mental health support, including disclosure of suicide and self-harm risk. While chatbot response quality, safety, and effectiveness could impact population-level suicide and self-harm rates, there are no systematic reviews of this burgeoning literature. &lt;strong&gt;Objective:&lt;/strong&gt; We conducted a systematic review of studies evaluating generative AI chatbot responses to disclosure of suicide and self-harm risk. &lt;strong&gt;Methods:&lt;/strong&gt; We followed PRISMA (Preferred Reporting Items for Systematic Reviews and Meta-Analyses) 2020 and PRISMA-S (PRISMA literature search extension) guidelines and preregistered the protocol on the Open Science Framework. We included English-language, peer-reviewed publications and preprints from 2020 to 2025 that empirically evaluated generative AI conversational responses to suicidal ideation, suicidal or nonsuicidal self-harm, or suicide-planning content. Eligible designs included audits, chatbot development, and adversarial “jailbreaking” studies. We excluded nongenerative systems, clinician-training tools, and prediction-only models. The final database searches were run from December 18 to 23, 2025 (PubMed, Embase, APA PsycINFO, IEEE Xplore, arXiv, and OSF Preprints). On July 26, 2026, publication status was rechecked to update preprint vs published classification. After deduplication, titles/abstracts and full texts were independently screened by 2 reviewers, with discrepancies resolved by consensus. Methodological quality was appraised with the Mixed Methods Appraisal Tool 2018. We conducted a narrative synthesis across 7 safety domains and 5 AI classifications (proprietary, open-weight, companions, clinical, and study-specific). &lt;strong&gt;Results:&lt;/strong&gt; Of 1042 deduplicated records, 126 full texts were reviewed, and 29 were included (16 published; 13 preprints). Most studies evaluated suicidal content only (21/29). Twenty were solely audit studies, 2 chatbot development, and 1 jailbreaking. The rest combined approaches. Most used synthetic, single-turn prompts; only 6 used multiturn conversations. Most studies evaluated ≥2 of our 5 AI classifications (17/29). Proprietary models were included most frequently (23/29). When compared, proprietary models (eg, ChatGPT and Claude) tended to provide higher-quality responses than open-weight models (eg, Llama and DeepSeek) and many companions (eg, Replika and Character.AI); however, this trend weakened with sensitivity analysis. Chatbots generally performed well on emotional support, but responses were often generic and lacked context. Chatbots rarely assessed suicide or self-harm risk and performed most poorly when disclosure was ambiguous or moderate. Responses were inconsistent, often requiring multiple turns before referral to crisis resources or human support. Overtly harmful responses were rare under standard conditions but common under jailbreaking. &lt;strong&gt;Conclusions:&lt;/strong&gt; Evidence is limited by the proportion of preprints, variable quality, synthetic single-turn prompts, small samples, heterogeneous outcomes, and rapid model versioning. Nonetheless, current chatbots tend to provide validation but struggle to escalate care, assess risk, and resist jailbreaking. Research is needed on stakeholder perspectives and on the short- and long-term clinical impact of chatbot responses using follow-up assessments in real-world or clinical settings. &lt;strong&gt;Trial Registration:&lt;/strong&gt; OSF Registries osf.io/7fz95; https://osf.io/7fz95 <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/4a835b64db7055df1fa78234ad2693de" />

### [How patients explain and make sense of their decision for assisted suicide in Switzerland (the Exit Project): qualitative exploratory study.](https://doi.org/10.1136/bmj-2026-100610)

- Source: `bmj_abstracts` · published `2026-09-15` · freshness `stale`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `ineligible`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:42744336`

<h4>Objective</h4>To explore how people actively pursuing assisted suicide in Switzerland frame and report the motivations underpinning their decision.<h4>Design</h4>Qualitative exploratory study using semistructured interviews analysed through reflexive thematic analysis within a constructivist framework.<h4>Setting</h4>Participants were recruited across Switzerland between October 2023 and July 2025 through three right-to-die associations and contact with healthcare professionals, including palliative care clinicians and general practitioners. Interviews were conducted in French, German, or Italian and were set in participants' homes or in hospitals.<h4>Participants</h4>30 adults with decision making capacity who were able to communicate verbally and had contacted a right-to-die association to initiate an assisted suicide request were recruited to the study. Participants were interviewed while actively engaged in the assisted suicide request process. Fourteen participants subsequently died by assisted suicide within weeks of the interview.<h4>Results</h4>Participants described the decision to request assisted suicide as the outcome of several motivational processes interacting rather than being caused by a single precipitating factor. Analysis identified six interrelated themes: imagining and shaping one's own death, considering assisted suicide as a trusted commodity, seeking validation and common ground, reclaiming personal power over the medicalisation of dying, crossing the unbearable threshold, and seizing the narrowing window of opportunity. These themes functioned as dynamic, interacting motivations that consolidated, stabilised, and reinforced the choice to pursue assisted suicide over time.<h4>Conclusions</h4>In this study, the pursuit of assisted suicide emerged as prospective, meaning making processes embedded in personal values, relational considerations, and awareness of time, rather than solely as a reactive response to physical suffering. Recognising this complexity may support more ethical clinical dialogue and inform policy discussions in jurisdictions where assisted dying is permitted or under consideration.

### [Renal outcomes in people with type 2 diabetes with and without albuminuria treated with SGLT-2 inhibitors and GLP-1 receptor agonists: target trial emulation.](https://doi.org/10.1136/bmj-2026-100574)

- Source: `bmj_abstracts` · published `2026-09-16` · freshness `stale`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `ineligible`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:42778221`

<h4>Objective</h4>To compare the risk of renal deterioration under treatment with glucagon-like peptide-1 (GLP-1) receptor agonists or sodium-glucose contransporter-2 (SGLT-2) inhibitors versus other second line treatments in people with type 2 diabetes with and without albuminuria (albumin to creatinine ratio ≥30 mg/g).<h4>Design</h4>Target trial emulation.<h4>Settings</h4>Linkage of electronic health records and claims data from 10 health systems and two national health insurance plans-data from BESTMED (observational evaluation of second line therapy medications in diabetes) study.<h4>Participants</h4>People with type 2 diabetes and moderate cardiovascular risk and estimated glomerular filtration rate ≥45 mL/min/1.73 m<sup>2</sup> on metformin monotherapy who started treatment with a GLP-1 receptor agonist or SGLT-2 inhibitor, a sulfonylurea, or a dipeptidyl peptidase-4 (DPP-4) inhibitor after metformin between 2014 and 2022.<h4>Main outcome measure</h4>Deterioration of kidney function defined as doubling of serum creatinine or estimated glomerular filtration rate <15 mL/min/1.73 m<sup>2</sup>.<h4>Results</h4>Among 75 455 eligible people (61 583 (81.76%) without albuminuria), median age was 60 years (interquartile range 51-68), 36 231 (48%) were women, median haemoglobin A<sub>1c</sub> was 7.8% (interquartile range 7.3-8.5%), and median estimated glomerular filtration rate was 92 mL/min/1.73 m<sup>2</sup> (interquartile range 77-102). People were followed for a median of 32 months (interquartile range 18-57). The estimated five year risk of the renal outcome was 2.4% (95% confidence interval 2.1% to 2.7%) for people taking GLP-1 receptor agonists or SGLT-2 inhibitors versus 2.1% (1.8% to 2.6%) for people taking DPP-4 inhibitors among those without albuminuria; and 3.2% (2.3% to 4.1%) versus 5.4% (4.2% to 6.7%), respectively, among people with albuminuria. The estimated five year risk ratio comparing the GLP-1 receptor agonist or SGLT-2 inhibitor group with the DPP-4 inhibitor group was 1.10 (95% confidence interval 0.90 to 1.38) among people without albuminuria and 0.60 (0.42 to 0.82) among people with albuminuria.<h4>Conclusions</h4>Compared with DPP-4 inhibitors, GLP-1 receptor agonists and SGLT-2 inhibitors reduce the risk of deterioration of renal function in people with albuminuria. Little evidence of renal benefit among people without albuminuria was observed over the study follow-up period.

### [Survival benefit of deceased donor kidney transplantation versus continued dialysis: international target trial emulation.](https://doi.org/10.1136/bmj-2026-100624)

- Source: `bmj_abstracts` · published `2026-09-18` · freshness `stale`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `ineligible`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:42759976`

<h4>Objective</h4>To quantify the survival benefit of deceased donor kidney transplantation versus continued dialysis according to patients' characteristics (age, diabetes, cardiovascular disease), donor quality (standard criteria donor or expanded criteria donor) and donor retrieval type (donation after brain death or donation after circulatory death).<h4>Design</h4>Target trial emulation.<h4>Setting</h4>European Renal Association Registry data from five countries or regions: Catalonia (Spain), Denmark, France, Norway, and the UK.<h4>Participants</h4>Patients aged ≥20 years receiving dialysis and waitlisted for a first deceased donor kidney-only transplant between 2000 and 2019.<h4>Main outcome measures</h4>Five year patient survival following deceased donor kidney transplantation versus continued dialysis within monthly sequential trials. Flexible parametric spline models estimated adjusted five year patient survival across recipient age.<h4>Results</h4>Of 64 036 waitlisted patients receiving dialysis, 39 244 received a deceased donor kidney transplant: 16 897 (43.1%) from a standard criteria donor/donation after brain death donor, 4324 (11.0%) from a standard criteria donor/donation after circulatory death donor, 14 799 (37.7%) from an expanded criteria donor/donation after brain death donor, and 3224 (8.2%) from an expanded criteria donor/donation after circulatory death donor. Standard criteria donor/donation after brain death and standard criteria donor/donation after circulatory death transplantation showed higher estimated five year survival compared with continued dialysis regardless of recipient age. However, the survival benefit associated with expanded criteria donor/donation after brain death transplantation and, particularly, expanded criteria donor/donation after circulatory death transplantation decreased with increasing recipient age, albeit with greater statistical uncertainty among older patients. For example, among 75 year olds, estimated five year survival following expanded criteria donor/donation after circulatory death transplantation did not differ significantly from that with continued dialysis (59% (95% confidence interval 51% to 66%) versus 55% (54% to 55%); hazard ratio 0.97, 95% confidence interval 0.70 to 1.33), irrespective of diabetes or cardiovascular disease status. This group had a threefold higher early post-transplant mortality than those continuing dialysis.<h4>Conclusions</h4>Standard criteria donor kidney transplantation was consistently associated with a survival benefit compared with continued dialysis. By contrast, the survival benefit associated with expanded criteria donor transplantation declined with increasing recipient age, particularly for expanded criteria donor/donation after circulatory death transplantation, such that the oldest recipients had a smaller and more statistically uncertain estimated survival advantage over continued dialysis.

### [Preliminary Calendar Year (CY) 2027 Medicare Clinical Laboratory Fee Schedule Payment Rates](https://www.cms.gov/newsroom/fact-sheets/preliminary-calendar-year-cy-2027-medicare-clinical-laboratory-fee-schedule-payment-rates)

- Source: `cms_fact_sheets` · published `2026-09-21` · freshness `stale`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `ineligible`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.cms.gov/newsroom/fact-sheets/preliminary-calendar-year-cy-2027-medicare-clinical-laboratory-fee-schedule-payment-rates`

Preliminary Calendar Year (CY) 2027 Medicare Clinical Laboratory Fee Schedule Payment Rates The Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services (CMS) posted preliminary weighted median private payor rates for certain clinical diagnostic laboratory test codes, marking a key step toward setting Medicare's calendar year (CY) 2027 payment rates under the Clinical Laboratory Fee Schedule (CLFS). This preliminary data reflects the second full data collection and reporting cycle for most tests required under the Protecting Access to Medicare Act of 2014 (PAMA), and CMS is inviting public comment before finalizing CY 2027 rates. Protecting Access to Medicare Act of 2014 (PAMA) Section 216(a) of PAMA changed how Medicare sets payment rates for clinical diagnostic laboratory tests. Before PAMA, tests generally were paid the lesser of billed charges, the fee schedule established by the Medicare contractor or the national limitation amount, which is a percentage of the median of the local fee schedule amounts as described in section 1833(h) of the Social Security Act (the Act). PAMA moved CMS to a market-based payment system that ties Medicare payment rates to the rates private payors actually pay for the same tests. Under PAMA, as implemented in section 1834A of the Act and CLFS regulations, most laboratories that meet certain revenue thresholds — known as “applicable laboratories” — must collect and report certain private payor rate and volume data to CMS during defined data collection and data reporting periods. CMS uses this data to calculate weighted median private payor rates, which is generally the basis for Medicare's CLFS payment rates for most tests. Section 1834A of the Act includes different requirements for new clinical diagnostic laboratory tests and advanced diagnostic laboratory tests. The first round of PAMA-based data collection took place in 2016, which was reported in 2017 and which generally became the basis of Medicare’s CLFS payment rates in 2018. Congress enacted several laws delaying the next full reporting cycle. Most recently, the Consolidated Appropriations Act, 2026 revised the data reporting period, data collection period, and requirements for the phase-in of payment reductions , requiring the second round of data reporting to occur in 2026. CMS used data reported during the 2026 reporting period to establish the preliminary CY 2027 rates. Clinical Laboratory Fee Schedule (CLFS) The CLFS is the payment system Medicare uses to pay laboratories for clinical diagnostic laboratory tests, such as blood tests, urinalysis, and molecular and genomic tests. Payment rates are set annually and updated under the framework established in section 1834A of the Act, which generally requires that CLFS rates for most tests be based on the weighted median of private payor rates for each test, weighted by test volume. Additionally, there are procedures for determining the basis for, and amount of, payment for new tests as described in 18333(h)(8) of the Act. Data Reporting Requirements Under the CLFS, reporting entities generally submit “applicable information” collected during a data collection period on behalf of their component applicable laboratories. This information includes private payor rates, the volume of tests paid at each rate, and the specific Healthcare Common Procedure Code System (HCPCS) code for each test. The data collection and reporting timeline for this round (as established by the Consolidated Appropriations Act, 2026 and reflected in section 1834A of the Act): Data collection period: January 1, 2025 – June 30, 2025 Data reporting period: May 1, 2026 – July 31, 2026 Methodology The weighted median is calculated by arraying the distribution of all private payor rates, weighted by the volume for each payor and each laboratory. CMS took quality control measures to address potentially unreliable private payor data. The following records were removed from the calculation: Duplicate records: Reported records where the TIN, NPI, HCPCS code, payment rate, and volume (if greater than 1) exactly match another record. Outlier records: Records where the reported payment rate exceeds 100 times the CY 2026 CLFS payment rate for that test. For transparency, CMS is publishing the preliminary payment rates with and without these exclusions and is requesting public comment on whether this approach is appropriate for calculating the final weighted median private payor rates. Key Statistics From This Data Reporting Cycle Measure Result Laboratories (NPIs) that submitted data 6,411 Applicable laboratories used after exclusions 6,304 Total applicable HCPCS codes 1,947 Codes with a weighted median from private payor data 1,528 (78.5%) Average potential change to CY 2026 CLFS rates ~16% lower Of the approximately 1,500 codes with a weighted median from private payor data: 186 codes have a higher weighted median compared to the CY 2026 CLFS rate. 169 codes have an equal weighted median compared to the CY 2026 CLFS rate. 1,171 codes have a lower weighted median compared to the CY 2026 CLFS rate. Note: Two HCPCS codes that have a weighted median from private payor data do not have a comparable CY 2026 CLFS rate. Results by Test Category Category Number of Codes Potential Weighted Rate Change Chemistry 387 -16% Molecular Pathology 211 -22% Genomic Sequencing 42 -23% Microbiology 224 -19.3% Proprietary Laboratory Analysis 151 -2.4% Immunology 205 -19.3% Growth in Data Reporting Participation Since 2017 The mix of reporting applicable laboratory types has shifted substantially since the first PAMA data reporting cycle, with total reporting laboratories increasing 230 percent overall. Specifically, while hospital labs made up only 1.1% of reporting applicable labs in 2017, in the 2026 reporting period, hospital labs account for approximately 13.6% of reporting applicable labs. Laboratory Type First Reporting (2017) Current Reporting (2026) Change Hospital 21 875 +4,067% Physician Office Laboratory 1,106 3,262 +195% Independent 658 775 +18% Other/Unknown (incl. FQHC, Urgent Care) 157 1,499 +855% Total 1,942 6,411 +230% Phase-In of Payment Reductions CLFS payment rate reductions will not take effect all at once. The Consolidated Appropriations Act, 2026 amended section 1834A of the Act to provide for a phase-in from calendar years 2027 through 2029; payment for a test may not be reduced by more than 15% per year compared with the amount established for that test in the preceding year. Tests With No Applicable Information For existing clinical diagnostic laboratory tests for which CMS received no applicable information, CMS held a public meeting September 15–16, 2026, 1 to gather public input on the appropriate payment methodology—crosswalking or gapfilling—for these tests. As required under section 1834(f), an expert panel of laboratory professionals provided recommendations on how rates for these tests should be set. 2 Additional CLFS Materials Published Concurrently Alongside the preliminary CY 2027 weighted median private payor rates, CMS is also publishing: Preliminary crosswalk and gapfill determinations for new 2026 clinical diagnostic laboratory tests. Final Medicare Administrative Contractor (MAC) recommendations for gapfill rates for laboratory tests new in 2025. To view the preliminary CY 2027 Clinical Laboratory Fee Schedule rates and data, visit https://www.cms.gov/medicare/payment/fee-schedules/clinical-laboratory-fee-schedule-clfs . 2019 Regulatory Changes to “Applicable Laboratory” In the CY 2019 Physician Fee Schedule final rule (83 FR 59667-59681), CMS made three revisions effective January 1, 2019: Medicare Advantage plan revenues are excluded from total Medicare revenues in the denominator of the majority-of-Medicare-revenues threshold. Hospitals billing for nonpatient laboratory services use Medicare revenues from Form CMS-1450 14x Type of Bill to determine whether their hospital outreach laboratories meet the majority-of-Medicare-revenues and low-expenditure thresholds. CMS also allowed reporting entities to condense certain applicable information at the TIN level, rather than reporting separately for each applicable laboratory at the NPI level. We believe these regulatory revisions provided for more hospital reporting in 2026. How to Submit Comments In accordance with confidentiality requirements in section 1834A(a)(10) of the Social Security Act and 42 C.F.R. 414.504(f), CMS is releasing raw data for HCPCS codes reported by 10 or more reporting entities to support public review. The public has 30 days after publication to review and submit comments on the preliminary CY 2027 CLFS rates and supporting data files. Written comments should be submitted electronically to CMS’ CLFS dedicated email box at CLFS_Annual_Public_Meeting@cms.hhs.gov . CMS will publish final CY 2027 CLFS rates on the CMS website in November 2026. ### 91 FR 23418-23421 Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services, “CLFS Advisory Panel,” last modified September 16, 2026, https://www.cms.gov/medicare/payment/fee-schedules/clinical-laboratory-fee-schedule-clfs/clfs-advisory-panel CMS News and Media Group Catherine Howden, Director Media Inquiries Form 202-690-6145

### [Federal Marketplace (FFE and SBE-FP) Anti-Fraud Actions](https://www.cms.gov/newsroom/fact-sheets/federal-marketplace-ffe-sbe-fp-anti-fraud-actions)

- Source: `cms_fact_sheets` · published `2026-09-22` · freshness `stale`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `ineligible`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.cms.gov/newsroom/fact-sheets/federal-marketplace-ffe-sbe-fp-anti-fraud-actions`

Federal Marketplace (FFE and SBE-FP) Anti-Fraud Actions Introduction Fraud and improper enrollments in the Health Insurance Marketplace ® harm consumers, burden taxpayers, and undermine the integrity of the Federal Marketplace. CMS has identified a pattern of unauthorized enrollments and suspicious agent and broker activity in the Federal Marketplace. In response, the agency has moved aggressively to address these issues through a three-pronged strategy: preventing fraudulent and improper enrollments upfront; removing existing unauthorized enrollments; and enforcing CMS regulations governing agents and brokers. CMS has launched coordinated efforts with health insurance companies to identify enrollees associated with suspected unauthorized enrollments and cancel confirmed unauthorized enrollments. At the same time, the agency has focused on strengthening policies and enforcing existing regulations governing agents and brokers who assist consumers with enrollment. Cancellation of Unauthorized Enrollments On August 31, 2026, CMS canceled approximately 315,000 enrollments covering over 760,000 individuals after confirmation that these enrollments were unauthorized. This conclusion was the outcome of CMS and health insurance companies review and investigation in accordance with CMS’s existing process for unauthorized enrollments. CMS expects this will result in a return of approximately $2.2 billion in advance payments of the premium tax credit (APTC) for these canceled enrollments. CMS will continue working with health insurance companies to identify and investigate potentially unauthorized enrollments, cancel those confirmed as unauthorized to prevent improper subsidy payments in the future, and recoup the associated past APTC payments. Termination of Non-Compliant Agents and Brokers Since January 2026, CMS has sent termination notices to over 200 non-compliant agents and brokers. This summer, CMS issued 569 notices of intent to terminate Exchange Agreements to agents and brokers that submitted 2026 applications without identifying applicant information, such as a Social Security Number (SSN). The timeline for non-compliant agents and brokers to respond for the first 100 of the 569 notices of intent to terminate has concluded, and 66 have already received termination notices. CMS expects to send additional termination notices once the timeline for non-compliant agents and brokers to respond to the remaining 469 notices of intent to terminate concludes. CMS will continue to investigate and issue notices of intent to terminate Exchange Agreements to agents and brokers who we identify are noncompliant with Marketplace standards. CMS will also support state Departments of Insurance and health insurance companies in their own efforts to identify and take action on non-compliant agents and brokers. Moratorium on New Agent/Broker Registration CMS data show that agents and brokers who first registered for the 2026 plan year represent a small fraction of all agent/broker-assisted enrollments, yet they account for a disproportionate share of unauthorized enrollments and other high-risk activity in the Marketplace. Compared to agents and brokers who registered before 2026, this group of newly registered agents and brokers is responsible for agent/broker-assisted enrollments that are: 2.8 times more likely to have unresolved income verification issues; 2.7 times more likely to be missing Social Security Numbers; 2.6 times more likely to have unresolved citizenship or immigration status verification issues; 1.6 times more likely to use Special Enrollment Periods not subject to verification; 1.4 times more likely to include Medicaid denial attestations; and 1.4 times more likely to be found dually enrolled in Medicaid/CHIP and Marketplace coverage To respond to the heightened risk presented by newly registered agents and brokers, CMS is announcing a temporary moratorium on the registration of agents and brokers for 2027 who do not have an active Exchange Agreement for 2026. Additional Program Integrity Protections In addition to the above actions, CMS has implemented several new protections against agent and broker fraud. First, all existing agents and brokers are now required to re-identity proof through either Login.gov or ID.me. Second, all applications involving an agent or broker must include Social Security Numbers or immigration document numbers that CMS can verify for all non-newborn applicants. Third, CMS now prohibits agents and brokers from being added to applications that consumers should be completing on their own through HealthCare.gov. Fourth, in advance of Open Enrollment, CMS will implement a requirement for electronic consumer authorization before an agent or broker can take any action on an application or enrollment. Strengthening State and Industry Partnerships State departments of insurance play an important role in anti-fraud efforts, and CMS maintains a longstanding relationship with them both directly and through the National Association of Insurance Commissioners (NAIC). CMS is strategically working with NAIC and states on anti-fraud work, including increased data sharing, enforcement, and best practices for protecting consumers. Looking ahead, CMS will continue to ramp up anti-fraud efforts. In preparation for Open Enrollment, CMS will provide training and communications to agents and brokers concerning new requirements. Consumers will also receive communications for tips on preventing fraud and protecting themselves. ### CMS News and Media Group Catherine Howden, Director Media Inquiries Form 202-690-6145

### [Contemporary menopausal hormone therapy and thrombotic disease: nationwide nested case-control study.](https://doi.org/10.1136/bmj-2026-100688)

- Source: `bmj_abstracts` · published `2026-09-23` · freshness `stale`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `ineligible`
