### [Rural Health Transformation Program: Year 2 Awards](https://www.cms.gov/newsroom/fact-sheets/rural-health-transformation-program-year-2-awards)

- Source: `cms_fact_sheets` · published `2026-11-09` · freshness `stale`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `ineligible`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.cms.gov/newsroom/fact-sheets/rural-health-transformation-program-year-2-awards`

Rural Health Transformation Program: Year 2 Awards The Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services (CMS) announced $10 billion in this year’s awards to all 50 states through the Rural Health Transformation Program (RHTP), continuing this historic investment to strengthen rural healthcare across America. Established through the Working Families Tax Cuts Act, RHTP provides $50 billion over five years to help states improve healthcare access, quality, and outcomes in rural communities. Rural health challenges extend beyond geography and long travel distances. Many communities also face shortages of health professionals, high rates of chronic disease, limited specialty and behavioral health capacity, barriers to healthy living, aging infrastructure, workforce recruitment and retention challenges, and fragmented care delivery. RHTP gives states resources and flexibility to address these interconnected challenges while establishing clear expectations for implementation, accountability, and measurable progress. RHTP at a Glance $50 billion over five years for rural health transformation. Each year $10 billion is awarded from fiscal year (FY) 2026 through FY 2030. All funds must be expended by Sept. 30, 2032, or they will be returned to the U.S. Treasury. All 50 states participate in the program. At least $100 million per state, per year, is given through equal baseline funding. State-led transformation plans address the needs of each state’s rural communities. Annual reporting and funding determinations help CMS monitor implementation and progress throughout the five-year program. RHTP supports transformation across the rural healthcare delivery system. It is not limited to one type of provider or designed simply to provide short-term operating support to individual hospitals. States are pursuing strategies involving hospitals, clinics, emergency medical services, behavioral health providers, community organizations, workforce partners, technology, and other parts of the rural health ecosystem. What Does RHTP Support? RHTP is designed to address the interconnected challenges facing rural communities and strengthen healthcare delivery across five strategic goals: Make rural America healthy again: Support rural health innovations and new access points to promote preventive health and address root causes of diseases. Projects will use evidence-based, outcomes-driven interventions to improve disease prevention, chronic disease management, behavioral health, and prenatal care. Sustainable access: Help rural providers become long-term access points for care by improving efficiency and sustainability. With RHTP support, rural facilities work together — or with high-quality regional systems — to share or coordinate operations, technology, primary and specialty care, and emergency services. Workforce development: Attract and retain a highly skilled healthcare workforce by strengthening recruitment and retention of healthcare providers in rural communities. Help rural providers practice at the top of their license and develop a broader set of providers to serve a rural community’s needs, such as community health workers, pharmacists, and individuals trained to help patients navigate the healthcare system. Innovative care: Spark the growth of innovative care models to improve health outcomes, coordinate care, and promote flexible care arrangements. Develop and implement payment mechanisms incentivizing providers or Accountable Care Organizations (ACOs) to reduce healthcare costs, improve quality of care, and shift care to lower cost settings. Tech innovation: Foster use of innovative technologies that promote efficient care delivery, data security, and access to digital health tools by rural facilities, providers, and patients. Projects support access to remote care, improve data sharing, strengthen cybersecurity, and invest in emerging technologies. The five strategic goals set the overall direction for RHTP. States must use RHTP funds for at least three approved uses under the program. These include prevention and chronic disease management; rural workforce recruitment and retention; technology-enabled care and health IT; behavioral health and substance use disorder services; innovative care and payment models; and investments that help rural communities strengthen and right-size their healthcare delivery systems. How Does RHTP Funding Work? Congress established a two-part structure for distributing the $10 billion available each year: 50% — Baseline Funding Half of the annual funding is distributed equally among all approved states. With all 50 states participating in RHTP, each state receives $100 million in baseline funding each year. 50% — Workload Funding The other half is allocated using factors established in the RHTP Notice of Funding Opportunity (NOFO). These factors account for differences among states and include: Characteristics of a state’s rural population and healthcare facilities; Initiatives included in the state’s rural health transformation plan; Certain state policy actions; and Other technical, data-driven factors described in the NOFO. The baseline and workload portions together determine each state’s annual RHTP award. What Is Different About Year 2? The initial FY 2026 awards reflected the statutory funding structure and evaluation of states’ plans. Subsequent funding is also determined pursuant to statutory requirements and is informed by states’ progress implementing their approved plans. All 50 states submitted annual reports to CMS documenting their progress during the program’s first year. CMS uses annual reporting to monitor implementation and inform funding for subsequent budget periods. For initiative-based portions of the funding framework, CMS uses an established checkpoint model to assess implementation progress. Checkpoints measure concrete steps in implementation, such as establishing program governance, developing and receiving approval of project plans, launching initiatives, reaching milestones, establishing methods for measuring results, and planning for long-term sustainability. This approach recognizes that transformation occurs over time. Early implementation may focus on building the capacity needed to produce longer-term changes in healthcare access, delivery, and outcomes. Why Can State Awards Change From Year to Year? RHTP was designed so that a portion of the funding is competitively allocated and can change from year to year. The equal baseline portion remains the same for participating states. The workload portion can change as CMS applies the program’s annual funding methodology, including factors that reflect implementation progress and progress toward state policy actions. As a result, a state’s total award may increase, decrease, or remain similar from one year to the next. A decrease in one year does not determine a state’s funding trajectory for the remainder of the program; funding is dynamic as determined annually under the program’s established methodology. Annual funding determinations do not change the program’s underlying goal: supporting all participating states as they implement long-term strategies to transform rural healthcare. Year 1: Building the Foundation During Year 1, states worked to establish governance structures, build partnerships, hire staff, develop and approve project plans, establish contracts and subawards, purchase equipment and technology, launch initiatives, and begin implementing programs in rural communities. Because states entered RHTP with different rural populations, workforce capacity, disease burdens, health system designs, and implementation plans, progress does not look identical in every state. Some initiatives are already reaching patients and communities, while others have focused initially on building the infrastructure needed to deliver new or restored services and produce measurable results over the remaining years of the program. Learn more about how states are putting their rural health transformation plans into action through CMS’ state snapshots and additional state progress materials, available at https://www.medicaid.gov/resources-for-states/rural-health . Understanding RHTP RHTP addresses more than geographic barriers. Long travel distances can make accessing care difficult in rural communities, but rural health challenges also include health professional shortages, chronic disease burden, limited specialty and behavioral health services, aging infrastructure, workforce instability, and fragmented delivery systems. RHTP gives states tools to address these challenges within their states. RHTP is about more than hospitals. Rural hospitals are an important part of rural health systems, but RHTP supports broader transformation involving providers, workforce, technology, emergency services, prevention, behavioral health, community partnerships, and new care-delivery models. States are in the driver’s seat. Each state developed its own rural health transformation plan based on the needs of its rural communities. CMS establishes program requirements and provides oversight, while states determine how to implement their approved strategies within those requirements. RHTP is a transformation program, not a replacement for other federal healthcare funding. RHTP funds must support approved transformation activities and may not supplant existing government funding sources or programs. Awards come with ongoing accountability. RHTP is a cooperative agreement, meaning CMS remains substantially involved throughout implementation. States must report progress, comply with federal requirements, and use funding for approved purposes. CMS provides ongoing technical assistance and monitors implementation through dedicated Project Officers, reporting, financial oversight, and other program controls. Funding, obligations and accountability. States must obligate RHTP funds within the applicable budget period. Funds that are properly obligated within that period generally remain available for expenditure through the end of the following fiscal year, subject to program requirements. CMS has published state obligation data through July 31, 2026, as a point-in-time look at states’ use of Year 1 funding. These figures do not, by themselves, measure a state’s overall progress or determine its Year 2 award. Unexpended funds are subject to redistribution under the statute. This redistribution process is based on the same methodology used to determine each state’s allocation amount and would be allocated to the other states in a different distribution cycle. It is distinct from the annual process CMS uses to determine each state’s next RHTP award. What Happens Next? This year’s awards mark the next phase of implementation as states build on the groundwork and partnerships established during the first year. States will begin more detailed quarterly reporting in November 2026. States will continue submitting required quarterly and annual reports to CMS throughout the program. CMS will use information submitted by states to monitor implementation, provide technical assistance, assess compliance, and inform annual funding determinations. Over time, this data will help CMS assess progress toward the access, workforce, health, technology, and sustainability goals states committed to pursue through their transformation plans. New RHTP funding allocations continue through FY 2030, and all RHTP funds must be expended by Sept. 30, 2032, or they will be returned to the U.S. Treasury. Additional Resources • Rural Health Transformation Program Webpage • RHTP Notice of Funding Opportunity • September 2026 RHTP Post-Award Frequently Asked Questions • RHTP Reporting and Rescoring Webinar • State Program Snapshots • RHTP Obligation Funds Report, Year 1 • State Data Maps CMS News and Media Group Catherine Howden, Director Media Inquiries Form 202-690-6145

### [A blended digital parenting intervention for the prevention of sexual violence, exploitative sex, and maltreatment of children at scale in Tanzania: a cluster-randomised, controlled trial.](https://doi.org/10.1016/s0140-6736(26)01819-2)

- Source: `lancet_abstracts` · published `2026-11-10` · freshness `stale`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `ineligible`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:43008768`

<h4>Background</h4>Childhood sexual, physical, and verbal violence are global public health priorities, affecting 1 billion children each year. ParentApp integrates sexual violence prevention into WHO-UNICEF's open-access Parenting for Lifelong Health programme, using innovative blended digital delivery. We aimed to test the efficacy of ParentApp in preventing sexual violence and maltreatment against adolescent-aged children and improving preventive mechanisms such as family mental health and adolescent-caregiver relationships.<h4>Methods</h4>This cluster-randomised, controlled trial was conducted in 80 Tanzanian sub-wards. Participants were primary caregivers aged 18 years and older and their adolescents (aged 10-17 years). Sub-wards were randomly assigned (1:1) to receive either ParentApp (intervention group) or a control WASHApp programme. Clusters were randomly assigned in blocks of six, stratified by urban versus rural location. Statisticians carrying out randomisation and data analysts were masked; it was not possible to mask participants or researchers. Intervention groups received a low-cost Android phone installed with ParentApp, a 12-module offline-first app, with one in-person orientation session and remote support via WhatsApp group chats, at a delivery cost of US$6 per family at scale, or $10 with 20% smartphone provision. Control groups received a low-cost Android phone installed with WASHApp, a 12-module offline-first app for water, sanitation, and hygiene, with one in-person orientation session. The primary outcomes were adolescent sexual violence victimisation and maltreatment and high-risk situations for sexual violence, assessed in all participants irrespective of uptake and engagement at 5 months and 18 months post-baseline. The trial was registered on March 14, 2023, with OSF (https://osf.io/t9fxz) and is completed.<h4>Findings</h4>Between May 11 and Oct 9, 2023, 2692 caregiver-adolescent dyads were screened for study eligibility; 2396 adolescents (1217 [50·8%] female and 1179 [49·2%] male) and 2396 caregivers (1795 [74·9%] female and 601 [25·1%] male) were recruited. 2145 (89·5%) adolescents and 2252 (94·0%) caregivers were retained in the study and included in analyses at 18 months. Adolescent girls in ParentApp clusters had a substantial reduction in past-month sexual violence victimisation compared with those in control clusters (incidence rate ratio [IRR] 0·43 [95% CI 0·21-0·86]; p=0·018), with no effect for boys (1·21 [0·59-2·43]; p=0·60). Past-month maltreatment (physical abuse, verbal abuse, and neglect) was lower in ParentApp clusters than in control clusters (adolescent report IRR 0·92 [0·88-0·96]; p<0·0001, caregiver report 0·87 [0·82-0·92]; p<0·0001). Past-month exposure to high-risk situations for sexual violence was lower in ParentApp clusters than in control clusters (adolescent girls IRR 0·87 [0·77-0·97]; p=0·012, adolescent boys 0·85 [0·79-0·91]; p<0·0001, caregiver report 0·81 [0·75-0·88]; p<0·0001). There were no reported adverse events.<h4>Interpretation</h4>A low-cost blended digital parenting programme in countries such as Tanzania could simultaneously reduce childhood sexual violence (for girls) and maltreatment and sexual violence risk situations (for girls and boys).<h4>Funding</h4>UK Research and Innovation Global Challenges Research Fund, The Oak Foundation, The LEGO Foundation, The European Research Council, and an anonymous donor.

### [Reframing disease elimination: an update from the Lancet Commission on chronic obstructive pulmonary disease.](https://doi.org/10.1016/s0140-6736(26)01947-1)

- Source: `lancet_abstracts` · published `2026-11-10` · freshness `stale`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `ineligible`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:43008767`

Chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD) remains a leading cause of morbidity and mortality worldwide, with increasing global burden driven by population growth and ageing, persistent harmful exposures, and substantial health inequities. The Lancet Commission on COPD articulated an ambitious vision for disease elimination with prevention, early detection, and intervention. This Review revisits that vision, assesses progress since 2022, and calls for a broader commitment to lifelong lung health as an update of the Commission. Across seven sections, this Review advances a unified framework integrating the biology of lung development and ageing, precision medicine, patient-centred outcomes, and regulatory reform. COPD is not a single, uniform disease; rather, a biologically complex condition shaped by genetic, developmental, environmental, infectious, and behavioural factors across the entire life course. Effective prevention and care should therefore move beyond the detection and treatment of late physiological impairment toward earlier disease recognition, biological stratification, and interventions to preserve lung health, reduce symptoms, and prevent morbidity and premature mortality. The Review charts a path from reactive, late-stage management toward proactive, equitable, precision-based prevention and care. The central premise is unequivocal: the global burden of COPD can be substantially reduced. Success should be measured not only by gains in lung function and survival but also by improvements in daily functioning, quality of life, and wellbeing. In this expanded vision, COPD is no longer viewed solely as a chronic disease to be managed but as a preventable and modifiable condition within a broader commitment to lifelong lung health.

<h4>Objectives</h4>To evaluate trends in pregabalin use and overdose in Australia and examine temporal relationships with media coverage (December 2018), prescribing and dispensing restrictions (April 2020), and boxed warnings (February 2021).<h4>Design</h4>Retrospective observational study of dispensing and overdose data, with changepoint, interrupted time series, and latent class analysis.<h4>Setting and participants</h4>Pharmaceutical Benefits Scheme data (PBS; 10% random sample of people with drug subsidies) and overdose calls (self-harm, recreational use, and misuse) reported to Australia's largest Poisons Centre (50% national sample), 2016-24.<h4>Main outcome measures</h4>Population adjusted rates of dispensing and overdose, timing of breakpoints and rate ratios, proportion of people who use pregabalin at high risk of misuse.<h4>Results</h4>227 864 people were dispensed 3 441 851 pregabalin prescriptions in the PBS 10% sample and 4871 pregabalin overdose calls were made to the Poisons Centre. Pregabalin use peaked in 2018 before plateauing and declining. Three breakpoints were found in dispensing data, with major decreases in level and slope: December 2018, May 2020, December 2021. The combined rate ratio for dispensing was 0.748 (95% confidence interval 0.700 to 0.800). Compared with the projected historical trend in the absence of interventions (counterfactual), approximately 118 390 (93 960 to 140 940) fewer pregabalin prescriptions were recorded nationally in December 2021, and an ongoing reduction of 0.7% (0.5% to 0.9%) was found each month. All breakpoints were associated with declines in overdose rates, with a combined rate ratio of 0.532 (95% CI 0.406 to 0.695). This reduction corresponded to approximately 41.3 (26.9 to 52.4) fewer pregabalin overdose calls in December 2021 and an ongoing reduction of 1.6% (1.0% to 2.3%) each month. Latent class analysis of 57 744 people dispensed pregabalin in the 12 months after the final breakpoint revealed four groups. The high risk group (7790, 13.5%) were more likely male, younger, dispensed more pregabalin, with shorter refill interval and high rates of opioids and benzodiazepine co-use (5120 (65.7%) and 2455 (31.5%), respectively).<h4>Conclusions</h4>Pregabalin use and overdose in Australia have declined in recent years. Major decreases in trend corresponded with media coverage (December 2018) and prescribing and dispensing restrictions (May 2020). The third breakpoint may be a delayed impact of boxed warnings (implemented February 2021). These findings suggest that regulatory and risk communication measures may contribute to reducing pregabalin related harm.

<h4>Importance</h4>Current treatments approved by the US Food and Drug Administration for internal actinide contamination (pentetate calcium trisodium and pentetate zinc trisodium) require intravenous injection or nebulized inhalation under medical supervision and have limited radionuclide coverage. HOPO 14-1, an oral form of 3,4,3-LI(1,2-HOPO), is the first shelf-stable, broad-spectrum actinide chelator in clinical development that does not require intravenous access or trained personnel. In preclinical models, 3,4,3-LI(1,2-HOPO) removed a broad range of actinides, and its oral route supports scalable emergency medical use.<h4>Objective</h4>To evaluate the safety, tolerability, pharmacokinetic parameters, and excretion of a single dose of HOPO 14-1 in healthy adults.<h4>Design, setting, and participants</h4>This was a phase 1 nonrandomized clinical trial including 42 healthy adults (6 per dose level) with evaluations for 14 days after administration of a single dose and conducted at a single site in the US. Participants were recruited and screened between March 2023 and July 2025 and follow-up was completed in August 2025.<h4>Intervention</h4>Single oral dose of HOPO 14-1 administered at 1 of 7 dose levels (100 mg, 200 mg, 500 mg, 1200 mg, 2500 mg, 5000 mg, or 7500 mg) with follow-up through day 14 after dose administration.<h4>Main outcomes and measures</h4>The primary outcomes were the incidence and severity of adverse events, drug-related adverse events, and serious adverse events. The secondary outcomes were plasma pharmacokinetic parameters and cumulative urinary and fecal excretion.<h4>Results</h4>Of the 42 healthy adults enrolled (median age, 43 [IQR, 34-51] years; 64% female), all received a single dose of HOPO 14-1 (administered across all dose levels as assigned). There were no serious adverse events or dose-limiting toxic effects. The drug-related adverse events included diarrhea (31%), vomiting (14%), headache (12%), and fatigue (7%); all were mild to moderate in severity. Gastrointestinal adverse events were the principal tolerability findings and most evident at the highest doses. The plasma pharmacokinetic parameters demonstrated rapid absorption (median time of maximum plasma concentration, 0.86 [range, 0.53-1.60] hours to 1.90 [range, 1.40-2.90] hours) and dose-dependent increases in systemic exposure, with greater than proportional increases at higher doses. Fecal excretion was the primary route of elimination; urinary excretion was minimal.<h4>Conclusions and relevance</h4>Single oral doses of HOPO 14-1 up to 7500 mg were administered without dose-limiting toxicity or serious adverse events in healthy adults. Gastrointestinal tolerability defined the practical single-dose ceiling. These findings establish the first human safety and pharmacokinetic profile of a novel, orally administered actinide chelator and support further clinical evaluation of HOPO 14-1 as a countermeasure for internal radionuclide and heavy metal contamination. Development of a deployable and population-level emergency intervention addresses a long-standing unmet need with important implications for public health preparedness.<h4>Trial registration</h4>ClinicalTrials.gov Identifier: NCT05628961.

<h4>Background</h4>Rapid weight loss, as induced by GLP-1 receptor agonists, is accompanied by pronounced muscle loss. GDF8 (myostatin) and activin A (ActA), acting via ActRIIA/B receptors, are negative regulators of muscle mass. The aim of this study was to evaluate the effect of antibodies blocking GDF8 (trevogrumab) and ActA (garetosmab) on semaglutide-induced weight and muscle loss.<h4>Methods</h4>COURAGE is a two-part, phase 2, double-blind, placebo-controlled, randomised trial. This multicentre (60 sites), US-based trial enrolled participants aged 18-80 years with obesity (BMI ≥30 kg/m<sup>2</sup>). In part A, participants were treated for 26 weeks with semaglutide (titrated to 2·4 mg subcutaneously weekly) in addition to a randomly assigned (1:1:1:1) concomitant treatment: placebo, trevogrumab 200 mg, trevogrumab 400 mg, or trevogrumab 400 mg and garetosmab 10 mg/kg. Trevogrumab was administered subcutaneously weekly and garetosmab was administered intravenously every 4 weeks. Part A also included a subsequent semaglutide withdrawal period, at which point participants (randomly assigned at baseline 1:1 within each group) received either trevogrumab 400 mg or placebo for an additional 26 weeks. In part B, participants were treated with semaglutide for 52 weeks and randomly assigned (1:2:2) to concomitant treatment with placebo, trevogrumab 25 mg, or trevogrumab 75 mg. Groups were stratified according to sex, BMI (<35 kg/m<sup>2</sup>vs ≥35 kg/m<sup>2</sup>), and participation in an exploratory MRI substudy assessing thigh muscle volume. Study participants, the principal investigators, and study site personnel were masked to all randomisation assignments throughout the study. Primary endpoints were percent change in lean mass, fat mass, and bodyweight from baseline to week 26 (part A) or week 52 (part B). Efficacy and safety analyses were done in all randomly assigned participants who received at least one dose of a study drug (efficacy, as randomly assigned; safety, as treated). COURAGE is registered with ClinicalTrials.gov, NCT06299098, and is currently active, not recruiting.<h4>Findings</h4>975 participants were enrolled between June 21, 2024, and April 14, 2025. Mean age was 49·0 years (SD 13·2) and mean BMI was 36·7 kg/m<sup>2</sup> (SD 5·0); 627 (64%) of 975 participants were female and 348 (36%) were male. In part A (n=605, of whom n=599 received at least one dose of study drug), a substantial decrease in lean mass was observed in the semaglutide plus placebo group at week 26 (-6·6% [95% CI -7·4 to -5·7]); the estimated treatment difference (ETD) versus semaglutide plus placebo was +3·1% (95% CI 1·9 to 4·3) with semaglutide plus trevogrumab 200 mg, +2·7% (1·5 to 4·0) with semaglutide plus trevogrumab 400 mg, and +4·5% (3·1 to 5·9) with semaglutide plus trevogrumab 400 mg plus garetosmab 10 mg/kg. In part B (n=376), lean mass percent change from baseline to week 52 for semaglutide plus placebo was -7·3% (-8·8 to -5·8); the ETD versus semaglutide plus placebo was +1·5% (-0·3 to 3·3) for semaglutide plus trevogrumab 25 mg, and +3·1% (1·3 to 4·9) for semaglutide plus trevogrumab 75 mg. Change from baseline in fat mass in part A was -15·6% (-17·4 to -13·9) in the semaglutide plus placebo group; the ETD was significant in the semaglutide plus trevogrumab 400 mg group (-3·4% [-5·9 to -0·9]) and the semaglutide plus trevogrumab 400 mg plus garetosmab 10 mg/kg group (-11·7% [-14·4 to -9·0]) in part A. No other groups in part A nor any in part B had significantly different changes in fat mass compared with semaglutide plus placebo. Percent change in bodyweight in part A was -10·6 (-11·6 to -9·7) in the semaglutide plus placebo group; the ETD was significant for the semaglutide plus trevogrumab 400 mg plus garetosmab 10 mg/kg group (-2·7 [-4·1 to -1·2]). No other groups in part A nor any in part B had significantly different changes in bodyweight compared with semaglutide plus placebo. Semaglutide plus trevogrumab (25 mg, 75 mg, 200 mg, or 400 mg) was generally well tolerated with a safety profile similar to semaglutide plus placebo; however, semaglutide plus trevogrumab 400 mg plus garetosmab 10 mg/kg had a high incidence of serious adverse events (14 [9%] of 149) and treatment discontinuations (48 [32%] of 149). Gastrointestinal adverse events were the most commonly reported, and they occurred with similar incidence across treatment groups. Muscle spasms occurred more frequently in the semaglutide plus trevogrumab 400 mg plus garetosmab 10 mg/kg group (61 [41%] of 149) compared with the semaglutide plus trevogrumab 200 mg or 400 mg groups (23 [8%] of 299) and semaglutide plus placebo groups (seven [5%] of 151). Most adverse events were mild to moderate in severity. Two deaths occurred during the study, both in the semaglutide plus trevogrumab 400 mg plus garetosmab 10 mg/kg group; a causal association between treatment and these events was not identified.<h4>Interpretation</h4>This study establishes proof of concept for GDF8 inhibition by trevogrumab alone as a generally well tolerated strategy to preserve lean mass during GLP-1 receptor agonist-induced weight loss. Although the addition of ActA inhibition by garetosmab further improved body composition and weight loss, the combination did not have favourable safety and tolerability. Further study is required to understand whether improved body composition during weight loss might preserve physical function and overall metabolic health.<h4>Funding</h4>Regeneron Pharmaceuticals, Inc.

During a median follow-up of 3.8 years, the primary cognitive outcome of all cause dementia or mild cognitive impairment occurred in 747 participants (3.69 events per 100 person years) in the intensive treatment group and 783 participants (3.89 events per 100 person years) in the standard treatment group (hazard ratio 0.96, 95% confidence interval (CI) 0.86 to 1.06; P=0.38). In the MRI substudy (428 intensive treatment <i>v</i> 434 standard treatment), the intensive treatment group had a smaller increase in white matter lesion volume compared with the standard treatment group (between group difference -0.09 inverse hyperbolic sine (asinh) transformed cm<sup>3</sup>, 95% CI -0.15 to -0.02; P=0.01).<h4>Conclusions</h4>Over a median of about four years, no statistically significant difference in incidence of a composite of all cause dementia or mild cognitive impairment was observed among adults with type 2 diabetes receiving standard treatment of SBP (target <140 mm Hg) and intensive treatment (target <120 mm Hg). Intensive treatment was, however, associated with a reduction in the accrual of cerebral white matter lesion volume.<h4>Trial registration</h4>ClinicalTrials.gov NCT03808311.

Median PFS by blinded independent central review was 4.17 months (95% CI, 3.61-4.53 months) with izalontamab brengitecan vs 1.97 months (95% CI, 1.61-2.73 months) with chemotherapy (hazard ratio, 0.50; 95% CI, 0.40-0.63; log-rank P < .001). Median OS was 9.79 months (95% CI, 7.89-11.01 months) with izalontamab brengitecan vs 7.20 months (95% CI, 6.18-7.82 months) with chemotherapy (hazard ratio, 0.63; 95% CI, 0.48-0.82; log-rank P < .001). ### [Lynch syndrome.](https://doi.org/10.1016/s0140-6736(26)01600-4)

- Source: `lancet_abstracts` · published `2026-11-12` · freshness `stale`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `ineligible`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:43020098`

Lynch syndrome is the most common inherited cancer predisposition syndrome, affecting approximately one in 350-450 individuals, yet the majority of carriers remain undiagnosed, representing a substantial and preventable public health burden. Caused by germline pathogenic variants in DNA mismatch repair (MMR) genes, Lynch syndrome confers markedly elevated and heterogeneous risks of colorectal, endometrial, and multiple extracolonic cancers. Advances in molecular diagnostics, particularly universal tumour testing for MMR deficiency, have transformed case identification and enabled targeted germline testing and cascade screening of relatives at risk. Despite these advances, implementation remains inconsistent, with substantial attrition across diagnostic pathways and inequities in access to care. Preventive strategies-including colonoscopic surveillance, aspirin chemoprevention, and risk-reducing surgery-are effective but require gene-specific and individualised application. Therapeutically, the immunogenic biology of MMR-deficient tumours has positioned Lynch syndrome at the forefront of precision oncology, with immune checkpoint inhibitors delivering substantial benefit and emerging vaccine strategies offering promise for cancer interception. In this Review, we provide an integrated overview of Lynch syndrome across the continuum of care, highlighting advances in biology, diagnosis, prevention, and treatment, while emphasising the crucial need to align scientific progress with equitable and effective health-system delivery.

### [Treatment of Acute Ischemic Stroke: A Review.](https://doi.org/10.1001/jama.2026.19482)

- Source: `jama_abstracts` · published `2026-11-12` · freshness `stale`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `ineligible`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:43018963`

<h4>Importance</h4>Acute ischemic stroke, caused by thrombotic or embolic occlusion of a cervical or cerebral artery, leads to neurologic injury due to sudden disruption of blood flow to the brain. Ischemic stroke is a leading cause of disability and death and accounts for about 85% of strokes in the US.<h4>Observations</h4>Symptoms of acute ischemic stroke vary based on the area of the affected brain. Common presentations include sudden arm or leg weakness, aphasia or slurred speech, sudden loss of balance or coordination, sudden visual difficulties, and/or facial drooping. Patients with suspected stroke should be transported to the nearest emergency department for urgent brain imaging, typically noncontrast computed tomography (CT). Based on 2026 American Heart Association/American Stroke Association (AHA/ASA) guidelines, first-line therapy for patients with acute ischemic stroke presenting within 4.5 hours from symptom onset with disabling symptoms (eg, affecting the ability to perform activities of daily living) is intravenous thrombolysis (tenecteplase or alteplase) in those without intracranial hemorrhage or other contraindications to thrombolysis. The treatment window can be extended to 9 hours-and up to 24 hours in patients with large vessel occlusion (eg, involving the internal carotid artery or proximal middle cerebral artery [M1 segment]) who are unable to receive thrombectomy-based on identification of salvageable tissue using perfusion imaging with CT perfusion or magnetic resonance imaging. In appropriately selected patients with acute ischemic stroke, pooled randomized trial data show an approximately 10% absolute increase in the probability of a better functional outcome with intravenous thrombolysis. Based on 2026 AHA/ASA guidelines, endovascular thrombectomy, which involves clot removal with an intra-arterial mechanical device, is recommended for patients with large vessel occlusion within 6 hours of stroke onset (class 1 recommendation) and is recommended in selected patients with large vessel occlusion within 24 hours of last known well (class 1 recommendation). Endovascular therapy should be performed after intravenous thrombolysis unless patients have contraindications to thrombolytic therapy. Compared with medical management, which includes intravenous thrombolysis when indicated, endovascular thrombectomy produces an approximately 20% absolute increase in functional independence.<h4>Conclusions and relevance</h4>Acute ischemic stroke causes substantial disability and mortality. First-line therapy for patients with disabling acute stroke symptoms is intravenous thrombolysis (if no contraindications) within 4.5 hours of stroke onset and up to 24 hours in carefully selected patients not receiving mechanical thrombectomy; for large vessel occlusions, mechanical thrombectomy may be performed up to 24 hours after stroke onset in eligible patients.

### [AI-Driven Reconstruction of Online Health Narratives Using Agent-Action-Outcome Modeling: Algorithm Development and Validation](https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e83496/)

- Source: `jmir_ai` · published `2026-11-13` · freshness `stale`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `ineligible`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e83496/`

Background: People share health experiences online as stories, not isolated facts. Most computational systems reduce these narratives to keywords or claims, overlooking the agency, causality, and interpretation that determine whether health information is safe or potentially harmful to act on. Content can use medically accurate terminology while describing unsafe behavior, rendering it invisible to fact-checking and keyword approaches. This limitation, which we term , constrains the ability of computational systems to extract, represent, and reason over narratives as coherent wholes. Objective: This study introduces the Narrative Builder, a computational framework that reconstructs unstructured online health stories into machine-readable agent-action-outcome (AAO) narrative graphs, preserving structural, causal, and interpretive dimensions. We formalize narrative blindness as a measurable property of natural language processing systems and evaluate whether theory-grounded reconstruction can address it. Methods: We operationalized Labov and Waletzky’s sociolinguistic narrative theory within a computational AAO schema, enriched with interpretive dimensions from psychological and clinical communication frameworks. From 5386 Reddit health threads (spanning February 2019 to November 2024), 2 domain experts (a biomedical researcher and a physician) annotated a gold-standard dataset of 2000 segments from 704 threads, with interrater reliability (Krippendorff α) of 0.75 to 0.82 across all annotated tasks (0.78-0.81 for the 4 interpretive classification tasks). A dual-model architecture combining BioBERT (Bidirectional Encoder Representations From Transformers for Biomedical Text Mining) with a conditional random field (CRF) layer for AAO extraction and multitask DistilBERT for interpretive classification was evaluated using 5-fold grouped cross-validation, grouped on the annotation-level key to prevent data leakage, with a separate held-out test set and a McNemar test against term frequency–inverse document frequency (TF-IDF) and BioBERT-without-CRF baselines. Results: The Narrative Builder achieved a span-level -score of 0.850 for AAO extraction on the held-out test set, with per-type of 0.90 (action), 0.83 (agent), and 0.77 (outcome), outperforming the TF-IDF baseline (0.781; &lt;.001). Labovian stage classification reached macro- of 0.501, and interpretive dimensions reached macro- values of 0.466 (emotion), 0.528 (stance), and 0.465 (tone). Clinical-value extraction achieved a precision of 0.90, and Unified Medical Language System concept normalization reached 100% coverage across 12,101 queries. A rule-based causal direction classifier identified 17.3% of segments as candidate outcome-to-action (inverted) patterns, though validation showed limited precision (0.277), and this distribution is reported as exploratory. Single-rater expert review of generated graphs indicated narrative coherence (mean 0.89, SD 0.08) and medical plausibility (mean 0.91, SD 0.07). Conclusions: The Narrative Builder reads health stories as coherent wholes rather than isolated facts, producing AAO narrative graphs that retain the agents, actions, outcomes, and emotional framing that determine how individuals interpret and act on health information. By formalizing narrative blindness as a measurable property and reconstructing narrative structure with strong extraction performance, this work establishes narrative reconstruction as a feasible complement to fact-based health informatics, providing structured input for downstream credibility assessment. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/6b371d6b6e7bd1aaeea5f3123833a13d" />

### [Effects of plastic exposure on human health.](https://doi.org/10.1136/bmj-2026-100388)

- Source: `bmj_abstracts` · published `2026-11-13` · freshness `stale`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `ineligible`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:43025876`

Plastic pollution is a critical environmental and public health challenge, with growing evidence linking human exposure to microplastics, nanoplastics, and associated chemicals to adverse health outcomes. This review summarises current knowledge on the health effects of plastic exposure, addressing both established findings and critical knowledge gaps. We examine human exposure to plastics and associated health risks based on available clinical and epidemiological evidence. Additionally, we explore chemicals associated with plastic, including their potential as carcinogens and disruptors of endocrine and immune function. Recognising the urgent need for preventive measures, we examine emerging strategies for reducing plastic burdens, including global regulatory efforts like the United Nations Plastics Treaty and proposed public health guidelines. By integrating current evidence, this review aims to provide a foundation for advancing research and informing strategies to mitigate health risks associated with plastic.

### [Fluid eMTBR-tau243 as a Biomarker of Mixed 3-Repeat and 4-Repeat Tau Pathology.](https://doi.org/10.1001/jama.2026.24689)

- Source: `jama_abstracts` · published `2026-11-13` · freshness `stale`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `ineligible`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:43026662`

<h4>Importance</h4>Neurodegenerative diseases with abnormal tau protein aggregation in the brain (ie, tauopathies) can be classified by the tau isoform that accumulates: 3-repeat (3R), 4-repeat (4R), or mixed 3R/4R tauopathies, which include chronic traumatic encephalopathy (CTE), some forms of frontotemporal lobar degeneration, and Alzheimer disease (AD). The endogenously cleaved form of tau microtubule binding region containing the residue 243 (eMTBR-tau243) can be detected in cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) and blood and has been associated with AD tau pathology, but its specificity and biological interpretation in non-AD tauopathies remains unclear.<h4>Objective</h4>To evaluate biofluid eMTBR-tau243 in a range of tauopathies.<h4>Design, setting, and participants</h4>This case-control study included 4 biorepositories based in the US that contributed CSF and plasma samples from participants with and without tauopathies, many of whom had antemortem biofluid samples paired with autopsy or genetically defined neuropathology. Samples were analyzed between August 2023 and December 2025. Data analysis occurred from August 2024 to July 2026.<h4>Exposure</h4>CSF and plasma eMTBR-tau243 concentrations measured using a mass spectrometry-based assay.<h4>Main outcomes and measures</h4>Outcomes included mixed 3R/4R tauopathy (eg, AD and CTE) based on autopsy, frontotemporal lobar degeneration with MAPT variants (FTLD-MAPT), and CTE stage. Differences in biomarker levels between groups were evaluated using analysis of variance or analysis of covariance, and area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUC) was calculated to evaluate the discriminative performance of fluid eMTBR-tau243.<h4>Results</h4>Among the 229 participants (196 with CSF samples and 55 with plasma samples [22 with both]), mean (SD) age at fluid sample collection was 63 (13) years and 108 (47%) were female. Median (IQR) CSF eMTBR-tau243 was increased in 10 patients with AD (0.459 [0.246-0.801] fmol/mL) compared with 29 cognitively unimpaired controls (0.002 [0.001-0.005] fmol/mL) (adjusted P < .001), but other autopsy-confirmed non-AD tauopathies (which included 52 individuals with conditions including progressive supranuclear palsy and corticobasal degeneration) did not demonstrate an increase (0.015 [0.008, 0.039] fmol/mL). Six symptomatic FTLD-MAPT variant carriers with mixed 3R/4R tauopathy (R406W, V337M, and G389R) had increased median (IQR) CSF eMTBR-tau243 (0.140 [0.055-0.174] fmol/mL) compared with 15 4R tauopathy variant carriers including N279K and P301L (0.004 [0.001-0.044] fmol/mL) (P = .001). Median (IQR) plasma eMTBR-tau243 was increased in 11 patients with CTE (1.93 [0.42-3.68] fmol/L) compared with 20 cognitively unimpaired controls (0.29 [0.05-0.62] fmol/L) (adjusted P = .007), but did not increase as high as among 14 patients with AD (11.25 [2.29-19.38] fmol/L). Plasma eMTBR-tau243 distinguished CTE stage III and IV from controls (AUC, 0.97; 95% CI, 0.92-1.00), but not CTE stage I from controls (AUC, 0.64; 95% CI, 0.32-0.97).<h4>Conclusions and relevance</h4>This study found that plasma and CSF eMTBR-tau243 were increased in mixed 3R/4R tauopathies including AD, high-stage CTE, and a subset of genetic FTLD-MAPT, compared with cognitively unimpaired controls. Further validation in larger cohorts is needed to determine the clinical utility of eMTBR-tau243 as a potential diagnostic biomarker for CTE and other tauopathies beyond AD.

### [Once-Weekly Ribupatide for Weight Loss in Adults in China With Overweight or Obesity: The GEMINI-1 Randomized Clinical Trial.](https://doi.org/10.1001/jama.2026.24796)

<h4>Importance</h4>Obesity is a major health problem in China; more pharmacotherapy choices for long-term weight management are needed.<h4>Objective</h4>To evaluate the efficacy and safety of once-weekly subcutaneous ribupatide, a dual glucose-dependent insulinotropic polypeptide/glucagon-like peptide-1 receptor agonist, for weight loss in adults in China with overweight or obesity without diabetes.<h4>Design, setting, and participants</h4>This multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, phase 3 clinical trial was conducted at 55 sites in China. From May to June 2024, adults without diabetes who had a body mass index of 28 or greater or 24 or greater with at least 1 weight-related comorbidity were enrolled, with final follow-up on June 20, 2025.<h4>Interventions</h4>Participants were randomized to receive once-weekly subcutaneous ribupatide (target doses of 2 mg, 4 mg, or 6 mg) or matching placebo for 48 weeks, with dose escalation for up to 20 weeks and concomitant lifestyle intervention.<h4>Main outcomes and measures</h4>The primary outcome was change in body weight from baseline to week 48, including percent change in body weight and the percentage of participants achieving at least 5% body weight reduction, analyzed under the treatment policy estimand.<h4>Results</h4>Among 567 randomized participants (309 [54.5%] female; mean [SD] age was 34.4 [8.4] years, body weight was 93.0 [17.3] kg, and body mass index was 33.3 [4.5]), 514 (90.7%) completed the 48-week treatment period. Least-squares mean percentage changes in body weight at week 48 were -10.7%, -16.4%, and -17.7% with ribupatide 2, 4, and 6 mg, respectively, vs -1.4% with placebo. At week 48, the placebo-adjusted differences in percentage change in body weight were -9.3 (95% CI, -11.4 to -7.3) percentage points with ribupatide 2 mg, -15.0 (95% CI, -17.1 to -12.9) percentage points with ribupatide 4 mg, and -16.3 (95% CI, -18.4 to -14.2) percentage points with ribupatide 6 mg (P < .001 for all). For at least 5% body weight reduction at week 48, the multiply imputed response rates were 68.1% (95% CI, 59.9% to 75.3%) for ribupatide 2 mg (97 of 142), 88.0% (95% CI, 81.3% to 92.6%) for ribupatide 4 mg (124 of 141), 85.7% (95% CI, 78.6% to 90.7%) with ribupatide 6 mg (121 of 141), and 16.0% (95% CI, 10.8% to 23.1%) with placebo (23 of 143). Placebo-adjusted differences for ribupatide 2, 4, and 6 mg were 52.0 (95% CI, 42.2 to 61.9), 72.0 (95% CI, 63.7 to 80.2), and 69.7 (95% CI, 61.1 to 78.3) percentage points, respectively (all P < .001). The most frequent treatment-emergent adverse events with ribupatide were gastrointestinal events, occurring in 47.9%, 58.2%, and 63.1% of participants receiving ribupatide 2 mg, 4 mg, and 6 mg, respectively, compared with 23.1% receiving placebo; most were mild to moderate in severity and few participants (0.7%) permanently discontinued study treatment because of adverse events.<h4>Conclusions and relevance</h4>Among adults in China with overweight or obesity without diabetes, once-weekly subcutaneous ribupatide resulted in significantly greater weight loss at 48 weeks. Gastrointestinal adverse events were the most frequent tolerability findings.<h4>Trial registration</h4>ClinicalTrials.gov Identifier: NCT06396429.

### [Tirzepatide in Adolescents With Obesity: The SURMOUNT-ADOLESCENTS Randomized Clinical Trial.](https://doi.org/10.1001/jama.2026.23420)

<h4>Importance</h4>Information on the effects of tirzepatide, a once-weekly glucose-dependent insulinotropic polypeptide and glucagon-like peptide-1 receptor agonist approved for obesity management in adults with obesity, remains limited in adolescents.<h4>Objective</h4>To evaluate the efficacy and safety of once-weekly tirzepatide in adolescents with obesity or overweight with at least 1 obesity-related complication.<h4>Design, setting, and participants</h4>This phase 3, randomized, double-blind, 72-week clinical trial was conducted at 31 medical research centers and hospitals in Canada, the Czech Republic, Israel, Italy, Poland, and the US from October 16, 2023, to June 2, 2026, among adolescents aged 12 years to younger than 18 years with obesity or overweight with at least 1 obesity-related complication.<h4>Interventions</h4>Participants were randomized 2:1 to receive once-weekly subcutaneous tirzepatide, escalated to a maximum tolerated dose (5, 10, or 15 mg), or placebo for 72 weeks, in addition to healthy lifestyle intervention.<h4>Main outcomes and measures</h4>The primary end point was percent change in body mass index (BMI; calculated as weight in kilograms divided by height in meters squared) from baseline to week 72. Key secondary end points included percentages of participants achieving BMI reductions of at least 5%, at least 10%, at least 15%, and at least 20%; changes in BMI SD score; BMI expressed as a percentage of the 95th percentile; and body weight, waist circumference, and physical functioning from baseline to week 72. Safety outcomes included treatment-emergent adverse events, serious adverse events, and discontinuations due to adverse events.<h4>Results</h4>A total of 160 participants were randomized to receive tirzepatide (n = 109) or placebo (n = 51). Mean (SD) age at baseline was 14.6 (1.6) years, 99 participants (61.9%) were female, mean body weight was 109.7 kg, and mean BMI was 38.6. At week 72, tirzepatide was associated with 18.5% reduction in BMI, compared with 0.5% increase in BMI with placebo, demonstrating superiority over placebo (estimated treatment difference, -19.0 [95% CI, -23.9 to -14.1] percentage points; P < .001). Of 10 key secondary outcomes, all demonstrated statistically significant differences favoring tirzepatide compared with placebo. Overall, 79.6% of tirzepatide-treated participants achieved BMI reduction of at least 5% compared with 28.5% of participants in the placebo group (risk difference, 51.2 [95% CI, 36.3-66.1] percentage points; P < .001). The most common adverse events with tirzepatide treatment were gastrointestinal (mostly mild to moderate).<h4>Conclusions and relevance</h4>In adolescents with obesity or overweight with obesity-related complications, tirzepatide, in addition to healthy lifestyle intervention, led to significant reductions in percent change in BMI compared with placebo at 72 weeks.<h4>Trial registration</h4>ClinicalTrials.gov Identifier: NCT06075667.

### [A Plasma eMTBR-Tau Immunoassay for Detection of Advanced Insoluble Tau Pathology.](https://doi.org/10.1001/jama.2026.25143)

- Source: `jama_abstracts` · published `2026-11-16` · freshness `stale`

<h4>Importance</h4>Blood biomarkers that reflect neurofibrillary tau burden, a defining hallmark of Alzheimer disease (AD), remain limited. Endogenously cleaved microtubule-binding region tau 243 (eMTBR-tau243) has shown promise when measured by mass spectrometry; a scalable immunoassay could broaden research access and support clinical evaluation.<h4>Objective</h4>To determine whether plasma levels of eMTBR-tau measured by a novel immunoassay are associated with tau neurofibrillary tangle pathology defined by neuropathology and tau positron emission tomography (PET).<h4>Design, setting, and participants</h4>This cross-sectional study included brain donors with antemortem plasma from 2 US autopsy cohorts, and individuals with plasma samples and tau PET from 2 imaging cohorts in Canada and Sweden, with visits between 2002 and 2025 depending on the cohort.<h4>Exposures</h4>Plasma eMTBR-tau measured with an immunoassay and eMTBR-tau243 measured with mass spectrometry.<h4>Main outcomes and measures</h4>Plasma eMTBR-tau levels across neuropathologic Braak neurofibrillary tangle stages (I-VI, ranging from tangles confined to the entorhinal region to widespread neocortical tangles) and tau-PET Braak stages (0-VI), expressed as fold changes. Correlations with regional tau-PET standardized uptake value ratios (SUVRs; higher values indicating more tau tangles), discrimination of regional tau-PET positivity (area under the curve [AUC]), and correlation with mass spectrometry-based eMTBR-tau243.<h4>Results</h4>The study included 728 participants in 4 cohorts (median age by cohort, 71.0-83.5 years; 41.3%-56.6% female). In the 2 autopsy cohorts (n = 241 and n = 240), eMTBR-tau age- and sex-adjusted geometric means were 0.0021 pg/mL and 0.0040 pg/mL, respectively, at Braak stages I-II vs 0.107 pg/mL and 0.101 pg/mL at Braak stage VI (50.5-fold [95% CI, 26.3-97.0] and 25.2-fold [95% CI, 15.8-40.3] higher for stage VI vs stages I-II; P < .001 for both cohorts). In one imaging cohort (n = 99), compared with tau-PET Braak stage 0, eMTBR-tau levels were 11.0-fold (95% CI, 6.9-17.5) higher at stages III-IV and 59.9-fold (95% CI, 39.5-90.7) higher at stages V-VI. Among participants positive for amyloid-β (Aβ) in another imaging cohort (n = 50), the Spearman rank correlations for eMTBR-tau with tau-PET SUVRs were ρ = 0.52 (95% CI, 0.28-0.70) in Braak regions I-II, 0.76 (95% CI, 0.61-0.86) in regions III-IV, and 0.85 (95% CI, 0.75-0.91) in regions V-VI (all P < .001). Immunoassay and mass spectrometry measurements were strongly correlated (Spearman rank correlation, ρ = 0.93 in all participants [n = 108] and ρ = 0.96 in Aβ-positive participants [n = 54]) and discriminated tau-PET positivity similarly (n = 102; AUC range, 0.96-0.99 for the immunoassay and 0.97-0.99 for mass spectrometry).<h4>Conclusions and relevance</h4>A plasma eMTBR-tau immunoassay identified neocortical tau pathology in autopsy and tau-PET cohorts and correlated strongly with mass spectrometry measurements, a proof of principle that eMTBR-tau can be measured by immunoassay as a marker of advanced neurofibrillary tangle pathology.

### [Comprehensive Regulations to Uncover Suspicious Healthcare (CRUSH) Proposed Rule (CMS-6098-P)](https://www.cms.gov/newsroom/fact-sheets/comprehensive-regulations-uncover-suspicious-healthcare-crush-proposed-rule-cms-6098-p)

- Source: `cms_fact_sheets` · published `2026-11-16` · freshness `stale`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `ineligible`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.cms.gov/newsroom/fact-sheets/comprehensive-regulations-uncover-suspicious-healthcare-crush-proposed-rule-cms-6098-p`

Comprehensive Regulations to Uncover Suspicious Healthcare (CRUSH) Proposed Rule (CMS-6098-P) On Nov. 16, 2026, the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services (CMS) issued a proposed rule that would strengthen program integrity (PI) oversight and the agency's ability to prevent, identify, and address fraud, waste, and abuse across Medicare, Medicaid, the Children's Health Insurance Program (CHIP), and the Health Insurance Marketplace. The Comprehensive Regulations to Uncover Suspicious Healthcare (CRUSH) proposed rule is a standalone, discretionary rule rather than an annual payment rule. It proposes changes to provider and supplier enrollment requirements; Medicare Advantage (MA) and Medicare Part D oversight; Medicaid and CHIP program integrity and eligibility oversight; Medicare durable medical equipment, prosthetics, orthotics, and supplies (DMEPOS) supplier requirements; and Marketplace agent and broker oversight. CMS intends to conduct program integrity rulemaking of this kind on an annual basis going forward. Background On Feb. 27, 2026, CMS issued a Request for Information (RFI) (91 FR 9803) seeking public input on regulatory changes that could help the agency more effectively prevent, identify, and address fraud, waste, and abuse and protect taxpayer dollars across its programs. CMS received over 560 comments and suggestions in response to the RFI, some of which informed the provisions of this proposed rule. This proposed rule supports Administration priorities on program integrity oversight, including the June 6, 2025, Presidential Memorandum on Eliminating Fraud, Waste, and Abuse in Medicaid, and Executive Order 14395, which established the White House Task Force to Eliminate Fraud. Medicare Provider Enrollment and Oversight Faster Reporting of Enrollment Changes Under current regulations, providers and suppliers generally have 30 calendar days to report critical changes to their Medicare enrollment information (such as changes in practice location, ownership, or adverse legal actions) and up to 90 calendar days to report other types of changes. CMS is proposing to require that all providers and suppliers report any change to their enrollment information within 30 calendar days, regardless of the type of change or the type of provider or supplier involved. CMS believes this accelerated timeline will improve the agency's ability to prevent, identify, and address fraud, waste, and abuse. New Grounds for Medicare Enrollment Denial and Revocation CMS is proposing to add a new ground for denial or revocation that would apply when a provider or supplier has a domestic or foreign owner, managing employee, organization, partner, officer, or director whose identity cannot be confirmed, or whom CMS determines poses an undue risk of fraud, waste, and abuse. CMS believes this new authority would help prevent bad actors from enrolling in, or remaining in, the Medicare program. Medicare Advantage and Part D Program Integrity Mandatory Fraud, Waste, and Abuse Reporting CMS currently encourages, but does not require, MA organizations and Part D plan sponsors to report referrals of substantiated or suspicious fraud, waste, and abuse. In addition, CMS requires reporting of inappropriate prescribing of opioids and payment suspensions for pharmacies based on credible allegations of fraud but does not require reporting of payment suspensions based on credible allegations of fraud for other provider or supplier types. For calendar year 2025, CMS determined, through its review of a frequency report, only 46% of Medicare Part C and Part D plan contracts voluntarily reported referrals of substantiated or suspicious fraud, waste, and abuse to CMS and only 19% of Medicare Part C and Part D plan contracts reported any payment suspensions based on credible allegations of fraud to CMS, including both pharmacies and other provider and supplier types. CMS is proposing to make referrals by MA organizations and Part D plan sponsors to CMS of substantiated or suspicious fraud, waste, and abuse mandatory and to expand MA organization and Part D plan sponsor reporting of payment suspensions based on credible allegations of fraud by pharmacies to all provider and supplier types. This proposal is also consistent with a July 2018 Office of Inspector General (OIG) recommendation that CMS require plan sponsors to report Part C and D fraud and abuse incidents and corrective actions to a centralized system. Medicare Advantage Provider Enrollment in Fee-for-Service Medicare Current regulations do not generally require providers and suppliers who bill MA plans to also be enrolled in Original Medicare. CMS currently relies on a preclusion list as an alternative screening mechanism, but this list operates reactively, after a provider has already begun billing MA plans, rather than screening providers before they begin furnishing services. CMS is proposing to add a requirement, at proposed § 422.222(a), that all eligible providers and suppliers billing MA be enrolled in Original Medicare in an approved status except for Program of All-Inclusive Care for the Elderly (PACE) plans. This would allow CMS to apply its full Original Medicare enrollment screening process, including identity verification, licensure review, site visits, and fingerprint-based background checks, to providers and suppliers participating in MA. If finalized, this requirement would take effect on the first day of the plan year beginning two years after publication of the final rule. Risk Adjustment Data Validation (RADV) Program Reforms The MA RADV program is CMS' primary way to address overpayments to MA organizations by identifying when there is a lack of documentation in the medical record to support the diagnoses reported for risk adjustment. Specifically, CMS is proposing to codify that it would not apply a fee-for-service (FFS) adjuster, would determine the applicable sampling methodology, and would begin extrapolation of audit findings with payment year (PY) 2018 audits. CMS estimates that this approach would enable the agency to collect an average of approximately $9 billion per payment year in MA program overpayments. RADV Hardship Exception Process Currently, the process for granting a hardship exception for medical record submission during a RADV audit exists only in sub-regulatory guidance, without a formal regulatory framework. CMS is proposing to establish a regulatory framework identifying the extraordinary circumstances that would qualify a plan sponsor for a hardship exception, which CMS expects would improve the consistency and transparency of hardship exception determinations. Medicare DMEPOS Program Integrity Declining Bonds from Noncompliant Sureties CMS currently lacks authority to decline a DMEPOS surety bond from a surety company that has previously failed to fulfill its financial obligations to CMS. CMS is proposing a new regulation, modeled on 31 C.F.R. § 223.17(b)(1), establishing a process by which CMS may decline bonds from noncompliant sureties, to protect the Medicare Trust Funds. DMEPOS Supplier Affiliation Reporting DMEPOS suppliers are currently required to report owners with a 5% or greater interest, general and limited partners, managing employees, and corporate officers and directors on the Form CMS-855S Medicare enrollment application. CMS is proposing to expand this disclosure requirement to include additional associated entities, such as companies that furnish marketing, fulfillment, wholesale, or device manufacturing services to the supplier. CMS believes this expanded disclosure would help expose interrelated fraudulent network operations. Redefining “Sufficient Evidence” for Surety Bond Liability Current regulations (42 C.F.R. § 424.57(a)) define “sufficient evidence” of surety bond liability, but CMS has found that sureties frequently claim the evidence provided is insufficient to avoid their payment obligations. CMS is proposing to revise this definition to include greater detail as to what constitutes “sufficient evidence.” CMS believes this would reduce sureties' ability to avoid their financial obligations. Expanding the Prohibition on Unsolicited Beneficiary Contact Current regulations (42 C.F.R. § 424.57(c)(11)) prohibit DMEPOS suppliers from contacting beneficiaries by telephone without prior permission, subject to limited exceptions, but this prohibition does not extend to email, text messages, social media, or door-to-door contact. CMS is proposing to expand this prohibition to also cover text messages and add new § 424.57(c)(31) to address social media direct messages, email without an opt-out feature, and door-to-door solicitation, subject to the same exceptions that currently apply to telephone contact. CMS believes this expanded protection would better protect beneficiaries from high-pressure marketing and fraudulent solicitation schemes. Medicaid and CHIP Program Integrity Reliance on Credible Allegation of Fraud (CAF) Determinations Under current regulations (42 C.F.R. § 455.23), a state Medicaid agency must suspend payments to a provider upon determining that a credible allegation of fraud (CAF) exists, unless good cause exists not to suspend. CMS cannot currently trigger a state's obligation to make this determination directly. CMS is proposing to allow states to rely on a CAF determination made by CMS or by another federal agency to establish their own CAF finding under § 455.23(a)(1)(i). CMS is also proposing to narrow the "good cause" exception at § 455.23(e). A state is not required to rely on another agency's CAF determination; where a state disagrees with a determination CMS has made, CMS encourages the state to engage with CMS' Center for Program Integrity to better understand the basis for CMS' determination. State Fraud and Abuse Reporting Requirements Current regulations (42 C.F.R. § 455.17), issued in 1978 and amended in 1983, require states to report suspected provider fraud and abuse complaints that warrant full investigation, using six specified data elements. CMS is proposing to add new required data elements, including a provider's National Provider Identifier (NPI), address and location, and all actions taken against the provider, under § 455.17(b)(6). CMS believes existing requirements no longer provide sufficient detail to support effective national oversight, trend analysis, or coordination between CMS and its state partners. Electronic Visit Verification (EVV) Enhancements The 21st Century Cures Act requires states to implement EVV systems for personal care services (PCS) and home health (HH) services, but current regulations do not require states to use EVV data as part of prepayment claims review or preliminary fraud, waste, and abuse investigations. CMS is proposing to modernize these requirements to require that states incorporate EVV information into Medicaid prepayment review activities and preliminary investigations, strengthening oversight in these historically high-risk service areas. National Provider Identifier Requirements for All Eligible Provider Types Under current regulations (42 C.F.R. Part 455, Subpart E), states are not required to consistently collect, maintain, or use NPIs across all eligible Medicaid and CHIP provider types, systems, and activities. CMS is proposing to require states to collect, maintain, and use NPIs for all eligible Medicaid and CHIP providers across enrollment, revalidation, claims processing, managed care oversight, program integrity, and reporting activities, consistent with 45 C.F.R. Part 162. CMS believes this would improve the ability of both states and CMS to prevent, identify, and address fraud, waste, and abuse at the individual provider level. Monthly State Reporting of Provider Denials and Terminations States are currently required to report "for cause" provider terminations within 30 calendar days, but the rationale for a termination is not always clearly documented, and current reporting excludes providers

### [Fiscal Year 2026 Improper Payments](https://www.cms.gov/newsroom/fact-sheets/fiscal-year-2026-improper-payments)

- Source: `cms_fact_sheets` · published `2026-11-16` · freshness `stale`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `ineligible`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.cms.gov/newsroom/fact-sheets/fiscal-year-2026-improper-payments`

Fiscal Year 2026 Improper Payments The Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services (CMS) is committed to being a responsible steward of public funds and promoting the sustainability of its programs for future generations. Measuring improper payments is an important part of that responsibility. An improper payment is a payment that does not meet CMS program requirements. Improper payments include overpayments, underpayments, and payments for which there is insufficient information to determine whether payment requirements were met. Improper payment rates do not measure fraud. While some improper payments may involve fraud or abuse, others reflect missing documentation or administrative errors. For example, a state or contractor may miss a required administrative step, where completing that step would have established the payment was proper. Estimated Fiscal Year (FY) 2026 Improper Payment Rates: Original Medicare: 7.32%, or $33.96 billion, compared to the FY 2025 reported rate of 6.55%, or $28.83 billion. The FY 2026 estimate marks the tenth consecutive year this figure has been below the 10% threshold for compliance established by improper payment statutory requirements. 1 Medicare Part C : 5.68%, or $23.86 billion, compared to the FY 2025 reported rate of 6.09%, or $23.67 billion. Most Part C improper payments were attributable to situations where the Medicare Advantage Organization’s supporting documentation failed to substantiate beneficiary diagnosis data submitted for payment. Medicare Part D : 3.85%, or $5.16 billion, compared to the FY 2025 reported rate of 4%, or $4.23 billion. Most Part D improper payments occurred because plan sponsors failed to provide sufficient documentation to support prescription data used for payment determinations. Medicaid: 7.08%, or $46.84 billion (composed of the past three cycles of approximately 17 states per cycle from reviews in 2024, 2025, and 2026), compared to the FY 2025 reported rate of 6.12%, or $37.39 billion. About 79.41% of the improper payments were the result of insufficient documentation. The Children’s Health Insurance Program (CHIP) : 9.81%, or $2.26 billion (composed of reviews in 2024, 2025, and 2026), compared to the FY 2025 reported rate of 7.05%, or $1.37 billion. About 55.93% of the improper payments were the result of insufficient documentation. The increase in national Medicaid and CHIP improper payment estimates reflects, in part, the effects of unwinding the flexibilities given to states during the COVID-19 public health emergency, such as conducting eligibility redeterminations and provider revalidation requirements, which resumed in April 2023. Advance Payments of the Premium Tax Credit (APTC) program for the Federally-Facilitated Exchange (FFE) for Benefit Year 2024 (Jan. 1 to Dec. 31, 2024): The FY 2026 improper payment rate, for BY 2024 is 0.84% or $830.37 million, compared to the FY 2025, for BY 2023, reported rate of 0.89%, or $657.46 million. The primary cause of improper payments was overpayments due to automated errors (59.24% of improper payments) where most relate to duplicate payment errors, which encapsulates duplicate member and duplicate use of Social Security Number (SSN) errors. The secondary cause of improper payments was overpayments found in manual errors related to failures to properly examine consumer submitted documents. While CMS’ improper payment reporting programs are not designed to measure fraud, the agency is committed to crushing fraud, waste, and abuse throughout its programs and fully expects these efforts to impact improper payment rates going forward. The 2026 Original Medicare and Medicaid/CHIP improper payment measurements are based on claims submitted from July 1, 2024, through June 30, 2025. This timeframe is before many of CMS’ current crushing fraud initiatives were implemented including: Medicare enrollment moratoria for high-risk provider types (including home health agencies and hospices); Large-scale provider enrollment revocations and payment suspensions targeting suspicious billing patterns; Greater use of advanced data analytics to identify high-risk Medicaid providers and coordinate rapid investigations with states and HHS' Office of Inspector General; Development of new data dashboards that give states a Super Intelligence-powered tool to track claims and catch Medicaid outliers before losses occur. To further strengthen stewardship of taxpayer dollars and secure the Medicare Trust Fund, CMS is also advancing a best-in-class commercial claims processing effort, ClaimsCore, that will re-platform Original Medicare claims processing onto a secure, configurable, and modern foundation. CMS estimates it will save millions through this work, not including the additional billions that may be saved through improved program integrity. ClaimsCore is designed to support modern payment integrity controls, faster and more transparent policy execution, and advanced capabilities such as Super Intelligence, while improving operational efficiency and care delivery over time. The impact of these and CMS’ other actions to combat fraud will begin to appear in the improper payment data for FY 2027, with more substantial effects expected in FY 2028. What You Need to Know: The Payment Integrity Information Act of 2019 (Public Law 116- 117) defines significant improper payments as either: Greater than $10 million and over 1.5% of all payments made under that program, or Greater than $100 million. The FY 2026 HHS Agency Financial Report provides the improper payment data for the Original Medicare, Medicare Part C, Medicare Part D, Medicaid, CHIP, and Affordable Care Act Health Insurance Exchange APTC programs. Most improper payments occurred in situations where a reviewer could not determine if a payment was proper because of insufficient documentation from a state, provider, a Medicare Advantage Organization, Part D Sponsor, or Marketplace insurer. Improper payments can result from a variety of circumstances, including: Items or services with no documentation; Items or services with insufficient documentation; Items or services with documentation that does not substantiate the payment; Items or services where the payment was to the right recipient for the right amount, but the payment process did not comply with applicable statutory or regulatory payment requirements; or With respect to Medicaid and CHIP, there is no record of the required verification of an individual’s eligibility factors, such as income. Proper payments occur when there is sufficient documentation to support payment in accordance with the program payment requirements. Two examples of proper payments include: Payments where CMS or the state appropriately maintained documentation of an eligibility verification requirement and appropriately determined eligibility based on program eligibility and payment requirements. Payments where sufficient documentation was provided to support program payment requirements. More information on CMS’ Improper Payments Measurement Programs can be found at: https://www.cms.gov/data-research/monitoring-programs/improper-payment-measurement-programs. To view the HHS Agency Financial Report, visit: www.hhs.gov/afr. _______________ 1 Originally published under the Improper Payments Elimination and Recovery Act of 2010 and adopted by the Payment Integrity Information Act of 2019. ### CMS News and Media Group Catherine Howden, Director Media Inquiries Form 202-690-6145

### [Red Teaming a Patient-Facing Clinical AI Platform: 2-Phase Prospective Study](https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e101912/)

- Source: `jmir_ai` · published `2026-11-16` · freshness `stale`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `ineligible`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e101912/`

&lt;strong&gt;Background:&lt;/strong&gt; Health systems are increasingly deploying patient-facing conversational AI platforms, yet validated methodologies for safety evaluation under realistic high-risk conditions are lacking. Prior work has documented safety failures in clinical AI but not shown whether iterative, health system–embedded testing can produce measurable safety improvement. &lt;strong&gt;Objective:&lt;/strong&gt; This study aimed to evaluate a structured red teaming methodology for the safety assessment of a patient-facing clinical AI platform and determine whether targeted remediation guided by initial testing reduced safety failures in subsequent rounds of high-risk evaluations. &lt;strong&gt;Methods:&lt;/strong&gt; A 2-phase prospective simulation study was conducted at a single academic health system in January and February 2026. Phase 1 enrolled 48 health care professionals who completed 6 randomized clinical scenarios (3 medium risk and 3 high risk). Following independent platform remediation informed by aggregated phase 1 findings, phase 2 enrolled 27 additional participants who completed 8 targeted high-risk scenarios. All 476 transcripts were independently scored by 3 calibrated raters using a 5-domain safety rubric. A strict firewall separated the research team from the platform developer, and only aggregated, deidentified findings were shared between phases. &lt;strong&gt;Results:&lt;/strong&gt; In phase 1, the high-risk failure rate was 30.2% (35/116) of transcripts vs 5.6% (7/126) for medium-risk scenarios. In phase 2, the high-risk failure rate was 8.5% (20/234), representing an absolute difference of 21.7 percentage points (participant-clustered 95% CI 11.1-32.2) and a 72% relative difference, with comparable performance across prespecified and improvised prompts within the targeted failure categories. Interrater reliability was moderate to strong in phase 1 (intraclass correlation coefficient=0.66, 95% CI 0.62-0.69) and moderate in phase 2 (intraclass correlation coefficient=0.49, 95% CI 0.43-0.55). Agreement on the binary failure classification remained stable between phases (Fleiss κ=0.70 in both phases), with observed agreement increasing from 90.9% (220/242) to 94.9% (222/234). Residual failures clustered around triage calibration errors and scope violations. &lt;strong&gt;Conclusions:&lt;/strong&gt; Structured iterative red teaming was associated with improved safety performance on new high-risk scenarios following targeted remediation. The 5-domain rubric and scenario taxonomy offer a replicable methodology for health systems conducting predeployment and ongoing clinical AI safety assessment. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/bcffc7bc582f01b4bafdb33a970c3a19" />

### [2027 Medicare Parts A & B Premiums and Deductibles](https://www.cms.gov/newsroom/fact-sheets/2027-medicare-parts-b-premiums-deductibles)
