2027 Medicare Parts A & B Premiums and Deductibles On November 17, 2026, the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services (CMS) released the 2027 Medicare Part A and Part B programs premiums, deductibles, and coinsurance, and the 2027 Medicare Part D income-related monthly adjustment amounts. Medicare Part A Premium and Deductible Medicare Part A covers inpatient hospitals, skilled nursing facilities, hospice, inpatient rehabilitation, and some home healthcare services. Approximately 99% of Medicare beneficiaries do not have a Part A premium since they have at least 40 quarters of Medicare-covered employment, as determined by the Social Security Administration. The Medicare Part A inpatient hospital deductible that beneficiaries pay if admitted to the hospital will be $1,776 in 2027, an increase of $40 from $1,736 in 2026. Part A inpatient hospital deductible covers beneficiaries’ share of costs for the first 60 days of Medicare-covered inpatient hospital care in a benefit period. In 2027, beneficiaries must pay a coinsurance amount of $444 per day for the 61 st through 90 th day of a hospitalization ($434 in 2026) in a benefit period and $888 per day for lifetime reserve days ($868 in 2025). For beneficiaries in skilled nursing facilities, the daily coinsurance for days 21 through 100 of extended care services in a benefit period will be $222 in 2027 ($217 in 2026). Part A Deductible and Coinsurance Amounts for Calendar Years 2026 and 2027 by Type of Cost Sharing 2026 2027 Inpatient hospital deductible $1,736 $1,776 Daily hospital coinsurance for 61 st -90 th day $434 $444 Daily hospital coinsurance for lifetime reserve days $868 $888 Skilled nursing facility daily coinsurance (days 21-100) $217 $222 Enrollees age 65 and older who have fewer than 40 quarters of coverage, and certain persons with disabilities, pay a monthly premium to voluntarily enroll in Medicare Part A. Individuals who had at least 30 quarters of coverage, or were married to someone with at least 30 quarters of coverage, may buy into Part A at a reduced monthly premium rate, which will be $328 in 2027, a $17 increase from $311 in 2026. Certain uninsured aged individuals who have fewer than 30 quarters of coverage, and certain individuals with disabilities who have exhausted other entitlements, will pay the full premium, which will be $597 a month in 2027, a $32 increase from $565 in 2026. Medicare Part B Premium and Deductible Medicare Part B covers physicians’ services, outpatient hospital services, certain home health services, durable medical equipment, and certain other medical and health services not covered by Medicare Part A. Each year, the Medicare Part B premium, deductible, and coinsurance rates are determined according to provisions of the Social Security Act. The standard monthly premium for Medicare Part B enrollees will be $209.40 for 2027, an increase of $6.50 from $202.90 in 2026. The annual deductible for all Medicare Part B beneficiaries will be $292 in 2027, an increase of $9 from the annual deductible of $283 in 2026. The increase in the 2027 Part B standard premium and deductible is mainly due to projected increases in healthcare spending. Beginning in 2023, individuals whose full Medicare coverage ended 36 months after a kidney transplant, and who do not have certain other types of insurance coverage, can elect to continue Part B coverage of immunosuppressive drugs by paying a premium. For 2027, the standard immunosuppressive drug premium is $125.70. Medicare Part B Income-Related Monthly Adjustment Amounts Since 2007, a beneficiary’s Part B monthly premium has been based on his or her income. These income-related monthly adjustment amounts affect roughly 9% of people with Medicare Part B. The 2027 Part B total premiums for high-income beneficiaries with full Part B coverage are shown in the following table: Full Part B Coverage Beneficiaries who file individual tax returns with Medicare Part D Income-Related Monthly Adjustment Amounts Since 2011, a beneficiary’s Part D monthly premium has been based on his or her income. Approximately 9% of people with Medicare Part D pay these income-related monthly adjustment amounts. These individuals will pay the income-related monthly adjustment amount in addition to their Part D premium. Part D premiums vary by plan and, regardless of how a beneficiary pays their Part D premium, the Part D income-related monthly adjustment amounts are deducted from Social Security benefit checks or paid directly to Medicare. Roughly two-thirds of beneficiaries pay premiums directly to the plan while the remainder have their premiums deducted from their Social Security benefit checks. ### [Acupoint Thread Embedding for Weight Loss in Women With Overweight or Obesity : A Randomized Controlled Trial.](https://doi.org/10.7326/annals-26-02294)

- Source: `annals_internal_abstracts` · published `2026-11-17` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `51` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:43038532`

<h4>Background</h4>Whether acupoint thread embedding (ATE), a traditional Chinese medicine intervention, produces weight reduction remains uncertain.<h4>Objective</h4>To evaluate the efficacy and safety of ATE for weight reduction in women with overweight or obesity.<h4>Design</h4>Randomized, sham-controlled trial masked to participants, outcome assessors, and analysts. (ClinicalTrials.gov: NCT06316310).<h4>Setting</h4>Acupuncture Department, Shanghai Municipal Hospital of Traditional Chinese Medicine, Shanghai, China.<h4>Participants</h4>132 women aged 18 to 45 years with a body mass index (BMI) of 24.0 kg/m<sup>2</sup> or greater.<h4>Intervention</h4>Participants were randomly assigned 1:1 to ATE (6 sessions over 12 weeks) or sham ATE (superficial needle insertion at the same acupoints without manipulation, de qi, thread placement, or needle retention), followed by a 12-week follow-up.<h4>Measurements</h4>The primary outcome was change in BMI from baseline to week 12, analyzed under the intention-to-treat principle using linear mixed-effects models.<h4>Results</h4>At week 12, BMI and body weight decreased 0.7 kg/m<sup>2</sup> (95% CI, -1.0 to -0.3 kg/m<sup>2</sup>) and 1.8 kg (CI, -2.7 to -0.9 kg), respectively, more with ATE than with sham ATE; at week 24, differences were -1.1 kg/m<sup>2</sup> (CI, -1.7 to -0.6 kg/m<sup>2</sup>) and -3.0 kg (CI, -4.5 to -1.6 kg), respectively. Weight reduction of 5% or more occurred in 25.8% versus 9.1% (risk difference, 16.7 percentage points [CI, 3.7 to 29.3 percentage points]) at 12 weeks. Acupoint thread embedding produced greater reductions in waist and hip circumference, body fat percentage, and appetite scores, whereas cardiometabolic and patient-reported outcomes did not differ. Adverse events were mild and comparably distributed.<h4>Limitation</h4>Single center, generally healthy women of reproductive age, unmasked acupuncturists, short-term follow-up, and underpowered for uncommon adverse events and subgroup analyses.<h4>Conclusion</h4>Acupoint thread embedding produced modest but sustained reductions in BMI and adiposity-related measures in Chinese women of reproductive age with overweight or obesity.<h4>Primary funding source</h4>National Facility for Translational Medicine (Shanghai).

### [Associations Between Technology Attitudes, App Use, and Health Outcomes in a Diabetes Self-Management Education and Support Intervention: Secondary Analysis of a Randomized Controlled Trial](https://mhealth.jmir.org/2026/1/e105830)

- Source: `jmir_mhealth` · published `2026-11-17` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `45` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://mhealth.jmir.org/2026/1/e105830`

<strong>Background:</strong> Mobile health (mHealth) apps are increasingly incorporated into diabetes self-management education and support (DSMES) programs to improve access to behavioral and clinical support. Although digital DSMES interventions have demonstrated benefits for self-management and glycemic control, considerable variability exists in app use, and less is known about whether preintervention technology attitudes are associated with subsequent app use and related outcomes. <strong>Objective:</strong> This study examined whether baseline technology attitudes are associated with app use and whether app use is associated with diabetes self-care and hemoglobin A<sub>1c</sub> (HbA<sub>1c</sub>) among adults participating in a digital DSMES intervention. <strong>Methods:</strong> We conducted a secondary analysis of the Living Healthier with Diabetes randomized controlled trial (RCT) in Texas (2020-2022). The RCT included 3 study arms: virtual DSMES, smartphone app–based DSMES, and a combined intervention. Analyses were restricted to the 2 intervention arms with app access. Baseline technology attitude was assessed using a 4-item composite scale. App use was measured using 2 indices: a primary app use index derived from days per week, minutes per day, and overall 3-month usage, and a feature-use index summing use across 10 app functions. Outcomes included HbA<sub>1c</sub> and diabetes self-care. Multivariable linear regression and linear mixed-effects models were used to evaluate associations after adjustment for intervention arm, age, gender, past month financial situation, and baseline outcome values. <strong>Results:</strong> Of the 189 participants enrolled in the primary trial, 129 randomized to the intervention arms with smartphone app access were included. Follow-up HbA<sub>1c</sub> and diabetes self-care data were available for 116 (89.9%) and 114 (88.4%) participants at 3 months and 117 (90.7%) and 116 (89.9%) participants at 6 months, respectively. More positive baseline technology attitudes were associated with greater app use at both 3 months (β=0.64, 95% CI 0.29 to 0.99; <i>P</i>&lt;.001) and 6 months (β=0.78, 95% CI 0.39 to 1.17; <i>P</i>&lt;.001) but were not associated with feature use. App use was not associated with HbA<sub>1c</sub> at 3 months but was associated with lower HbA<sub>1c</sub> at 6 months (β=−0.04, 95% CI −0.074 to −0.013; <i>P</i>=.005) and in longitudinal models (β=−0.04, 95% CI −0.074 to −0.006; <i>P</i>=.02). Greater app use was associated with higher self-care at 3 months (β=0.56, 95% CI 0.29 to 0.82; <i>P</i>&lt;.001) and 6 months (β=0.38, 95% CI 0.14 to 0.62; <i>P</i>=.002) and remained associated with self-care in longitudinal analyses (β=0.44, 95% CI 0.21 to 0.68; <i>P</i>&lt;.001). App and feature use did not change significantly between 3 and 6 months (<i>P</i>=.14 and <i>P</i>=.11, respectively). <strong>Conclusions:</strong> These findings suggest that preintervention technology attitudes are associated with greater app use within digital DSMES programs and that greater use of the app may represent an important pathway linking mHealth interventions with behavioral and clinical outcomes in adults with type 2 diabetes. <strong>Trial Registration:</strong> ClinicalTrials.gov NCT06370494; http://clinicaltrials.gov/ct2/show/NCT06370494

### [Blood Pressure Estimation for Hemodynamic Monitoring Using Reduced Photoplethysmography Features in Intensive Care Units: Prospective Machine Learning Study](https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e105057/)

- Source: `jmir_ai` · published `2026-11-17` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `55` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e105057/`

Background: Continuous, noninvasive blood pressure (BP) monitoring is clinically important for managing hemodynamic instability in the intensive care unit (ICU). However, current ICU practice primarily relies on invasive arterial catheterization. While this is the clinical gold standard for continuous measurement, it carries risks such as infection and thrombosis and is typically limited to select critical care settings. Objective: To reduce reliance on invasive arterial lines, we developed an interpretable estimation framework for systolic blood pressure (SBP), mean arterial pressure (MAP), and diastolic blood pressure (DBP) from single-sensor photoplethysmography (PPG). Methods: Synchronized PPG and arterial line 10-minute recordings from 100 patients in the ICU were analyzed. Using a hybrid feature selection strategy (RReliefF and intrinsic model–specific rankings), we reduced 201 initial features to an optimized subset of 30 core features. Model development and evaluation were designed to minimize information leakage and overfitting, incorporating nested cross-validation and patient-wise testing to assess generalizability across heterogeneous ICU populations. We developed and validated predictive models using machine learning techniques, specifically XGBoost (XGB), random forest, and support vector regression, with XGB serving as our primary model. Results: Among the evaluated models, the Hybrid Top 30 XGB configuration provided the best balance between estimation performance, model compactness, and computational efficiency. The model achieved mean absolute errors of 8.61 mm Hg (SBP), 3.50 mm Hg (MAP), and 2.62 mm Hg (DBP). For MAP and DBP, mean difference (SD) values of −0.09 (4.54) mm Hg and −1.07 (3.51) mm Hg, respectively, satisfied the Association for the Advancement of Medical Instrumentation numerical criteria, while performance corresponded to Grade A according to the British Hypertension Society grading criteria. Although SBP exhibited greater variability, estimation performance was largely preserved using a physiologically informed subset of 30 features (approximately 15% of the original feature set). Feature importance analysis revealed distinct physiological feature patterns across SBP, MAP, and DBP estimation. Conclusions: Interpretability analysis identified key predictors associated with wave reflection, systolic timing, low-frequency vasomotor activity, and autonomic regulation. By explicitly linking predictive features to underlying cardiovascular physiology, the proposed framework addresses a key limitation of existing black-box cuffless BP models. Ultimately, this study demonstrates that an optimized XGB framework using physiologically informed PPG features can provide accurate and computationally efficient BP estimation in ICU settings and may support the assessment of clinically relevant hemodynamic states using a single-sensor PPG platform. These findings provide a foundation for future development of more robust and generalizable interpretable BP estimation models and cuffless monitoring technologies. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/b48fb079002e9bfd8276c34cf4fa049d" />

### [Dietary and Exercise Relapse Management in Mobile Health Apps for Weight and Glycemic Control: Descriptive Qualitative Study](https://mhealth.jmir.org/2026/1/e94792)

- Source: `jmir_mhealth` · published `2026-11-17` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `44` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://mhealth.jmir.org/2026/1/e94792`

Background: Mobile health (mHealth) apps for weight and glycemic control support self-monitoring and behavior change, but engagement often declines over time. Although relapse in diet and exercise is common, little is known about how users experience relapse within digital contexts or how apps should respond. Objective: This study aimed to explore how users of mHealth apps for weight and glycemic control experience, manage, and wish to be supported during dietary and exercise relapses. Methods: A descriptive qualitative design was adopted. Semistructured online interviews were conducted with 26 adults who had used weight or glycemic control apps for at least 3 months and reported at least 1 relapse. Data were analyzed using inductive thematic analysis. Results: Seven themes emerged: stress and reduced capacity as conditions surrounding relapse; cultural, temporal, and environmental misalignment; psychological disengagement and avoidance; social and relational influences; relapse as emotional disengagement rather than simply a behavioral lapse; informal relapse management and self-directed recalibration; and desire for compassionate, context-aware support. Participants described relapse in relation to stress, grief, caregiving and occupational demands, religious observances, festivals, shift work, travel, and constrained food environments. Relapse was frequently experienced as emotional disengagement, characterized by guilt, shame, identity threat, and loss of meaning, and was often accompanied by avoidance of the app or monitoring devices. Participants managed relapse through self-directed strategies such as temporal resets, goal adjustment, partial engagement, and social support, but reported few structured relapse-recovery tools within apps. They expressed a strong preference for flexible goals, context-aware modes, mood-sensitive feedback, neutral language, and gentle reentry pathways. Conclusions: Participants’ accounts suggest that relapse in mHealth contexts involves emotional responses, contextual constraints, and changing self-management capacity. Flexible goals, neutral feedback, context-sensitive support, and graded reentry are promising design targets for relapse-responsive apps, but their effects on engagement and clinical outcomes require prospective evaluation.

### [Effect of Teledermatologist Supervision in AI Skin Cancer Apps on Patients’ Trust and Adherence Intentions: Randomized Controlled Trial](https://mhealth.jmir.org/2026/1/e93991)

- Source: `jmir_mhealth` · published `2026-11-17` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `62` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://mhealth.jmir.org/2026/1/e93991`

Background: Skin cancer incidence is rising globally, increasing pressure on dermatology services and making efficient triage increasingly important. AI-driven skin cancer mobile health apps may help address this need, but their usefulness depends on whether users trust the advice and intend to follow it. Teledermatologist supervision has been proposed to combine AI efficiency with human clinical oversight, but dermatologist shortages make it critical to understand whether such supervision improves patient trust and adherence at the population level, or whether AI-only advice suffices for most users. Objective: This study tests whether teledermatologist supervision in an AI-driven skin cancer app affects patient trust and adherence intentions, compared with AI-only advice, and whether these effects differ by motivational context and risk assessment level. Methods: The randomized controlled, online vignette experiment with repeated measures was conducted from May to June 2025 as a single-center study among Dutch adults via an academic hospital-based patient panel. Eligible participants were adult members of the Erasmus MC Digital Patient Panel invited by email; those not completing all 4 vignette trials were excluded from the analysis. Participants completed 4 simulated mobile app scenarios varying in advice source (AI-only vs hybrid teledermatologist and AI) and risk assessment level (high vs low). The primary outcomes were trust in the advice (0‐100 scale) and adherence intention (recoded 3-level ordinal scale). Mixed-effects models were used to analyze the data. Results: Of the 2707 patient panel members, 879 participated (response rate: 32.5%). Participants were aged 18 to 93 (mean 62.5, SD 12.64) years; 50.4% (n=443) were female, and 21.4% (n=188) had a history of skin cancer. Participants were randomly allocated to motivation conditions (concern: n=438 vs curiosity: n=441). Trust was higher for hybrid advice than AI-only advice (mean 76.9, SE 0.73 vs mean 65.7, SE 0.73; estimated difference 11.98; partial ²=0.22; <.001). The odds of adherence were 1.7 times greater with hybrid vs AI-only (odds ratio 1/0.59=1.7, 95% CI 1.28-2.22; <.001). High-risk assessments increased adherence (odds ratio 2.9, 95% CI 2.2-3.8; <.001), with a moderated effect by motivation (=.03), showing stronger adherence with concern vs curiosity. The effect of advice source on adherence was fully mediated by trust. Conclusions: Teledermatologist supervision of AI-based advice robustly increased patient trust and adherence intentions, particularly for high-risk assessments and concerned users. Our experiment is innovative in testing teledermatologist supervision in a population-level AI skin cancer app and in examining how motivational context shapes responses to advice. Unlike studies focused on postreferral teledermatology or chatbot-based advice, this study isolates the effect of hybrid AI-human advice in a store-and-forward screening context. These findings suggest that developers and health care implementers may consider integrating teledermatologist supervision to maximize patient trust of and adherence to advice, potentially improving triaging efficiency and safety. Trial Registration: Open Science Framework 64pjn; https://osf.io/64pjn

### [Natural ligand-based CAR T-cell therapy targeting BCMA and TACI for relapsed or refractory multiple myeloma (TriPRIL): a non-randomised, phase 1, first-in-human trial.](https://doi.org/10.1016/s0140-6736(26)01806-4)

- Source: `lancet_abstracts` · published `2026-11-17` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `63` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:43044617`

<h4>Background</h4>Functional loss of B-cell maturation antigen (BCMA) is a recognised mechanism of relapse after BCMA-directed chimeric antigen receptor (CAR) T-cell therapy in patients with relapsed or refractory multiple myeloma. Targeting multiple tumour antigens simultaneously with a single CAR has the potential to mitigate antigen escape and prolong disease responses. Transmembrane activator and CAML interactor (TACI) and BCMA are both highly expressed on most myeloma cells and bind to the same natural ligand-a proliferation-inducing ligand (APRIL). We designed TriPRIL, a CAR that uses an engineered trimeric form of APRIL, to target both BCMA and TACI. We aimed to establish the safety, tolerability, preliminary efficacy, and recommended phase 2 dose of autologous TriPRIL CAR T cells.<h4>Methods</h4>In this first-in-human, open-label, dose-escalation and dose-expansion, phase 1 study, we evaluated TriPRIL in patients with relapsed or refractory multiple myeloma at Massachusetts General Hospital (Boston, MA, USA). Eligible patients had received at least three previous lines of therapy or had triple-class refractory disease. Previous CAR T-cell therapy and bispecific antibody therapy exposure were permitted. Patients received 3 days of lymphodepletion with intravenous cyclophosphamide (300 mg/m<sup>2</sup>) and intravenous fludarabine (30 mg/m<sup>2</sup>) daily on days -5, -4, and -3 before intravenous TriPRIL infusion on day 0. In the dose escalation phase, patients received 1·0 × 10<sup>8</sup> or 3·0 × 10<sup>8</sup> TriPRIL CAR T cells to establish the recommended phase 2 dose, which was further evaluated in the dose expansion phase. The primary endpoint was the incidence of study-related adverse events (graded according to Common Terminology Criteria for Adverse Events version 5) and dose-limiting toxicities in all patients who received an infusion of TriPRIL CAR T cells, until study completion at 24 months or progressive disease. The data cutoff date was March 24, 2026, when all infused patients had completed at least one assessment of disease response. This trial was registered at ClinicalTrials.gov (NCT05020444), has fully accrued, and is ongoing.<h4>Findings</h4>Between Sept 17, 2021, and Jan 27, 2026, 16 patients were enrolled and underwent leukapheresis, with 15 patients subsequently receiving a TriPRIL cell infusion. Ten (67%) of 15 infused patients were female, five (33%)were male, and the median age was 69 years (IQR 65-75). Nine (60%) of 15 infused patients had previously received BCMA-directed CAR T-cell therapy, and four (27%) had previously received a BCMA-directed or GPRC5D-directed bispecific T-cell engager. Six patients were treated with 1·0 × 10<sup>8</sup> CAR T cells, and nine were treated with 3·0 × 10<sup>8</sup> CAR T cells; there were no dose-limiting toxicities at either dose. The most common adverse events were neutropenia (14 [93%] of 15 patients), nausea (12 [80%]), hypogammaglobulinaemia (eight [53%]), fatigue (seven [47%]), and fever (seven [47%]). Non-haematological adverse events grade 3 or worse occurred in five (33%) patients and all resolved. Cytokine release syndrome (grade 1 or 2 only) occurred in 13 (87%, 95% CI 60-98) infused patients. One (7%, 0-32) patient had grade 2 immune effector cell-associated neurotoxicity syndrome, and one (7%, 0-32) had grade 2 immune effector cell-associated haemophagocytic lymphohistiocytosis-like syndrome; both of these patients had received 3·0 × 10<sup>8</sup> CAR T cells, and both conditions resolved with corticosteroids. Infections occurred in six (40%, 16-68) infused patients, with a grade 3 infection in one patient (7%, 0-32). There were no treatment-related deaths. The recommended phase 2 dose of CAR T cells was set as 3·0 × 10<sup>8</sup> cells.<h4>Interpretation</h4>TriPRIL showed a favourable safety profile and warrants further investigation in patients with relapsed or refractory multiple myeloma, including those previously treated with BCMA-directed CAR T-cell therapy. The study findings support further development of this dual-targeted, natural ligand-based CAR T-cell approach.<h4>Funding</h4>Mass General Brigham and V Foundation.

### [Toward a Healthier Future Through Obesity Prevention and Care: The JAMA Summit Report on Obesity.](https://doi.org/10.1001/jama.2026.19759)

- Source: `jama_abstracts` · published `2026-11-17` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `49` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:43042892`

<h4>Importance</h4>Obesity is one of the most difficult challenges of the modern era, affecting more than 1 billion people worldwide. Nearly half of US adults and 1 in 5 US children meet the medical definition of having obesity, with major consequences for cardiometabolic, kidney, hepatic, pulmonary, musculoskeletal, reproductive, oncologic, and mental health. Obesity also carries social and economic harms through stigma, discrimination, and bias.<h4>Observations</h4>Drivers of the obesity epidemic include changes in food production and consumption accompanied by social, economic, and technological shifts that reduce food preparation, disrupt sleep, and decrease physical activity. The burden of these changes is not equally distributed, with higher obesity prevalence among socioeconomically disadvantaged populations. Current prevention measures include nutrition standards, food and menu labeling, school meal policies, sugar-sweetened beverage taxes, community-based lifestyle programs, and public assistance efforts. The impact of these approaches has been inadequate. Contemporary clinical management recognizes obesity as a chronic disease characterized by genetic, biological, and behavioral susceptibilities. Access, affordability, tolerability, and the chronicity of use remain major challenges for all available obesity management strategies, including new glucagon-like peptide-1 receptor agonist therapies. Potential solutions require a coordinated, multisector response and include efforts to prevent obesity at the societal level, improve care of individuals with obesity, and strengthen research and innovation. Priority areas include improving the food and physical activity environment; strengthening communication and education; strengthening childhood obesity prevention; addressing health inequities; reducing stigma and bias; modernizing obesity care delivery; expanding access to lifestyle, healthy food, medication, and surgical interventions; and supporting long-term treatment adherence and maintenance. Proposed actions include new government policies on food production, labeling, marketing, and school nutrition; modernization of drug evaluation; payment reform to support comprehensive obesity treatment; development of multidisciplinary care models; incentives to promote cross-sector partnerships; and research to advance personalized prevention and treatment strategies.<h4>Conclusions and relevance</h4>The emergence of effective obesity management medications offers a significant opportunity to curb the prevalence of obesity. However, no single intervention will be enough. Sustained improvement will require addressing the underlying structural and environmental drivers of obesity, along with person-centered, multilevel strategies that simultaneously improve prevention, reduce stigma, and expand equitable access to evidence-based treatment. Coordinated action across government, health care systems, clinicians, payers, industry, schools, communities, and researchers will be essential to achieving long-term improvement.

### [Designer mucin-responsive nanomedicine for enhanced solid tumour penetration and therapy](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41551-026-01843-z)

- Source: `nature_biomedical_engineering` · published `2026-11-18` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `66` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41551-026-01843-z`

While nanomedicines hold great promise for solid tumour therapy, their penetration and access to tumour cells is limited by large glycosylated proteins called mucins that are enriched in the tumour microenvironment. Here we show that mucins induce the disassembly of self-assembled mPEG–polyester micellar nanoparticles into smaller unimers. Leveraging this finding, we designed a mucin-responsive, paclitaxel-conjugated nanomedicine (mrPNM) whose unimers are capable of escaping the mucin-binding barrier. Following intravenous injection, this designer mucin-responsive nanomedicine exhibited markedly deeper penetration than the mucin-nonresponsive nanomedicine and albumin, resulting in 3.9- and 1.5-fold increases in tumour cell delivery, respectively, in a pancreatic mouse tumour model. The mrPNMs also achieved superior tumour growth inhibition compared to both the mucin-nonresponsive nanomedicine and Abraxane, the FDA-approved nanoparticle albumin-bound paclitaxel, in three solid tumour models. The mucin-responsive nanomedicine platform could provide a general strategy for improving the treatment of solid tumours. Tumour-associated mucins induce the disassembly of a designer PEGylated paclitaxel-conjugated nanomedicine into small unimers that escape the mucin-binding barrier, enhancing tumour penetration and therapeutic efficacy in mouse models of solid tumours.

### [Effect of intrapartum hyoscine butylbromide and sodium bicarbonate on spontaneous vaginal birth in nulliparous women after induction of labour (SAINT): multicentre randomised placebo controlled factorial trial.](https://doi.org/10.1136/bmj-2026-101069)

- Source: `bmj_abstracts` · published `2026-11-18` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `69` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:43051685`

<h4>Objective</h4>To determine whether intrapartum hyoscine butylbromide, sodium bicarbonate, and their combination increase the rate of spontaneous vaginal birth in nulliparous women undergoing induction of labour.<h4>Design</h4>Multicentre, randomised, double blind, placebo controlled, 2×2 factorial phase 3 trial (SAINT).<h4>Setting</h4>11 Norwegian hospitals providing comprehensive obstetric care.<h4>Participants</h4>Nulliparous women aged 18-50 years with a singleton live fetus in cephalic presentation at term with an indication for induction of labour.<h4>Interventions</h4>Participants were randomly assigned (1:1:1:1) to oral sodium bicarbonate (4×1 g per dose) plus intravenous hyoscine butylbromide (20 mg per dose), oral sodium bicarbonate plus intravenous placebo, oral placebo plus intravenous hyoscine butylbromide, or double placebo. The investigational medicinal product was administered at the onset of active labour and repeated every four hours to a maximum of three doses.<h4>Main outcome measure</h4>Spontaneous vaginal birth (no instrumental or caesarean delivery).<h4>Results</h4>Between 4 January 2023 and 29 January 2025, 3094 nulliparous women were randomised, and 2963 comprised the full analysis set. Spontaneous vaginal birth occurred in 57% (422/741) in the bicarbonate plus hyoscine butylbromide arm, 61% (445/734) in the bicarbonate arm, 61% (449/741) in the hyoscine butylbromide arm, and 56% (422/747) in the double placebo arm. Marginal risk differences for spontaneous vaginal birth compared with double placebo were -0.2 (95% confidence interval -5.1 to 4.7; P=0.93) percentage points for combined treatment, 3.1 (-1.7 to 8.0; P=0.21) percentage points for bicarbonate, and 4.0 (-0.8 to 8.9; P=0.10) percentage points for hyoscine butylbromide. Serious adverse events were similar between groups, and no treatment related serious morbidity or deaths were observed.<h4>Conclusions</h4>No clear evidence was found that intrapartum hyoscine butylbromide, sodium bicarbonate, or their combination increased spontaneous vaginal birth compared with placebo. Although small beneficial effects cannot be excluded, the findings do not support routine intrapartum use of either agent to facilitate spontaneous vaginal birth after induction of labour.<h4>Trial registration</h4>European Clinical Trials Database (EudraCT; 2021-000392-37); EU Clinical Trials Information System (CTIS; EU CT number 2024-511848-55-00); ClinicalTrials.gov (NCT05719467).

### [New Insights and Effectiveness of Digital Interventions for Health Anxiety: Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis](https://mhealth.jmir.org/2026/1/e91108)

- Source: `jmir_mhealth` · published `2026-11-18` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `53` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://mhealth.jmir.org/2026/1/e91108`

Background: Health anxiety (HA), classified in the ) under illness anxiety disorder (IAD) or somatic symptom disorder, is an increasingly prevalent condition associated with substantial psychological and health care burdens. Digital mental health interventions (DMHIs) offer scalable and accessible approaches to care, with digital behavioral interventions (DBIs), particularly internet-delivered cognitive and behavioral therapies, among the most frequently studied. However, their effectiveness in reducing HA has not been comprehensively synthesized. Objective: This study aimed to systematically evaluate the effectiveness of DBIs for reducing HA in adults and to examine their effects on related psychological outcomes. Methods: This systematic review and meta-analysis followed the PRISMA 2020 statement and Cochrane guidance. PubMed, Embase, Web of Science, ScienceDirect, PsycINFO, and the Cochrane Library were searched from database inception to November 1, 2025. Eligible studies included randomized controlled trials (RCTs), quasi-experimental studies, and cohort studies involving adults with HA. HA severity was the primary outcome. Random-effects meta-analyses were restricted to RCTs. Methodological quality was appraised using Joanna Briggs Institute tools, risk of bias in RCTs using Risk of Bias 2 (RoB 2), and certainty of evidence using Grading of Recommendations Assessment, Development, and Evaluation (GRADE). Results: Sixteen studies involving 2581 participants were included, comprising 9 RCTs, 6 quasi-experimental studies, and 1 cohort study. Six RCTs involving 585 participants contributed to the primary meta-analysis. Digital interventions significantly reduced HA compared with control conditions (Hedges =−0.77, 95% CI −1.25 to –0.30; =.002), although between-study heterogeneity was substantial (²=86.7%). Leave-one-out analyses showed that this effect remained significant after exclusion of any individual trial. By contrast, pooled effects on illness-related attitudes, general anxiety, anxiety sensitivity, and depressive symptoms were not statistically significant and were characterized by substantial heterogeneity. Among the 9 RCTs, 4 were judged as having low overall risk of bias, 2 had some concerns, and 3 were at high risk. GRADE certainty was low for HA and very low for the secondary outcomes. Conclusions: Digital interventions show potential for reducing core health-anxiety symptoms, but confidence in the magnitude and generalizability of the effect is limited by substantial heterogeneity, risk-of-bias concerns, and low certainty of evidence. Evidence for broader psychological outcomes remains inconclusive. Adequately powered, well-reported RCTs using rigorous outcome assessment, appropriate active comparators, and longer follow-up are needed to confirm effectiveness and clarify which patients benefit most and under what delivery conditions. Trial Registration: PROSPERO CRD420251238746; https://www.crd.york.ac.uk/prospero/

### [Passive Monitoring for Detecting Hypomanic Symptoms in Bipolar Disorder Using Explainable Machine Learning: Longitudinal Observational Study](https://mhealth.jmir.org/2026/1/e105647)

- Source: `jmir_mhealth` · published `2026-11-18` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `62` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://mhealth.jmir.org/2026/1/e105647`

Background: When trying to intervene early to prevent manic episodes, clinicians are hindered by patients underreporting their symptoms as they are becoming hypomanic or manic (ie, emerging symptoms). Continuous passive monitoring using wearable technology offers a potential solution to address this problem by capturing behavioral signals that do not depend on self-awareness. However, its clinical usefulness requires validation before implementation. Objective: This study aimed to evaluate whether passively collected wearable data can detect hypomanic symptoms using an explainable machine learning framework. Methods: We applied explainable machine learning to densely sampled multimodal data from a 2-year observational study of digital monitoring in 178 patients with a diagnosis of bipolar disorder (mean follow-up 540 days; range 120-706 days). Data on activity and sleep were collected continuously using wearable technology. Self-reported data were collected daily on their mood, energy, and anxiety, and weekly using the Altman Self-Rating Mania Scale (ASRM). To identify robust digital markers of hypomanic symptoms, we analyzed 252 time- and frequency-domain features from 43 participants who experienced at least 1 ASRM-defined symptomatic interval. Results: Passively collected data achieved high accuracy, comparable with daily self-reported data in detecting symptomatic intervals (area under the receiver operating characteristic curve 0.91 vs 0.93, respectively). Combined daily passive wearable data features achieved higher sensitivity (92.0% vs 85.4%), balanced accuracy (0.90 vs 0.89), and stronger rule-out usefulness (negative predictive value 97.8% vs 96.1%) than self-report data, with daily sleep features alone driving most of this signal (area under the receiver operating characteristic curve 0.91, sensitivity 91.8%). The high classification performance using wearable data was explained by reductions in spectral entropy and flux across domains, reflecting a decreased complexity and variability in mood, activity, and sleep patterns, consistent with a shift toward pathologically stable states. Conclusions: Passive monitoring using wearable technology can detect hypomanic symptoms with high accuracy, supporting its potential as a clinically meaningful tool for continuous, objective monitoring in patients who underreport hypomanic symptoms. These findings are associative and concurrent in nature; they do not establish predictive validity. Prospective studies are needed to evaluate predictive validity for early detection of ASRM-defined symptomatic intervals.

### [Physical activity coaching and text messaging for adults with mobility limitations (ComeBACK): hybrid type 1 effectiveness implementation randomised controlled trial.](https://doi.org/10.1136/bmj-2026-101157)

- Source: `bmj_abstracts` · published `2026-11-18` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `63` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:43052007`

<h4>Objective</h4>To evaluate the effectiveness of a physical activity coaching intervention compared with text messaging intervention and waitlist control in adults with mobility limitations.<h4>Design</h4>Three arm, pragmatic, superiority, hybrid type 1 effectiveness implementation randomised controlled trial.<h4>Setting</h4>Health services and general communities across five Australian states, 19 December 2018 to 7 March 2023.<h4>Participants</h4>512 community dwelling adults (≥18 years) who self-reported mobility limitations (difficulty or inability to walk 800 m, with or without a walking aid) but were still able to leave home without physical assistance from another person.<h4>Interventions</h4>Participants were randomly assigned in a 1:1:1 allocation ratio to: a six month, multicomponent intervention including fortnightly physiotherapist delivered telephone coaching (coaching group, n=172); a six month, multicomponent intervention including regular unidirectional text messages (texting group, n=171); and no intervention for six months, followed by the six month text messaging intervention (waitlist control group, n=169). Participants and physiotherapists were unmasked to group allocation.<h4>Main outcome measure</h4>The primary outcome was device measured mean daily steps at six months, analysed according to treatment allocation using all available data. Outcome assessors and data analysts were masked to group allocation.<h4>Results</h4>Participants' mean age was 69 years (standard deviation (SD) 10 years) and 76% (n=388/511) were women. More than half (n=263/508, 51%) of participants reported multiple long term conditions as the main reason for their mobility limitation. At six months, mean daily steps were 931 higher (95% confidence interval 409 to 1452 steps; P=0.001) in the coaching group (mean 7256 (SD 3736) steps) compared with waitlist group (6526 (3003) steps); and 571 steps higher (58 to 1084, P=0.03) compared with the texting group (6529 (3029) steps). Mean daily steps in the texting group were 359 higher (-91 to 810, P=0.12) compared with the waitlist group, but this was not statistically significant. The number of adverse events was similar between groups (11/172 (6%) in the coaching group<i>,</i> 7/171 (4%) in the texting group, and 13/169 (8%) in the waitlist group) and none were serious.<h4>Conclusion</h4>A person centred coaching intervention produced modest increases in physical activity and was more effective than a less intensive texting intervention or no intervention among adults with mobility limitations.<h4>Trial registration</h4>Australian New Zealand Clinical Trials Registry ACTRN12618001983291.

### [Assessing Multiple Treatment Regimens for Tuberculosis.](https://doi.org/10.1056/nejmoa2611913)

- Source: `nejm_abstracts` · published `2026-11-19` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `69` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:43056221`

<h4>Background</h4>Ganfeborole and BTZ-043 are new drugs that may improve tuberculosis treatment.<h4>Methods</h4>In this phase 2, randomized, controlled trial, we assigned participants with rifampin-susceptible pulmonary tuberculosis to receive the standard four-drug combination (isoniazid-rifampin-pyrazinamide-ethambutol; HRZE) for 24 weeks; bedaquiline-delamanid-moxifloxacin for 16 weeks; or one of 10 new four-drug combinations for 16 weeks. The new regimens contained ganfeborole, BTZ-043, or both, with combinations of bedaquiline, delamanid, moxifloxacin, pyrazinamide, pretomanid, and linezolid. The primary end point was the log<sub>10</sub>(time to positivity) slope (rate of change from baseline to week 12 in the time to <i>Mycobacterium tuberculosis</i> growth in a positive sputum culture). The treatment effect was assessed as the Bayesian posterior probability of superiority of each new regimen to HRZE with respect to the primary end point (results were used to select regimens for further evaluation, rather than for characterization of efficacy).<h4>Results</h4>A total of 401 participants underwent randomization; 371 were included in the modified intention-to-treat population and 400 in the safety population. We identified two promising regimens for further evaluation: ganfeborole-bedaquiline-pretomanid-moxifloxacin (BPMG) and BTZ-043-bedaquiline-delamanid-moxifloxacin (BDMT). The log<sub>10</sub>(time to positivity) slope was 0.129 log<sub>10</sub>(days) per week (95% Bayesian credible interval, 0.112 to 0.146) with BPMG and 0.142 log<sub>10</sub>(days) per week (95% Bayesian credible interval, 0.126 to 0.160) with BDMT, as compared with 0.118 log<sub>10</sub>(days) per week (95% Bayesian credible interval, 0.105 to 0.131) with HRZE, for a relative difference of 9.1% (95% Bayesian credible interval, -8.6 to 29.0) and 20.5% (95% Bayesian credible interval, 2.7 to 41.1), respectively, and a posterior probability of superiority of 0.83 and 0.99. The incidence of adverse events of grade 3 or higher was 38% in the BPMG group (none of the participants discontinued), 28% in the BDMT group (one discontinued because of a drug reaction with eosinophilia and systemic symptoms), and 24% in the HRZE group (none discontinued). Bedaquiline-moxifloxacin-pyrazinamide-BTZ-043 was discontinued because of hepatotoxic effects.<h4>Conclusions</h4>In this trial, two four-drug regimens, one with ganfeborole and one with BTZ-043, showed evidence of potential efficacy and a safety profile that did not preclude progression to further evaluation. (Funded by Innovative Medicines Initiative 2 Joint Undertaking and others; PARADIGM4TB ClinicalTrials.gov number, NCT06114628.).

### [Mobile Apps for Women’s Physiology Tracking: Structured Narrative Review](https://mhealth.jmir.org/2026/1/e92968)

- Source: `jmir_mhealth` · published `2026-11-19` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `45` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://mhealth.jmir.org/2026/1/e92968`

Background: Mobile health (mHealth) apps for menstrual cycle and fertility tracking are widely used to support self-monitoring, reproductive planning, and health awareness among women. These tools also promise personalized predictions and convenient access to reproductive health information. Still, concerns persist regarding their clinical accuracy, adaptability to irregular cycles, algorithmic transparency, and real-world user experience. Objective: This structured review aimed to evaluate the features, physiological integration, predictive performance, validation practices, and user-reported outcomes of mobile apps designed for menstrual and fertility tracking, and to contextualize the current evidence using the COSMIN (Consensus-based Standards for the Selection of Health Measurement Instruments) and ISPOR (International Society for Health Economics and Outcomes Research) evaluation frameworks. Methods: A structured narrative review of studies evaluating mobile apps for menstrual cycle and fertility tracking was conducted. Literature published between January 2013 and October 2025 was identified through searches of PubMed, Embase, Scopus, and Web of Science, supplemented by semantic and citation-based searches in Semantic Scholar, OpenAlex, and Google Scholar. AI-assisted prioritization was used only to support screening efficiency, whereas human reviewers performed all eligibility decisions and data verification. Forty studies met the predefined eligibility criteria and were included in the qualitative synthesis. Because of substantial heterogeneity in study designs, outcomes, and validation methods, no meta-analysis was performed. Results: Of the 40 included studies, most were observational and relied on self-reported data from a predominantly high-income, technologically literate population. Twenty-four apps incorporated physiological inputs, such as basal body temperature, luteinizing hormone measurements, or wearable-derived metrics, whereas others relied primarily on calendar-based predictions. Multiparameter and sensor-augmented approaches generally demonstrated higher agreement with biological or clinical reference standards than calendar-only methods, with selected studies reporting fertile-window prediction accuracies approaching 85% to 90% under optimal adherence conditions. However, only a small subset of apps has reported formal clinical validation or regulatory clearance. User satisfaction was strongly associated with perceived accuracy, personalization, and usability, whereas inaccurate predictions, particularly among users with irregular cycles, were linked to frustration, anxiety, and high attrition. Conclusions: Menstrual and fertility tracking apps that integrate physiological signals outperform calendar-based approaches in terms of predictive performance; however, robust clinical validation, transparency, and inclusivity remain limited. Reported accuracy metrics should be interpreted cautiously because real-world adherence, irregular cycle patterns, and algorithmic bias substantially affect reliability. These tools are best positioned as decision-support and self-awareness technologies, rather than as autonomous diagnostic instruments. Future evaluations should apply standardized frameworks, such as COSMIN and ISPOR, explicitly communicate uncertainty, and prioritize diverse and irregular-cycle populations to ensure equitable and clinically meaningful digital reproductive health solutions.

### [Plasma eMTBR-tau as a Biomarker for Chronic Traumatic Encephalopathy Tau Tangle Neuropathology.](https://doi.org/10.1001/jama.2026.24780)

- Source: `jama_abstracts` · published `2026-11-19` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `61` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:43056497`

<h4>Importance</h4>Chronic traumatic encephalopathy (CTE) is a neurodegenerative disease that cannot accurately be diagnosed during life due to the absence of in vivo biomarkers for CTE neuropathology.<h4>Objective</h4>To evaluate plasma endogenously cleaved microtubule-binding region tau (eMTBR-tau) as a candidate biomarker for CTE tau tangle neuropathology.<h4>Design, setting, and participants</h4>This case series included 21 former American-style football players who completed a blood draw as part of participation in clinical research at Boston University beginning April 17, 2008, and who subsequently died, donated their brain, and had completed autopsy by October 9, 2026.<h4>Exposures</h4>Plasma eMTBR-tau nucleic acid-linked immunosandwich assay single-plex immunoassay and plasma phosphorylated tau 217 (p-tau217) (SP-X n = 20; Elecsys n = 1).<h4>Main outcomes and measures</h4>CTE status and stage. Cumulative neuropathologic tau burden scores ranged from 0 to 33 (higher scores indicate tau burden; 0, no tau burden).<h4>Results</h4>Among 21 male brain donors (median age at blood draw, CTE positive: 68 years [IQR, 61-77]; CTE negative: 49 years [IQR, 47-56]), 18 had a neuropathologic diagnosis of CTE. Of these participants, 16 had a diagnosis of high-stage CTE (stage IV = 8, stage III = 8); and 2, of low-stage CTE (stage II = 1, stage I = 1). Five CTE-positive participants had intermediate or high Alzheimer disease neuropathologic change (ADNC). Plasma eMTBR-tau concentration was significantly higher in CTE-positive participants (median, 0.019 pg/mL [IQR, 0.004-0.030]) compared with CTE-negative participants (0.001 pg/mL [IQR, 0.001-0.001]; β = 1.61 [95% CI, 0.51-2.70]). Plasma eMTBR-tau concentrations were highest among CTE-positive participants with comorbid ADNC (0.082 pg/mL [IQR, 0.059-0.131]) and intermediate among those with high-stage CTE without ADNC (n = 11; median, 0.009 pg/mL [IQR, 0.004-0.021]). Plasma eMTBR-tau concentration was significantly higher in high-stage CTE (median, 0.021 pg/mL [IQR, 0.006-0.038]) compared with low-stage CTE (median, 0.001 pg/mL [IQR, 0.001-0.002]; β = 1.79 [95% CI, 0.45-3.13]) or no CTE (median, 0.001 pg/mL [IQR, 0.001-0.001]; β = 1.78 [95% CI, 0.85-2.71]). Differences remained significant after exclusion of 5 cases with comorbid ADNC. Plasma eMTBR-tau concentration was associated with higher cumulative neuropathologic tau burden score across 11 brain regions (β = 0.71 [95% CI, 0.38-1.05]). Plasma p-tau217 concentration was positively associated with eMTBR-tau (β = 0.68 [95% CI, 0.32-1.04]) but was not associated with CTE. P-tau217 concentrations were in the negative range (n = 11) or intermediate range (n = 5) for participants without ADNC copathology. Four participants with ADNC had p-tau217 concentrations in the positive range; and 1, in the intermediate range.<h4>Conclusions and relevance</h4>Participants with high-stage CTE had higher concentrations of plasma eMTBR-tau than those with low-stage or no CTE; concentrations were highest among those with CTE and comorbid ADNC. Plasma eMTBR-tau may represent a feasible biomarker candidate for high-stage CTE tau tangle neuropathology. Larger autopsy and clinical validation studies are needed to establish overall diagnostic accuracy and specificity of plasma eMTBR-tau for CTE neuropathology, in addition to the associated prognostic and clinical implications.

### [Spontaneous coronary artery dissection: implications for women's health, clinical care, and future research.](https://doi.org/10.1016/s0140-6736(26)01485-6)

- Source: `lancet_abstracts` · published `2026-11-19` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `51` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:43057279`

Spontaneous coronary artery dissection (SCAD) is an increasingly recognised cause of acute coronary syndromes, characterised by spontaneous separation of the layers of the coronary arterial wall. It predominantly affects younger women, and recent registry and imaging studies indicate that SCAD is more common than previously appreciated, challenging its historical classification as a rare condition. Clinically, SCAD is associated with a substantial early risk of adverse events, particularly in the first weeks after the index dissection. Given the high frequency of spontaneous healing, conservative management is preferred for most patients, whereas revascularisation is generally reserved for ongoing ischaemia or haemodynamic compromise. However, evidence to guide both pharmacological and interventional management remains scarce. This Review summarises current evidence on SCAD epidemiology, underlying mechanisms including genetic susceptibility, diagnostic approaches, management principles, implications for reproductive and women's health, and research priorities to improve outcomes for affected patients.

### [What Kind of Claims Are Transparency, Explainability, and Interpretability? A Definitional Taxonomy for Health AI](https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e95742/)

- Source: `jmir_ai` · published `2026-11-19` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `51` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e95742/`

Transparency, explainability, and interpretability are ubiquitous in the health AI literature, yet are used inconsistently and often interchangeably. This viewpoint does not attempt to define the 3 terms. It asks a prior, meta-level question: what kind of claim do we make when we call a system transparent, explainable, or interpretable? I argue that the 3 terms pick out 3 different kinds of claim. A transparency claim is structural: it concerns what can be inspected about a system. An explainability claim is relational: it concerns whether understanding is successfully conveyed to a specified class of observer. An interpretability claim is functional: it concerns whether a bounded human agent can, in principle, rederive the system’s output by following its procedure. I use the framing example of a clinical decision support system that uses machine learning to identify patients at risk of deterioration. I develop each claim type through competing technical accounts, consider salient objections to each, and defend the taxonomy against them. I close by drawing out what the taxonomy implies for the design, evaluation, reporting, and regulation of health AI, and for physician autonomy at the point of care. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/4a75a6347e9e5cf5947db6107d1707d7" />

### [Balancing Human and AI Features for Digital Autism Diagnostics from Home Videos: Human-In-The-Loop Machine Learning Study](https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e84699/)

- Source: `jmir_ai` · published `2026-11-20` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `52` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e84699/`

&lt;strong&gt;Background:&lt;/strong&gt; Autism spectrum disorder (ASD or autism) diagnosis faces significant delays due to specialist shortages and subjective, clinic-based assessments. Although AI offers a potentially scalable solution via automated video analysis, current state-of-the-art deep learning models often fail to capture subtle, clinically nuanced social behaviors essential for reliable detection. &lt;strong&gt;Objective:&lt;/strong&gt; This study aimed to develop and evaluate a human-in-the-loop (HITL) framework that combines automated AI feature extraction with crowdsourced human annotation for autism classification on naturalistic home videos. We targeted low-confidence AI facial feature extraction outputs to be replaced by human features. We also sought to quantify the relationship between the confidence threshold used for swapping AI features with human features, the volume of human replacement of AI features, and the predictive diagnostic performance. &lt;strong&gt;Methods:&lt;/strong&gt; We implemented a HITL pipeline integrating deep learning models for facial emotion (7-class) feature extraction. A confidence threshold applied to the emitted probability of the AI feature extractor model was used to identify low-confidence facial features or those that the AI model was unable to make a prediction for (henceforth referred to as “unresolved”), which were then replaced by human “feature extraction” (ie, annotation) conducted by rigorously vetted crowd workers. We evaluated this approach on 100 high-quality videos (50 autism and 50 neurotypical) from the GuessWhat? digital therapeutic app. Performance was assessed across decision thresholds (0.1-0.9 in increments of 0.1) using sensitivity, specificity, &lt;i&gt;F&lt;/i&gt;&lt;sub&gt;1&lt;/sub&gt;-score, and area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUROC), with 5-fold cross-validation. &lt;strong&gt;Results:&lt;/strong&gt; Across decision thresholds, performance improved overall but was nonmonotonic at intermediate thresholds. At a threshold of 0.9, 59.7% (597/1000) of frames were replaced by human annotations, and mean sensitivity increased from 0.51 (SD 0.24) at a threshold of 0.1 to 0.66 (SD 0.10) at a threshold of 0.9, an absolute increase of approximately 15 percentage points. The HITL-enhanced multimodal model achieved an aggregate out-of-fold AUROC of 0.76, compared with 0.54 for the AI-only model. &lt;strong&gt;Conclusions:&lt;/strong&gt; In this proof-of-concept study, replacing AI features with human features tended to be associated with higher observed autism classification performance in this particular dataset. We targeted low-confidence and unresolved frames for human annotation. We note that these findings are exploratory and thus do not isolate the independent causal effect of human replacement or establish clinical diagnostic validity. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/cf2866daa2c6589a5290d75fb8c4886c" />

### [Constraint Programming and Neural-Inspired Optimization for Hospital Diagnostic Examination Scheduling: Comparative Development and Evaluation Study](https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e97738/)

- Source: `jmir_ai` · published `2026-11-20` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `57` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e97738/`

Background: Diagnostic examination scheduling is a recurrent operational challenge in health care because limited daily capacity must be allocated across heterogeneous requests with different urgency and follow-up timing requirements. In this context, optimization methods can support more timely and efficient scheduling decisions. Objective: In collaboration with Unidade Local de Saúde de Santo António, an optimization-based approach was proposed to improve the management of outpatient examination scheduling. Methods: Anonymous operational data from Unidade Local de Saúde de Santo António, corresponding to 2025, were analyzed to model the scheduling process over a 4-week (28-d) horizon comprising 2460 examination requests, including 156 examinations with follow-up requirements. Two optimization approaches, Constraint Programming Satisfiability (CP-SAT) and a neural combinatorial optimization (NCO)–inspired constructive approach, were evaluated under the same daily capacity structure, prioritization logic, and penalty-based framework, confirmed across 10 independent runs under 4 solver configurations, with operational outcomes remaining unchanged. The best-performing approach was then compared with the hospital scheduling solution for the same period. Results: CP-SAT achieved the best overall performance among the 2 optimization approaches. It scheduled 1400 examinations vs 1391 for the NCO-inspired approach, reduced free time from 42 to 3 minutes, and achieved a substantially lower total penalty (49,790.78 vs 83,652.10). Both methods achieved 0 unscheduled due examinations, 0 delayed scheduled examinations, and 0 delayed unscheduled examinations. The NCO-inspired approach required less runtime than CP-SAT (17.2 vs 214.6 s). When compared with the hospital scheduling solution, CP-SAT scheduled more examinations (1400 vs 1147) and eliminated all unscheduled due examinations (0 vs 80), delayed scheduled examinations (0 vs 23), and delayed unscheduled examinations (0 vs 40). Conclusions: Both optimization approaches generated feasible schedules under the same evaluation framework, but CP-SAT produced the best overall results in the tested setting. The findings suggest that optimization-based scheduling can improve the timely allocation of diagnostic examinations and reduce follow-up–related delays when compared with routine hospital scheduling practice. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/f51dcf81cc417865b13f65e11eb8a7d8" />

### [Contract Year 2028 Policy and Technical Changes to the Medicare Advantage Program, Medicare Prescription Drug Benefit Program, and Medicare Cost Plan Program (CMS-4214-P2)](https://www.cms.gov/newsroom/fact-sheets/contract-year-2028-policy-technical-changes-medicare-advantage-program-medicare-prescription-drug)

- Source: `cms_fact_sheets` · published `2026-11-20` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `57` · status: `needs_review`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.cms.gov/newsroom/fact-sheets/contract-year-2028-policy-technical-changes-medicare-advantage-program-medicare-prescription-drug`

Contract Year 2028 Policy and Technical Changes to the Medicare Advantage Program, Medicare Prescription Drug Benefit Program, and Medicare Cost Plan Program (CMS-4214-P2) Background On November 20, the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services (CMS) issued a proposed rule that is intended to revise the Medicare Advantage (MA) Program, Medicare Prescription Drug Benefit Program (Part D), and Programs of All-Inclusive Care for the Elderly (PACE) Program. The Contract Year (CY) 2028 MA and Part D proposed rule aims to revitalize competition in MA and Part D and give beneficiaries more meaningful choices in how they receive care. If finalized, the proposed policy updates, alongside a new CMS Innovation Center (CMMI) model, would break down administrative barriers for new market entrants and pioneer localized care innovations. This is the second rule proposing changes applicable to CY 2028. The first rule, proposing changes to implement sections 6219, 6220, and 6224 of the Consolidated Appropriations Act, 2026 ( CMS-4214-P ), was issued on October 16, 2026. Establishing a Standardized Definition of “Healthy Food” for MA Supplemental Benefits CMS proposes to establish a clear “healthy food” standard that is aligned with the 2025–2030 Dietary Guidelines for Americans and apply it to all supplemental benefit categories that include food. Under this proposal, food items must either meet the CMS-defined standard or be specifically recommended by a healthcare provider to meet an enrollee’s medical needs. For example, items like certain high-caloric oral nutrient supplements could still be covered if specifically recommended by a health care provider to meet an enrollee's medical needs. Aligning Data and Value to Nurture Competition and Efficiency in Medicare Advantage (ADVaNCE MA) Model CMS is proposing the CMS Innovation Center’s first mandatory MA model. The model aims to spur local plan competition, enhance beneficiary choice and value, reduce Medicare expenditures, improve quality, and protect taxpayers. If finalized, the 10-year model test would begin in CY 2029, with a pre-implementation period starting on June 8, 2027, operating in randomly selected states with participation from a variety of plan types. The components of the ADVaNCE MA model are: CMMI Inferred Risk Adjustment Model: Explore, through shadow testing, an innovative risk adjustment methodology that builds upon the current CMS Hierarchical Condition Category (HCC) risk adjustment model by integrating multiple, existing data sources already available to CMS, including Original Medicare (OM) claims data, MA encounter data, and enrollment data. This shadow test would compile information on the usability of an alternative approach that could offer greater payment accuracy compared to the traditional risk adjustment methodology while not disrupting the MA market. The learnings from the shadow test are expected to inform future risk adjustment policy. The Diamond Ratings System: A redesigned quality program implemented in select states would replace the current MA and Part D Star Ratings and Quality Bonus Payment system with a localized, cohort-based, quality framework to incentivize continuous improvement and enable beneficiaries to make meaningful, geographically relevant plan comparisons in their local areas. Targeted Supplemental Benefits for Low-Income Subsidy (LIS)-Eligible Individuals: New regulatory flexibilities would allow MA organizations to offer/target to Part D LIS-eligible individuals certain supplemental benefits, some of which are currently only available to chronically ill beneficiaries through Special Supplemental Benefits for the Chronically Ill. These supplemental benefits – healthy food and produce, transportation for non-medical needs, and supports for caregivers – have a focus on high-value prevention and serious illness support, with the goal of improving quality outcomes and reducing Medicare expenditures by avoiding and delaying costly, downstream care. Dialysis Carveout for Beneficiaries with End-Stage Renal Disease: CMS is considering, but not currently proposing, to exclude payment for dialysis services from MA for beneficiaries with end-stage renal disease (ESRD) as a component of the ADVaNCE MA Model. Under this carveout, for beneficiaries with ESRD, dialysis would be covered by OM and paid through the ESRD Prospective Payment System (PPS), while the beneficiaries’ MA organizations would remain financially responsible for all other covered services. If proposed as a future component of the ADVaNCE MA model, details regarding interactions under the ESRD PPS would be included in future rulemaking. Strengthening Current Medicare Advantage and Medicare Prescription Drug Benefit Program Policies Using a CMS Approval Mechanism for Use of the Disaster/Emergency Special Enrollment Period (SEP) and Other Technical Changes: To provide proper oversight of the disaster/emergency SEP, CMS is proposing to require beneficiaries to obtain prior approval through 1-800-MEDICARE. This measure is intended to promote appropriate use of the SEP and reduce the risk of fraud and abuse. Data regarding the use of MA and Part D SEPs suggest that plans may be misusing the disaster/emergency SEP to enroll beneficiaries who were not affected by a declared disaster or emergency, or whose ability to make an MA or Part D election could not reasonably have been affected by such an event. The proposed requirement would help prevent plans, agents, and brokers from directing beneficiaries to use the disaster or emergency SEP when they are not eligible. Cost Sharing Standards for Ground Ambulance and Emergency Services : CMS proposes new dollar limits for ground ambulance and emergency service cost-sharing, based on the latest OM data. Current limits rely on 2021 figures, and CMS has never set a specific cost-sharing dollar cap for ground ambulance services. CMS would update the emergency services cost-sharing limit to $180–$240 (range based on the plan’s maximum out-of-pocket (MOOP) amount), reflecting current OM cost projections. CMS would also cap ground ambulance services coinsurance at 30–50% of the Medicare average cost, translating to a $180–$300 limit (range also based on the plan’s MOOP amount). This would give ground ambulance services a defined dollar cap, with the $300 limit for a lower MOOP limit matching the existing 50% coinsurance standard used for this service (currently for all MOOP types). If finalized, going forward, CMS would update the ground ambulance and emergency services limits every year using the latest projections from the CMS Office of the Actuary (OACT). This is consistent with how CMS already updates copayment limits annually for other service categories, like professional services, to keep cost-sharing limits current. Enrollment Capacity Limits : CMS is proposing to formalize the requirements for MA plans to submit a request for an enrollment capacity limit by establishing requirements for evaluating an enrollment capacity request as part of the bid submission process. CMS is also proposing to rescind the regulation that allows MA plans to request an enrollment capacity limit outside the bid submission process to eliminate gaming opportunities. Finally, CMS proposes to establish a SEP for enrollees who select a plan that is at its enrollment capacity and miss the opportunity to elect a different MA plan. This would ensure that enrollees are still able to select a plan, even if their first choice may not be available. Use of Existing Interoperability Application Programming Interface (APIs) for Prior Authorization Transactions : CMS proposes to establish a requirement that MA organization contracts with providers must include a term requiring in-network providers to submit prior authorization requests and associated documentation through the plan’s FHIR-based Prior Authorization API. CMS would also require MA organizations to test and confirm the ability of their in-network providers to send and receive information through the FHIR-based Prior Authorization API annually. The proposal also includes a Request for Information (RFI) regarding potential implications for rural and other health care providers, as well as an RFI regarding options for further advancing the health tech ecosystem. Enrollee Notification Requirements for Provider Terminations, Continuity of Care, and SEP Notice Consolidation : CMS proposes to define a "significant change" to an MA plan's provider network and require plans to notify enrollees when the significant change is final. A network change would be significant if the provider termination meets either of two thresholds: the termination affects at least 10% of the plan's enrolled population, or the terminated provider represents at least 10% of the plan's contracted network for a given specialty. When a significant change occurs, affected enrollees would be informed by their MA plan that they are eligible for a SEP. CMS also proposes revising the definition of "affected enrollee" at 42 CFR § 422.62(b)(23)(ii) to align with 42 CFR § 422.111(e), applying a consistent one-year lookback period regardless of provider type. Under this standard, any enrollee who saw the terminated provider within the year before termination—and whose plan meets the 10% significance threshold—qualifies for notification and the SEP. Finally, CMS proposes a new continuity-of-care requirement: MA plans must continue covering services furnished by terminated providers to affected enrollees at in-network rates with in-network cost sharing for at least 90 days following a provider termination. Seamless Continuation of Coverage for Newly Medicare-Eligible Individuals: To streamline the enrollment process into an MA plan for individuals currently enrolled in Marketplace or commercial plans who are approaching Medicare eligibility, CMS is considering creating a seamless continuation of coverage process, similar to what existed prior to 2018, where certain newly Medicare-eligible individuals could be automatically enrolled in an MA plan offered by the same organization that the individual chose for their current non-Medicare coverage. This would improve the transition into Medicare by automatically placing newly eligible beneficiaries into coverage that more closely aligns with their health insurance experience prior to Medicare eligibility, while maintaining their ability to choose whatever Medicare coverage option works best for them. Transforming Agent and Broker Compensation and Oversight CMS is proposing several changes to reduce burden, add guardrails, streamline regulations, and provide a more flexible framework for agent and broker compensation and oversight. Specifically, CMS is proposing to: Eliminate CMS defined compensation caps and renewal limits for agent and broker compensation; Streamline agent and broker oversight with market-based compensation guardrails, Eliminate duplicative reporting and training requirements, Eliminate referral fee caps; Revise and clarify definitions; Enhance compensation data collection to increase transparency; Create a methodology to identify compensation outliers with restrictions around payment of outlier amounts; Permit organizations to change compensation amounts during the plan year, with advance notification to CMS; Remove unneeded or overly prescriptive regulatory requirements. Strengthening Maximum Fair Price Effectuation Policies for Medicare Advantage CMS is proposing policies to facilitate the effectuation of the maximum fair price (MFP) under the Medicare Drug Price Negotiation Program for selected drugs payable under Part B that are furnished or administered to an individual enrolled in MA. Specifically, CMS is proposing: To require that any contract between the MA organization and a provider, or between a first tier, downstream, or related entity and a provider on the MA organization's behalf, for the provider’s participation in one or more of t
