### [Lifestyle and Clinical Factors Associated With Cardiometabolic Multimorbidity Among Adults in Beijing: Cross-Sectional Bayesian Network Analysis](https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e91167)

- Source: `jmir_medinform` · published `2026-11-25` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `55` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e91167`

Background: Cardiometabolic multimorbidity (CMM), defined as the co-occurrence of at least 2 of 3 conditions (diabetes, coronary heart disease, and stroke), is associated with higher mortality than the individual conditions. Lifestyle behaviors, adiposity, and clinical abnormalities are interrelated, but their conditional dependency structure and sex-specific organization remain incompletely understood. Objective: We aimed to characterize sex-specific dependency patterns linking lifestyle factors, adiposity, clinical indicators, and CMM using Bayesian networks (BNs); identify structurally important nodes; and compare BN performance with that of other machine learning models. Methods: We analyzed baseline data from 76,128 adults in the Beijing Health Management Cohort. Within each sex, data were split into training (80%) and held-out test (20%) sets before preprocessing. Imputation, discretization, and feature selection were performed using training data only. Sex-specific BNs were learned using hill climbing with bootstrap arc-strength thresholding and characterized using graph-theoretic metrics. BN predictions were based on the conditional probability table of CMM given its parent nodes. BN performance was compared with that of extreme gradient boosting (XGBoost), neural network, and random forest models. Sensitivity analyses were performed by varying the arc-strength threshold from 0.40 to 0.85. Results: The male BN included 15 nodes and 39 edges, whereas the female BN was composed of 13 nodes and 26 edges. Age and hypertension were directly connected to CMM in both sexes, whereas a direct connection between high triglyceride levels and CMM was observed only in males. Age had the highest betweenness centrality in males, whereas hypertension ranked highest in females. Body roundness index (BRI) was not directly connected to CMM in either overall network. Its betweenness centrality was below the network mean in males (0.043 vs 0.059) but above the network mean in females (0.138 vs 0.078). In held-out testing, the areas under the receiver operating characteristic curve for the BNs were 0.810 (95% CI 0.782‐0.838) in males and 0.846 (95% CI 0.810‐0.882) in females. The areas under the precision-recall curve were 0.090 and 0.084, respectively, representing 3.6 and 5.6 times the respective prevalence baselines. Mean predicted risks approximated observed prevalence (2.54% vs 2.53% in males and 1.52% vs 1.51% in females), with calibration slopes of 1.11 and 1.03, respectively. BN discrimination was significantly lower than that of XGBoost in both sexes and that of the neural network in females but significantly higher than that of the random forest in both sexes. The principal direct connections to CMM were preserved across all thresholds. Conclusions: Sex-specific BNs characterized conditional associations linking lifestyle, adiposity, and clinical factors with CMM. Age and hypertension showed direct connections to CMM in both sexes, whereas a direct connection between high triglyceride levels and CMM was observed only in males. BRI was not directly connected to CMM in either overall network and showed above-average betweenness in females. Although BNs showed favorable calibration, their discrimination did not exceed that of the strongest comparator models. Given the cross-sectional design, these relationships are associational and require longitudinal confirmation.

### [Multimodal AI for bacterial vs viral upper respiratory tract infection multicentre development and internal validation](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41746-026-03492-7)

- Source: `npj_digital_medicine` · published `2026-11-25` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `59` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41746-026-03492-7`

Distinguishing Group A streptococcus (GAS) pharyngitis from viral upper respiratory tract infections (URTIs) is critical for appropriate antibiotic use but remains difficult on symptoms alone. We developed and internally validated a multimodal artificial intelligence (AI) model fusing pharyngeal images (Swin Transformer) with clinical metadata via gradient boosting decision trees, using 6666 visits and 119,988 images from 44 facilities collected March 2023–August 2025. For discriminating GAS from non-GAS (influenza A/B, COVID-19), the fusion model achieved area under the curve (AUC) 0.917 (95% confidence interval 0.907–0.926), outperforming image-only (0.902), metadata-only (0.871) and proxy Centor/McIsaac scores. At a pre-specified high-specificity threshold (≥0.90) sensitivity was 0.761; simulations indicated antibiotic-prescription reductions of 23–56% across GAS prevalences of 5–20%. External validation and prospective evaluation are needed.

### [Myocardial injury with non-obstructive coronary arteries: diagnostic pathways and stratified treatment.](https://doi.org/10.1016/s0140-6736(26)01640-5)

- Source: `lancet_abstracts` · published `2026-11-25` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `47` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:43085372`

Myocardial injury with non-obstructive coronary arteries (MINOCA), formerly known as myocardial infarction with non-obstructive coronary arteries, challenges the long-standing assumption that myocardial infarction is synonymous with obstructive coronary artery disease. Rather than a paradox, MINOCA reflects the limitations of angiography-centred paradigms and the complexity of contemporary cardiovascular pathophysiology. Consistent with this updated definition, this Review approaches MINOCA as a working diagnosis that encompasses diverse mechanisms, including plaque disruption, coronary vasomotor dysfunction, microvascular disease, and non-coronary conditions. We propose a pragmatic, mechanism-oriented framework for selecting and sequencing complementary investigations, highlighting how intracoronary imaging, cardiac magnetic resonance, and coronary functional testing can be integrated according to the clinical scenario to refine diagnosis, while balancing diagnostic yield with procedural risk and resource availability. We discuss the implications for management, emphasising that treatment should be guided by underlying pathophysiology rather than a uniform approach derived from obstructive myocardial infarction. Emerging evidence supports the implementation of a stratified therapy to improve patient-centred outcomes. Finally, we discuss research priorities for improving mechanism-specific diagnosis and treatment, arguing that MINOCA represents both a conceptual advance and a transitional step toward a mechanism-based taxonomy of myocardial injury and infarction.

### [Potential of multimedia-supported informed consent in clinical practice: a systematic review](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41746-026-03460-1)

- Source: `npj_digital_medicine` · published `2026-11-25` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `54` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41746-026-03460-1`

Providing targeted, quality-assured patient information is essential for informed and autonomous decision-making but remains resource-intensive when delivered face-to-face. Multimedia-supported informed consent may enhance patient understanding and satisfaction while reducing physician time by complementing, rather than replacing, direct interaction. Our PRISMA-based systematic review, aiming to enable clinicians to implement and evaluate such a supplement, identified 907 articles of which 110 reports from OECD countries were included, including 62 randomized controlled trials. Among studies assessing the outcomes of interest, 71% reported improved patient understanding with multimedia tools compared to standard consent, 41% reported increased patient satisfaction (none reported decreases), and 91% found reduced or unchanged patient anxiety. In synthesis, to the extent possible despite substantial heterogeneity, findings suggest benefits and potential efficiency gains, and we were able to derive recommendations. However, considerable methodological variability and limitations affecting internal validity highlight the need for high-quality head-to-head comparative studies to draw more robust conclusions.

### [Preconception antidepressant use in fathers and risk of metabolic disorders in offspring: Danish population based cohort study.](https://doi.org/10.1136/bmj-2026-101239)

- Source: `bmj_abstracts` · published `2026-11-25` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `60` · status: `scored`
- Event: `updated` · identity: `pmid:43085809`

<h4>Objectives</h4>To investigate the associations between paternal antidepressant use during spermatogenesis and risk of metabolic disorders in offspring.<h4>Design</h4>Nationwide population based cohort study.<h4>Setting</h4>Danish national registers.<h4>Participants</h4>1 365 887 liveborn singleton infants in Denmark from 2000 to 2023, followed from birth until 31 December 2023, death, emigration, or first occurrence of outcome, whichever came first.<h4>Main outcome measures</h4>Metabolic disorders in offspring, including type 2 diabetes, hypertension, hypercholesterolaemia, and overweight or obesity, were identified from hospital and prescription records. Offspring of fathers who took antidepressants during spermatogenesis (three months before pregnancy) were compared with those of fathers who did not take antidepressants during this period and fathers who used antidepressants only during pregnancy to further account for underlying paternal mental health conditions. Associations between paternal antidepressant use and metabolic disorders, collectively and individually, were estimated as propensity score weighted hazard ratios using Cox regression.<h4>Results</h4>23 572 (1.7%) offspring were born to fathers who used antidepressants during spermatogenesis. During a mean follow-up of 12.1 years (standard deviation 7.0 years), 18 353 (1.3%) offspring were diagnosed with a metabolic disorder, including 364 whose fathers used antidepressants during spermatogenesis and 17 989 whose fathers did not use antidepressants during this period (1.39 <i>v</i> 1.11 events per 1000 person years, respectively). Offspring whose fathers used antidepressants during spermatogenesis had a slightly higher risk of developing metabolic disorders (weighted hazard ratio 1.20, 95% confidence interval (CI) 1.04 to 1.38) compared with offspring who were unexposed. The associations varied by specific metabolic outcomes, with weighted hazard ratios ranging from 0.81 (95% CI 0.45 to 1.47) for hypercholesterolaemia to 1.64 (95% CI 1.21 to 2.22) for hypertension. However, when compared with offspring of fathers who took antidepressants during the pregnancy period only, no increased risk was found of any metabolic disorders (0.75, 95% CI 0.58 to 0.98) or of specific metabolic disorders.<h4>Conclusions</h4>The slightly increased risk of metabolic disorders in offspring of fathers using antidepressants during spermatogenesis was probably attributable to underlying paternal mental health disorders. These findings offer reassurance for men treated with antidepressants before pregnancy.

### [Predicting Rheumatoid Arthritis Flares Using Wearable Device Data: Deep Learning Study](https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e95089)

- Source: `jmir` · published `2026-11-25` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `56` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e95089`

Background: Rheumatoid arthritis (RA) flares are common and unpredictable. Current monitoring relies on episodic clinical visits and laboratory testing, thereby limiting the ability to anticipate disease worsening. Wearable devices may support continuous remote monitoring, but models must account for missing real-world data, heterogeneous consumer devices, and the distinction between symptomatic disease activity and systemic inflammation. Objective: The study aimed to develop and evaluate deep learning (DL) time-series models using wearable-derived physiological data to predict symptomatic and inflammatory RA flares up to 28 days in advance. Methods: In a prospective cohort study, adults with RA used a smartwatch (Apple Watch; Apple Inc), a wearable fitness tracker (Fitbit; Google LLC), or a smart ring (Oura Ring; Oura Health Oy). Daily wearable-derived heart rate, heart rate variability, and step-count were harmonized across devices and linked with smartphone-based patient-reported outcomes (PROs) and available C-reactive protein results. Symptomatic flares were defined using longitudinal RAPID3 (Routine Assessment of Patient Index Data 3) scores, and inflammatory flares were defined using a prespecified C-reactive protein threshold. Classical machine learning and DL models were trained to predict daily flare vs remission status at 0-, 7-, 14-, 21-, and 28-day horizons. Model evaluation used a temporally ordered 70/30 within-participant train-test split. Performance was assessed using receiver operating characteristic area under the curve (ROCAUC), area under the precision-recall curve (AUPRC), and participant-level sensitivity. This prediction-model analysis was not prospectively registered. Results: Forty-nine participants contributed 5624 days of overlapping wearable and clinical data. Participants used Apple Watch (n=31), Fitbit (n=15), or Oura Ring (n=3). DL models outperformed machine learning models using imputed wearable data, with average ROCAUC improvements of 5.3% for symptomatic flares and 18.4% for inflammatory flares, and AUPRC improvements of 9.3% and 44.1%, respectively. For 7-day-ahead prediction, transformer models incorporating wearable-derived physiology and demographic features achieved ROCAUCs of 0.95 for both symptomatic and inflammatory flares, with AUPRCs of 0.92 and 0.91, respectively. Adding daily PRO improved participant-level sensitivity for inflammatory flare prediction from 0.67 to 0.87. Performance remained stable across prediction horizons for up to 4 weeks. Conclusions: Wearable-derived physiological signals analyzed with DL may support personalized forecasting of symptomatic and inflammatory RA flare states weeks in advance. By integrating passive digital biomarkers with PROs, this approach offers a scalable framework for proactive RA monitoring that could help identify disease exacerbations earlier than episodic clinical assessment alone. Further validation in larger, more diverse cohorts and prospective studies is needed to determine whether wearable-guided forecasting improves clinical decision-making, timely intervention, and long-term outcomes. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/36fa56424f327db9a7db5959bf80c202" />

### [Sequential framework for predicting postoperative recurrence and antiseizure-medication withdrawal risk in mesial temporal lobe epilepsy](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41746-026-03502-8)

- Source: `npj_digital_medicine` · published `2026-11-25` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `65` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41746-026-03502-8`

Mesial temporal lobe epilepsy (mTLE) is a common drug-resistant epilepsy. Temporal lobe resection is the most effective treatment, but postoperative recurrence and antiseizure medication (ASM) withdrawal remain major clinical challenges. Existing prediction tools are limited by endpoint specificity, restricted external validation, and lack of integration into sequential postoperative management pathways. We developed a sequential modelling strategy in mTLE, spanning recurrence prediction to ASM withdrawal risk stratification. In phase 1, postoperative recurrence models were developed and validated using 52 preoperative multimodal features in 1278 patients across cohorts 1–4 (three retrospective, one prospective). Five top-ranked models achieved AUCs of 0.926–0.932 (internal) and 0.830–0.931 (three external sets), with a three-tier (low/intermediate/high) risk stratification. In phase 2, ASM withdrawal recurrence models were developed and validated in 679 seizure-free, phase 1 low/intermediate-risk patients eligible for withdrawal across cohorts 5–8 (three retrospective, one prospective). Five top-ranked models achieved AUCs of 0.897–0.902 (internal) and 0.789–0.933 (three external sets), with risk stratification for individualized withdrawal estimation. Models showed good calibration, net clinical benefit, and SHAP-based interpretability. A research-based risk-estimation web tool was developed to support potential clinical translation. This promising modelling strategy integrates population-level risk stratification with individualized prediction, providing preliminary postoperative guidance: high-recurrence-risk patients may not be ideal for early ASM withdrawal despite seizure freedom, while low-risk patients may be considered for earlier withdrawal evaluation. Given the primarily retrospective design and relatively small phase 2 prospective cohort, further prospective external validation and clinical-impact studies are required before routine implementation.

### [Trustworthiness Evaluation of Medical Vision-Language Models: Scoping Review and Proposed Minimum Evaluation Checklist](https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e102330/)

- Source: `jmir_ai` · published `2026-11-25` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `59` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e102330/`

Background: Medical vision-language models combine images and text to answer questions, generate reports, and support image interpretation. Task accuracy alone does not establish whether their outputs depend on relevant visual evidence or remain reliable under changes in the input. Objective: This study aimed to describe how trustworthiness is evaluated in medical vision-language models, identify gaps in evaluation and reporting, and develop a preliminary minimum evaluation checklist. Methods: We conducted a scoping review using the PRISMA-ScR (Preferred Reporting Items for Systematic Reviews and Meta-Analyses extension for Scoping Reviews). We searched PubMed/MEDLINE, Embase, Scopus, Web of Science Core Collection, and IEEE Xplore for English-language publications from January 1, 2022, to March 25, 2026. Eligible reports were peer-reviewed full papers that evaluated an image-and-language model in a medical imaging context and reported a measured result on at least 1 of 8 trustworthiness dimensions. One reviewer charted the studies, and a second verified the extraction. We summarized evaluation practices descriptively and applied the proposed 8-item MiTEC (Minimum Trustworthiness Evaluation Checklist) retrospectively. We did not perform a risk-of-bias appraisal. Results: We identified 516 records, screened 506 database records based on the title and abstract, and assessed 72 unique reports after merging duplicate full-text entries. The revised synthesis includes 29 studies: 11 journal articles and 18 conference papers. Robustness was evaluated in 37.9% (11/29), hallucination in 24.1% (7/29), distribution shift in 20.7% (6/29), and visual grounding in 17.2% (5/29). Interpretability and fairness were each evaluated in 13.8% (4/29), uncertainty or confidence reliability in 6.9% (2/29), and safety in 3.4% (1/29). Two studies used a text-only baseline, and 4 used at least 1 image-reliance control. No included study was confirmed to report a formal calibration metric or a selective-prediction curve. Retrospective MiTEC scoring identified 41 (17.7%) reported items among 232 study-item pairs. Conclusions: Evaluation practices varied across the included studies. Image-reliance controls, formal calibration measures, and subgroup analyses were uncommon. MiTEC is an author-proposed item set that requires independent testing and stakeholder consensus. The findings describe the included sample; incomplete linkage of preprints to published versions limits claims about the wider literature. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/4f502c8b3d7895341a7713a3e97c14ad" />

### [Application of Intelligent Digital Monitoring for Colorectal Cancer Nutrition: Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis](https://mhealth.jmir.org/2026/1/e91224)

- Source: `jmir_mhealth` · published `2026-11-26` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `49` · status: `scored`
- Event: `new` · identity: `url:https://mhealth.jmir.org/2026/1/e91224`

Background: Nutritional problems are common during colorectal cancer (CRC) treatment and recovery; digital technologies may extend nutrition care beyond clinical visits. Intermittent care may miss intake, symptoms, and nutritional status; prior reviews addressed broader digital health or mobile app interventions rather than monitoring-informed CRC nutrition management. Objective: This review evaluated intelligent digital monitoring–supported nutrition management with respect to anthropometric outcomes, nutritional status, dietary intake, quality of life, and nutrition-related biochemical markers in adults with CRC. Methods: We searched PubMed, Embase, Web of Science, Scopus, CENTRAL, ProQuest Dissertations and Theses Global, China National Knowledge Infrastructure, Wanfang Data, VIP Database, and SinoMed from inception through June 30, 2026. Eligible interventions required repeated nutrition-related data collection, digitally traceable recording or transmission, and data-informed feedback or action. We included randomized controlled trials (RCTs), nonrandomized comparative studies, prospective cohorts, and prospective single-arm studies. Risk of bias was assessed using Cochrane Risk of Bias 2 (RoB 2) and ROBINS-I (Risk of Bias in Non-Randomized Studies of Interventions) tools. Comparable RCTs were pooled using random-effects models; all other studies were synthesized narratively. Evidence certainty was assessed using the GRADE (Grading of Recommendations Assessment, Development, and Evaluation) approach. Results: Ultimately, 14 unique studies from 15 reports (2617 participants), including 7 RCTs, were included; 9 were conducted in China. All RCTs had some concerns; 5 of 7 nonrandomized studies had serious risk of bias. Body weight was synthesized narratively. Patient-Generated Subjective Global Assessment (PG-SGA) scores were lower than in controls (3 RCTs; n=244; mean difference [MD] −1.50, 95% CI −1.85 to −1.15; moderate certainty). Primary analysis showed higher energy intake (2 RCTs; n=138; MD 204.30 kcal/d, 95% CI 191.57 to 217.04; very low certainty), but this finding was not robust in a post hoc small-sample sensitivity analysis (95% CI −379.28 to 787.88; =.14). Effects were unclear for BMI (3 RCTs; n=305; MD 0.82 kg/m², 95% CI −0.27 to 1.90; low certainty), protein intake (2 RCTs; n=138; MD 20.16 g/d, 95% CI −37.39 to 77.72; ²=74%; very low certainty), quality of life (3 RCTs; n=188; MD 16.82, 95% CI −14.62 to 48.26; ²=95%; very low certainty), albumin (2 RCTs; n=188; MD 4.75 g/L, 95% CI −36.48 to 45.98; ²=98%; very low certainty), and hemoglobin (2 RCTs; n=188; MD 9.36 g/L, 95% CI −104.87 to 123.59; ²=95%; very low certainty). Conclusions: Intelligent digital monitoring–supported nutrition management may improve nutritional status, but effects on energy intake and other outcomes remain uncertain. Monitoring should complement, not replace, professional nutritional assessment and individualized care. Few trials, risk-of-bias concerns, heterogeneity, and geographic concentration limit conclusions; larger multicenter RCTs with standardized outcomes and defined monitoring-feedback components are needed. Trial Registration: PROSPERO CRD420251218785; https://www.crd.york.ac.uk/PROSPERO/view/CRD420251218785

### [Authors’ Reply: Baseline Fidelity and Evaluation Independence in Symptom Terminology Normalization](https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e115495)

- Source: `jmir_medinform` · published `2026-11-26` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `31` · status: `scored`
- Event: `new` · identity: `url:https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e115495`

We respond to methodological concerns regarding our study of symptom terminology normalization in traditional Chinese medicine, clarifying the adaptation of comparison models, the preparation of alignment examples, and the selection of the entity-recognition component. We distinguish the inference rules used in our implementation from the capabilities of the original models and agree with the readers’ recommendations for strengthening the evaluation design. With sufficient additional annotated records, we plan to improve patient-level data partitioning. We will restrict corpus-derived resources and augmentation to training data, using validation data for model selection. The finalized pipeline will be evaluated on an independent test set, with ablation analyses and separate assessments of terms with wording seen or unseen during training.

### [Baseline Fidelity and Evaluation Independence in Symptom Terminology Normalization](https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e113829)

- Source: `jmir_medinform` · published `2026-11-26` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `55` · status: `scored`
- Event: `new` · identity: `url:https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e113829`

Yao et al proposed a split-then-concatenate normalization framework for standardizing complex symptom expressions in traditional Chinese medicine. We highlight two issues relevant to interpreting the reported performance gains: the fidelity of the PLM-ICD baseline implementation and the independence of the evaluation process. The original PLM-ICD method supports multilabel prediction, whereas the study describes it as a single-output classifier. In addition, corpus-derived terminology resources were constructed using all 500 electronic medical records despite a 400/100 train-test split. Clarification of baseline implementation, training-pair provenance, model-selection procedures, and evaluation on untouched data would strengthen interpretation of the framework’s contribution.

### [Clinical Plausibility and Pretraining Utility of Virtual Narrow-Band Imaging for Laryngeal Lesion Classification: Multicenter Model Development and Validation Study](https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e109811)

- Source: `jmir_medinform` · published `2026-11-26` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `62` · status: `scored`
- Event: `new` · identity: `url:https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e109811`

Background: Pathology-labeled optical narrow-band imaging (NBI) is difficult to assemble for laryngeal lesion classification. Translating existing white-light imaging (WLI) into virtual NBI (Vir-NBI) may improve learning from limited real-NBI data, but its value beyond that of the original images is uncertain. Objective: This study aimed to determine whether Vir-NBI pretraining improves real-NBI classification or label efficiency compared with pretraining on the same pathology-labeled WLI. Methods: This multicenter study compared ResNet-50 classifiers pretrained on pathology-labeled WLI or paired Vir-NBI images generated by a frozen, region-of-interest–guided CycleGAN (cycle-consistent generative adversarial network). The primary outcome was the image-level area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUC) difference (Vir-NBI minus WLI). Label efficiency was evaluated in these external cohorts with 10 to 100 labeled real-NBI patients and 25 paired training runs per sample size. Color-matched augmentation and paired test-modality analyses examined the influence of color change and test modality. Prospective temporal validation and a 12-clinician assessment evaluated patient-level performance and perceived image quality. Results: The CHCAMS (National Cancer Center/Cancer Hospital, Chinese Academy of Medical Sciences and Peking Union Medical College) cohort comprised 1245 patients with 8476 paired WLI–Vir-NBI images. External validation included 182 patients from CHSZCAMS (Cancer Hospital & Shenzhen Hospital, Chinese Academy of Medical Sciences and Peking Union Medical College) and 129 from People’s Hospital of Wenshan Prefecture (WSPH). External image-level AUCs were 0.806 with WLI and 0.787 with Vir-NBI (difference −0.019, 95% CI −0.047 to 0.008; =.17). With 10 labeled real-NBI patients, external patient-level AUCs were 0.843 and 0.773, respectively (difference −0.070, 95% CI −0.116 to −0.029; Holm-adjusted =.006). The normalized area-under-the-learning-curve difference across 10 to 100 patients favored WLI (−0.049, 95% CI −0.090 to −0.011; Holm-adjusted =.01). Color-matched WLI augmentation yielded higher external image-level AUC than with Vir-NBI (0.774 vs 0.739; difference 0.036, unadjusted 95% CI 0.004-0.070). In the 58-case examination-linked cohort, relative Vir-NBI performance improved on translated test images (primary-model interaction 0.235, unadjusted 95% CI 0.080-0.390); fixed-duration and all-view sensitivity analyses had case-level CIs that included 0. In the CHCAMS prospective validation cohort (138 patients), patient-level AUCs were 0.846 (95% CI 0.775‐0.910) and 0.844 (95% CI 0.777‐0.905), respectively. WLI identified 90.9% (60/66) of high-risk patients, compared with 81.8% (54/66) with Vir-NBI, reflecting 6 discordant patients (exact McNemar =.03; Holm-adjusted =.06). Among 100 image pairs from the CHCAMS source cohort, clinicians rated plausibility as 4 to 5 in 86.9% (1043/1200) of ratings; intraclass correlation coefficients for the mean ratings of 12 clinicians ranged from 0.398 (95% CI 0.265-0.497) to 0.583 (95% CI 0.471-0.659). Conclusions: Vir-NBI pretraining did not demonstrate incremental value over WLI for real-NBI classification. External learning curves favored WLI with small real-NBI training sets. Test-domain dependence suggests that compatibility with real-NBI may contribute to performance differences.

### [Developing an Interdisciplinary Framework for Behavioral Data Collection to Advance Coupled Behavioral-Epidemic Models: Cross-Sectional Study in 6 European Countries](https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e102346)

- Source: `jmir` · published `2026-11-26` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `63` · status: `scored`
- Event: `new` · identity: `url:https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e102346`

Background: Human behavior influences infectious disease spread and the effectiveness of public health interventions. However, behavioral dynamics are often simplified in policy-oriented epidemic models, largely owing to the scarcity of suitable empirical data. Objective: This study aimed to develop an interdisciplinary approach to collecting theory-grounded behavioral data on SARS-CoV-2 vaccination, capturing the complexity and temporal dynamics of preventive health behaviors while quantifying factors relevant to epidemic modeling. Methods: We conducted a cross-sectional online survey of adults in France, Germany, Hungary, Italy, Spain, and the United Kingdom from March to July 2024, using age-, sex-, education-, and region-based quotas. Guided by both the capability, opportunity, motivation-behavior model and the requirements of epidemic modeling, we combined validated instruments with modeling-oriented measures in the questionnaire. We assessed temporal changes in retrospectively reported perceived COVID-19 severity and willingness to vaccinate across 5 time points, associations between vaccination willingness, uptake, and timeliness, and differences in behavioral indicators by vaccination status. To capture interactions relevant to both disease transmission and information exchange, we measured age-specific in-person and discussion contacts. Selected measurements then informed a coupled behavioral-epidemic model to illustrate how intention-behavior gaps and changing intention shape simulated vaccination dynamics. Results: After data quality filtering, 22,228 survey responses were analyzed. Vaccination willingness correlated with uptake and timeliness, although the proportion of initially unwilling respondents who received ≥2 vaccine doses ranged from 5.7% (95% CI 3.2%‐8.3%) to 17.9% (95% CI 12.8%‐23.1%) across countries. Respondents with low willingness delayed vaccination by approximately 2 months compared to those with high willingness (low willingness: 5.3 months, 95% CI 5‐5.8; high willingness: 3.2 months, 95% CI 3‐3.4). Respondents retrospectively reported declining perceived COVID-19 severity and willingness to vaccinate over time. Behavioral differences between vaccinated and unvaccinated respondents extended across 96% of the analyzed indicators (Wilcoxon rank-sum tests, &lt;.001) and were largest in the motivation domain. Vaccinated respondents reported higher vaccine confidence and trust in health authorities and lower concerns about vaccine safety. Average daily discussion contacts mirrored age-specific patterns of in-person contacts, ranging from 1.4 (95% CI 1.2‐1.6) to 2.9 (95% CI 2.5‐3.2) among adults aged &lt;60 years across countries, compared with 0.5 (95% CI 0.4‐0.6) to 1.2 (95% CI 1‐1.4) among those aged ≥60 years. In model simulations, incorporating the intention-behavior gap increased vaccination coverage, while allowing willingness to change over time slowed uptake and reduced final coverage. Conclusions: This interdisciplinary approach provides a practical route from behavioral theory to empirical measurements that inform states, transitions, vaccination and social contact processes in behavioral-epidemic models. By providing an empirical basis for representing behavioral heterogeneity and dynamics, this study reduces reliance on simplified behavioral assumptions, supporting the development of more realistic behavioral-epidemic models and effective response strategies. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/ffd7a6adacb71a5401a572edd3334370" />

### [Diagnostic Performance of Imaging-Based AI vs Radiologists for Preoperative Lymph Node Metastasis in Esophageal Cancer: Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis](https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e95893)

- Source: `jmir` · published `2026-11-26` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `54` · status: `scored`
- Event: `new` · identity: `url:https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e95893`

Background: Imaging-based AI technologies, particularly deep learning and radiomics, offer a novel approach to improving the accuracy of lymph node metastasis (LNM) diagnosis. Objective: This study aimed to evaluate the diagnostic performance of imaging-based AI models compared with radiologists in detecting preoperative LNM in esophageal cancer (EC). Methods: A systematic literature search was conducted across 5 databases (PubMed, Embase, Web of Science, Cochrane Library, and IEEE Xplore) up to August 19, 2026. Eligible studies assessed imaging-based AI for preoperative LNM detection in EC (pathology reference standard). The protocol was prospectively registered in the International Prospective Register of Systematic Reviews (PROSPERO CRD420251169109). The PROBAST+AI (Prediction Model Risk of Bias Assessment Tool with AI) tool was used for quality assessment. Bivariate random-effects models were used to calculate pooled sensitivity, specificity, diagnostic odds ratio (DOR), and area under the curve (AUC); between-study heterogeneity was described using ² and ² (interpreted descriptively rather than categorically); homogeneity of study effects was tested with the statistic, and 95% prediction intervals (PIs) were derived for all pooled estimates. Head-to-head comparisons used a random-effects meta-analysis of within-study paired differences (restricted maximum likelihood with Hartung-Knapp-Sidik-Jonkman adjustment). Results: A total of 18 studies were included. Across the 17 internal validation sets (2485 participants), AI models demonstrated a pooled sensitivity of 0.77 (95% CI 0.73-0.81; 95% PI 0.62-0.87), specificity of 0.79 (95% CI 0.73-0.84; 95% PI 0.48-0.94), a DOR of 12.88 (95% CI 9.57-17.33; 95% PI 4.5-37.0), and an AUC of 0.83 (95% CI 0.81-0.86; 95% PI 0.80-0.86). In the 10 head-to-head studies, AI showed higher sensitivity than radiologists (a paired difference of +0.27, 95% CI 0.11-0.37; 95% PI −0.26 to 0.45; =.006) and higher discrimination (DOR ratio 4.65, 95% CI 2.53-8.56; 95% PI 0.97-22.42; &lt;.001), whereas specificity did not differ (+0.06, 95% CI −0.08 to 0.16; 95% PI −0.39 to 0.26; =.30). Pooled arm-specific AUCs were 0.86 (95% CI 0.82-0.89; 95% PI 0.79-0.91) for AI and 0.67 (95% CI 0.59-0.75; 95% PI 0.44-0.84) for radiologists. In 4 external validation studies, pooled sensitivity was 0.66 (95% CI 0.46-0.81; 95% PI 0.31-0.90), specificity was 0.85 (95% CI 0.75-0.91; 95% PI 0.63-0.94), and the DOR was 10.42 (95% CI 3.50-31.01; 95% PI 0.6-142.0). These estimates remain subject to substantial heterogeneity and design-related bias. Conclusions: Imaging-based AI models demonstrated promising pooled diagnostic performance for preoperative LNM detection in EC. By pairing AI with radiologists’ performance in the same cohorts, reporting PIs alongside CIs, and separating internal from external validation, this review provides a more realistic assessment than previous average-effect estimates. In predominantly retrospective and heterogeneous cohorts, AI had higher pooled sensitivity and discrimination than radiologists, whereas specificity did not differ; the selective extraction of the model with the highest AUC and very low certainty of evidence precludes conclusions about clinical superiority. These findings support AI as a decision-support tool, warranting prospective, multicenter external validation and prespecified human-AI comparisons rather than replacing radiologists. Trial Registration: PROSPERO CRD420251169109; https://tinyurl.com/8efd2cay <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/2b54fa0c5b91a38de85654e29de6f41f" />

### [Four Imperatives for US Health Systems Governing Racial Bias in Health Care AI](https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e103163/)

- Source: `jmir_ai` · published `2026-11-26` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `failed`
- Event: `new` · identity: `url:https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e103163/`

Racial and ethnic disparities in US health care are long-standing, and AI is being adopted across clinical and administrative functions faster than the governance needed to detect inequity. This Viewpoint is written for the leaders of US health systems who decide what AI tools are bought, deployed, and monitored and who therefore hold the most direct leverage over whether those tools narrow or widen disparities. It advances 4 imperatives: govern before you deploy, demand transparency from vendors, build equity into clinical workflows, and make disparities visible. The imperatives are a leadership-level operationalization of existing frameworks (the Coalition for Health AI Blueprint, the National Institute of Standards and Technology AI Risk Management Framework, and the Rajkomar et al health equity framework), not a new framework. The contributions claimed are limited to 4 main points: allocating specific decisions to specific leadership functions; 3 reusable tools provided as Multimedia Appendices (a preprocurement equity audit checklist, illustrative contract clauses, and criteria for retaining race or ancestry as a model variable); a tiered approach for small and safety-net systems; and an assessment of how the imperatives hold up as US federal requirements are narrowed. The evidence base is concentrated on anti-Black bias, where it is deepest, and the paper identifies evidence gaps for other groups. A brief mapping to the European Union AI Act and the Assessment List for Trustworthy AI is offered for readers outside the United States. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/3b77725eeed59b1571cd75a7438dfb0f" />

### [Frequent Pattern Mining for Early Risk Signal Detection in Population Health Data: Nationwide Retrospective Cohort Study](https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e94284)

- Source: `jmir_medinform` · published `2026-11-26` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `56` · status: `scored`
- Event: `new` · identity: `url:https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e94284`

Background: Many diseases often develop through sequences of related conditions over time. Identifying how diagnoses occur over time may help detect early risk signals before severe outcomes arise. Clinically significant patterns are limited because many population-level studies focus on disease co-occurrence rather than the temporal order of diagnoses. Objective: The study aimed to identify and validate temporal disease associations using frequent pattern mining and statistical validation techniques in a nationwide patient-record database. Methods: We analyzed health records of 3,987,382 Finnish patients. The records were transformed into patient timelines by using the date on which the first record was entered into the database. To identify the most common patterns for each unique disease, we applied the frequent pattern growth algorithm using a minimum support of 5% of patients with the target disease and at least 5 patients. To validate each pattern, we applied a combination of relative risk (RR), a 95% CI, and relative width to measure precision. Results: We identified several clinically interpretable temporal disease connections. For example, acute kidney failure was most strongly preceded by unspecified kidney failure (RR 15.13, 95% CI 14.24‐16.07), chronic kidney disease (RR 11.02, 95% CI 10.53‐11.54), and sepsis (RR 9.76, 95% CI 9.27‐10.27). It was also grouped with heart failure–related combinations (RR 10.76, 95% CI 10.27‐11.27). Diabetic foot ulcer with type 1 or type 2 diabetes was strongly associated with osteomyelitis, with an RR of 157.02 (95% CI 137.03‐179.93) for type 1 and 84.84 (95% CI 77.07‐93.39) for type 2. At the block level, cerebrovascular diseases were linked to hypertension (RR 2.47; 95% CI 2.44‐2.49), atherosclerosis (RR 2.52; 95% CI 2.47‐2.58), and dementia (RR 2.96; 95% CI 2.90‐3.01). Drug poisoning patterns were also connected to psychiatric diagnoses, including mood disorders (RR 7.24, 95% CI 7.03-7.46) and combinations of alcohol and mood disorders (RR 18.25, 95% CI 17.51-19.01). The generated patterns and statistical measures are publicly available in a web interface for research purposes. Conclusions: Frequent pattern mining, when integrated with RR/CI and precision filtering, produces clinically interpretable temporal connections that can support hypothesis generation. External validation with other datasets and cohorts is essential.

### [Governing Embodied AI in Clinical Care: From Digital Recommendation to Recoverability-Gated Physical Action](https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e104088)

- Source: `jmir` · published `2026-11-26` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `53` · status: `scored`
- Event: `new` · identity: `url:https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e104088`

Clinical AI is expanding from recommending care to participating in physical action through surgical assistance, rehabilitation, patient handling, and AI-connected actuators. These applications may improve precision and support clinical work, but errors can produce immediate, persistent bodily harm. This viewpoint argues that crossing the digital-physical actuation boundary changes the requirements for clinical AI governance. We define embodied clinical AI by its causal coupling to physical action and propose recoverability-gated entrustment: demonstrated competence is necessary, while the recoverability of credible failures constrains permitted action authority. Recoverability comprises detectability, interruptibility, reversibility or clinical compensability, and consequence boundedness. The complete perception-decision-action loop should be the unit of governance and action-grounded validation. Meaningful human control must be assigned where intervention can actually affect outcomes, and risk-proportionate forensic records must support safety learning and fair accountability. Our contribution is to integrate existing AI, medical-device, and robotics safeguards into an action-specific authorization approach, rather than propose a separate regulatory regime. We operationalize this approach through a physical entrustment ladder and PACER-AI: perception reliability; action scope and actuation envelope; clinical context and control locus; evidence through action-grounded validation; and recoverability, responsibility, and forensic sufficiency. An illustrative patient-repositioning decision flow shows how failed recovery criteria require restriction, redesign, or refusal of authorization. Intended for clinicians, institutional governance teams, developers, evaluators, and regulators, this conceptual framework seeks proportionate, interruptible, auditable, and accountable physical action and requires empirical validation. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/7829eb626b493d3b5de1b16c170be9bf" />

### [Integrating Multidimensional Clinical Data to Develop a Visual Nomogram for Individualized Prediction of Postinfectious Bone Loss in Patients With Spinal Infection: Retrospective Cohort Study](https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e93326)

- Source: `jmir` · published `2026-11-26` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `53` · status: `scored`
- Event: `new` · identity: `url:https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e93326`

&lt;strong&gt;Background:&lt;/strong&gt; Postinfectious bone loss can lead to spinal instability and severe neurological complications, with potentially devastating outcomes. However, prognostic models to support individualized risk assessment remain limited. &lt;strong&gt;Objective:&lt;/strong&gt; This study aimed to develop and internally validate a visual nomogram integrating multimodal clinical data to estimate postinfectious bone loss risk in patients with spinal infection and inform individualized monitoring strategies. &lt;strong&gt;Methods:&lt;/strong&gt; This retrospective cohort study used consecutive sampling at Shandong Public Health Clinical Center. Baseline clinical and laboratory data were extracted from medical records. Eligible patients had confirmed spinal infection, a baseline vertebral bone destruction height ratio less than one-third, and complete baseline and follow-up data. Postinfectious bone loss was defined as a height ratio greater than or equal to one-third on computed tomography 12 months after baseline. Predictors were selected from 31 baseline variables using univariable logistic regression followed by least absolute shrinkage and selection operator (LASSO) regression. Multivariable logistic regression was used to construct the nomogram. Internal validation used 500 bootstrap resamples, repeating all predictor selection and model-fitting steps. Performance was assessed using the area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUC), calibration curves, the Brier score, decision curve analysis, and clinical impact curve analysis. &lt;strong&gt;Results:&lt;/strong&gt; Postinfectious bone loss occurred in 48.9% (109/223, 95% CI 42.4%-55.4%) of patients. The nomogram combined infection characteristics (multidrug-resistant bacterial infection and infected spinal segment), skeletal status (osteoporosis), and laboratory markers (interleukin-6, albumin, and plasma fibrinogen) to estimate individual risk. In the multivariable model, the odds ratios (ORs) were 2.718 (95% CI 0.993-7.444; &lt;i&gt;P&lt;/i&gt;=.05) for multidrug-resistant bacterial infection and 7.345 (95% CI 2.757-19.570; &lt;i&gt;P&lt;/i&gt;&amp;lt;.001) for osteoporosis. Relative to cervical infection, ORs were 14.041 (95% CI 1.079-182.662; &lt;i&gt;P&lt;/i&gt;=.04) for thoracic, 28.876 (95% CI 2.648-314.895; &lt;i&gt;P&lt;/i&gt;=.006) for lumbar, and 12.604 (95% CI 0.436-364.075; &lt;i&gt;P&lt;/i&gt;=.14) for thoracolumbar infection. Per unit increase, ORs were 1.004 (95% CI 0.996-1.012; &lt;i&gt;P&lt;/i&gt;=.30) for interleukin-6 (pg/mL), 3.673 (95% CI 2.410-5.598; &lt;i&gt;P&lt;/i&gt;&amp;lt;.001) for plasma fibrinogen (g/L), and 0.834 (95% CI 0.749-0.929; &lt;i&gt;P&lt;/i&gt;=.001) for albumin (g/L). Discrimination remained high after optimism correction, with an AUC of 0.946 (95% CI 0.919-0.972), compared with an apparent AUC of 0.954 (95% CI 0.931-0.978). Calibration assessment yielded an intercept of 0.00 (95% CI –0.43 to 0.43) and a slope of 1.00 (95% CI 0.75-1.25), while the Brier score was 0.096 (95% CI 0.064-0.118), indicating close agreement between predicted and observed risks and low overall prediction error. &lt;strong&gt;Conclusions:&lt;/strong&gt; This study developed a visual nomogram integrating baseline clinical characteristics, imaging features, and laboratory parameters to predict postinfectious bone loss in patients with spinal infection. Extending previous association studies to individualized risk prediction, the model showed good discrimination and calibration in internal validation. This approach may help identify high-risk patients and inform closer monitoring and timely reassessment before further structural deterioration and neurological complications. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/a64906440f24fb8bccd33c47653c1b4f" />

### [Machine Learning Models Using Wearable and Digital Phenotyping Data for Attention-Deficit/Hyperactivity Disorder Assessment: Systematic Review and Diagnostic Test Accuracy Meta-Analysis](https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e101227)

- Source: `jmir` · published `2026-11-26` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `62` · status: `scored`
- Event: `new` · identity: `url:https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e101227`

Background: Attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder (ADHD) assessment requires integration of developmental history, impairment, psychosocial context, and information from multiple informants and settings. Wearable and digital phenotyping data may provide objective behavioral, physiological, and sensor-derived signals relevant to ADHD assessment, but the diagnostic performance and clinical readiness of machine learning (ML) and deep learning (DL) models using these data remain uncertain. Objective: This review evaluated ML/DL models using wearable or digital phenotyping data for ADHD assessment and examined heterogeneity across study, data source, population, algorithm, and validation characteristics. Methods: We searched PubMed, Embase, Web of Science, the Cochrane Library, and IEEE Xplore from inception through January 23, 2026. Eligible full-text reports evaluated ML/DL classifiers using objective data derived from wearable devices or digital platforms in participants with ADHD and comparators. We excluded nonoriginal reports and studies lacking ML/DL models or eligible objective digital inputs. Reports providing eligible participant-level 2×2 estimates entered quantitative synthesis, with 1 estimate retained per cohort; others were summarized qualitatively. Risk of bias was assessed with QUADAS-AI (Quality Assessment of Diagnostic Accuracy Studies–AI). Bayesian bivariate Reitsma models estimated pooled sensitivity and specificity, with a secondary HSROC (hierarchical summary receiver operating characteristic) analysis. Descriptive subgroup analyses examined the validation approach, data sources, population, and algorithms. Results: After 254 duplicates were removed, 616 records were screened. A total of 34 eligible reports were included, with report-level sample sizes totaling 10,476 participants (3402 with ADHD and 7074 comparators). Of these, 17 reports contributed 1 retained participant-level 2×2 estimate per cohort to the quantitative synthesis, and 17 were summarized qualitatively. Data sources were wearable devices (n=18), digital task–based assessments (n=8), other or multimodal recordings (n=7), and smartphone passive sensing (n=1); 17 reports involved laboratory-based assessments, whereas 17 involved ambulatory or real-world data collection. Pooled sensitivity was 0.875 (95% credible interval [CrI] 0.808‐0.927), and pooled specificity was 0.904 (95% CrI 0.840‐0.948); the secondary HSROC area under the receiver operating characteristic curve was 0.936 (95% CrI 0.880‐0.970). Pooled positive and negative likelihood ratios were 9.153 (95% CrI 5.255‐17.041) and 0.138 (95% CrI 0.079‐0.219), respectively. The Deeks funnel-plot asymmetry test was considered inconclusive because of limited power (=.99). Certainty of evidence was very low for both pooled sensitivity and pooled specificity. Reference standards varied, and patient-selection risk was high in all 17 quantitative reports and 16 of 17 qualitative reports. Conclusions: ML/DL models showed high apparent discrimination in selected datasets; however, case-control designs, healthy controls, heterogeneous reference standards, limited external validation, and high patient-selection risk limit transportability to routine clinical practice. They should remain investigational adjuncts, not stand-alone diagnostic tools. Progress toward clinical use will require external validation in clinically representative cohorts, assessment of model calibration, and evidence of incremental clinical value within ADHD assessment pathways. ### [Multistakeholder Expectations and Implementation Concerns Regarding AI-Based Pregnancy Risk Assessment in Maternity Care: Qualitative Descriptive Study](https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e102485)

- Source: `jmir` · published `2026-11-26` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `57` · status: `scored`
- Event: `new` · identity: `url:https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e102485`

Background: Pregnancy risk assessment requires not only accurate identification of risk but also communication, referral, and follow-up across different levels of care. Although AI-based tools are increasingly proposed to support this process, evidence remains limited on how women, primary health care professionals, and hospital-based maternity care providers expect AI-generated risk information to be interpreted and used in care, as well as on what conditions they consider important for its practical use. Objective: This study aimed to explore multistakeholder expectations and anticipated implementation concerns regarding AI-based pregnancy risk assessment in maternity care, with particular attention to how AI-generated risk information could be interpreted, confirmed, and used to inform care decisions and subsequent management. Methods: This qualitative descriptive study used semistructured interviews with 93 participants in Guizhou Province, China, including 33 (33.5%) pregnant or postpartum women, 23 (24.7%) primary health care professionals, and 37 (39.8%) hospital-based maternity care providers. Purposive sampling with maximum variation was used. Participants were recruited from 18 health care facilities, including primary health care facilities and secondary and tertiary hospitals. Interviews were conducted between August 30, 2024, and May 2026. Brief standardized conceptual material was used to support discussion of AI-based pregnancy risk assessment; it did not depict a specific product or prototype. Data were analyzed using inductive qualitative content analysis. Results: Five descriptive themes and 16 subthemes were developed. The expected value of AI-based pregnancy risk assessment depended on whether it addressed practical gaps in pregnancy risk management, although priorities differed across stakeholder groups. Women emphasized understandable risk information and care-seeking guidance; primary health care professionals emphasized referral, feedback, and follow-up continuity; and hospital-based providers valued clinically interpretable information for assessment, management, and cross-level coordination. Participants valued early and dynamic risk recognition because pregnancy risk could emerge, worsen, or be reclassified as pregnancy progressed, making sustained visibility of risk information important. AI-generated risk information was not treated as self-evident; it needed to be interpreted and confirmed in relation to women’s bodily experience, clinicians’ judgment, examination results, referral rules, and higher-level confirmation. Risk recognition was considered useful when it could be translated into guidance, referral, feedback, and follow-up. Implementation concerns centered on risk communication and anxiety management for women and on workload, usability, training, and organizational support for health care professionals. Conclusions: Stakeholders expected AI-based pregnancy risk assessment to provide risk information that could become meaningful and actionable within existing maternity care pathways. Future AI tools in maternity care should therefore be evaluated not only for predictive accuracy but also for whether risk information can be understood, communicated, confirmed, and carried forward into follow-up and coordinated action across levels of care. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/0182d340e3e23389a2bda79d3f90bdff" />

### [Patterns of HIV Cure and HIV Cure–Directed Research Discussions in an Online Community Over 14 Years: Global Social Listening Approach](https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e95086)

- Source: `jmir` · published `2026-11-26` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `51` · status: `scored`
- Event: `new` · identity: `url:https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e95086`

Background: HIV cure research has advanced since the first reported cure case in 2008, but confirmed cure outcomes remain extremely rare. Community discussions on platforms such as Reddit’s r/hivaids both reflect and shape perceptions of cure research, stigma, and willingness to participate in studies. Understanding this online dialogue is therefore critical for optimizing outreach, education, and information strategies. Objective: The goal of our study was to determine whether social listening methods can characterize the scope, patterns, and content of online discourse about HIV cure and cure-directed research within a social media community. Methods: We conducted a social listening analysis of more than 142,000 posts from 12,227 authors on r/hivaids (2011‐2025). Using natural language processing, we characterized communication themes and engagement patterns and evaluated how discussion volume related to trends in the scientific publication landscape. User geography was inferred from self-disclosed information in posts and comments. Results: Location analysis showed overrepresentation from North America, consistent with Reddit’s largely US‑based user population (approximately 49.6% of daily users), and limited participation from high‑burden regions such as Africa and Asia. “Cure” was the most common term, whereas more specific research language (eg, “remission” or “functional cure”) was rarely used. Cure‑related discussion volume closely paralleled the appearance of new scientific reports rather than general news cycles. Thematic analysis identified “hope for a cure” as the dominant topic, with frequent positive and optimistic themes—particularly among non‑US users. In contrast, US‑based posts more often included limited content on research details and policy (eg, profitability and funding for HIV cure research, clinical trials, bone marrow transplantation, a vaccine, etc), while non‑US posts also highlighted mental health challenges, optimism after diagnosis, and ongoing social issues such as stigma and discrimination. Only 16% (743/4644) of cure‑related posts referenced cure-directed research directly. Engagement was highly concentrated among a small number of “nano‑influencer” users. Conclusions: Online HIV cure and HIV cure–directed research discussions on Reddit are largely optimistic, use relatively simple language, and show engagement peaks that align with new scientific data rather than general news cycles. These conversations also vary markedly by geography. Closing language gaps, strategically partnering with key community influencers, and extending outreach into high-burden regions should be priorities to maximize global impact. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/7ed4ec6aaeff5c5ba61ed8c38c550f5b" />

### [Wearables and Machine Learning Use for Activity of Daily Life and Fall Management in Older Adults: Systematic Review](https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e93640/)

- Source: `jmir_ai` · published `2026-11-26` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `51` · status: `scored`
- Event: `new` · identity: `url:https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e93640/`

Background: Population aging has increased interest in digital strategies that may support functional independence and safety in older adults. Consumer-grade wearable technologies combined with machine learning have been increasingly studied for monitoring activities of daily living, mobility assessment, fall-related applications, and other autonomy-relevant functions. Objective: This systematic review aimed to identify, appraise, and synthesize primary studies on consumer-grade wearable technologies combined with machine learning in older adults, focusing on application domains, methodological characteristics, validation strategies, and reported outcomes, and relevance to autonomy, daily functioning, mobility monitoring, and fall-related safety. Methods: We conducted a systematic review in accordance with the Cochrane handbook and PRISMA (Preferred Reporting Items for Systematic Reviews and Meta-Analyses) guidelines to synthesize evidence on the use of wearable technologies combined with AI, particularly machine learning methods, to support daily living and fall-related safety among older adults. The search was revised and conducted in PubMed/MEDLINE, Scopus, Institute of Electrical and Electronics Engineers Xplore, and the Association for Computing Machinery Digital Library from database inception through May 2026, with no date or language restrictions. Eligible reports were full primary studies, including conference proceedings when sufficient methodological detail and results were available for assessment. Search reporting was aligned with PRISMA-S (Preferred Reporting Items for Systematic Reviews and Meta-Analyses Literature Search Extension). Results: Thirty studies were included. Most studies were observational or methodological and relied primarily on inertial sensing, with smartphone and mobile-sensing approaches contributing to specific subdomains. The main application domains were activities of daily living monitoring, gait and mobility assessment, cognitive/dementia-related monitoring, Parkinson-related monitoring, fall detection or fall-risk assessment, and frailty assessment. Classical machine learning models, such as support vector machines and random forests, and deep learning architectures, such as convolutional neural networks and long short-term memory networks, were commonly used. However, the evidence base was highly heterogeneous, frequently based on small samples, and rarely supported by external validation or clinically meaningful outcomes. Conclusions: Consumer-grade wearable technologies combined with machine learning appear technically promising for monitoring autonomy-relevant domains in older adults, including daily functioning, mobility, and fall-related applications. However, the current evidence remains exploratory and is limited by substantial methodological heterogeneity, small sample sizes, limited external validation, and infrequent reporting of clinically meaningful or patient-centered outcomes. Currently, the literature supports technical feasibility more clearly than robust real-world generalizability or routine clinical adoption. Future research should prioritize free-living evaluation, transparent reporting, external validation, interdisciplinary co-design, and patient-centered outcomes to strengthen real-world relevance. Trial Registration: PROSPERO CRD420251044449; https://www.crd.york.ac.uk/PROSPERO/view/CRD420251044449 <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/b9ff2ce27cf022e14974413bc91d0cd5" />
